How the practice works

  • Type answers the way you would on a calculator: 3e^(2t) - t sin(4t) means $3e^{2t}-t\sin 4t$, (2s+1)/(s^2+9) means $\dfrac{2s+1}{s^2+9}$, and u(t-2) means the unit step $\mathcal{U}(t-2)$. The line underneath shows how your answer was read, with proper fractions, so you can catch bracket mistakes before checking.
  • Answers are checked by value, not by form. Any equivalent expression is accepted, and for “find a particular solution” questions any function that actually satisfies the equation is accepted.
  • Hints come one at a time. The full solution is always one click away. Press New problem for different numbers.
  • The dot next to each section in the contents fills in as you solve problems there. Progress stays in this browser.

Chapter 1 · Section 1.1

Definitions and terminology

A differential equation is an equation that contains derivatives of an unknown function. Engineering is full of them because physical laws are usually statements about rates of change. This section sets up the vocabulary used in the rest of the course.

A differential equation is a statement about rates of change. Physical laws are usually stated that way: a hot object cools at a rate proportional to how much hotter it is than the room; a population grows at a rate proportional to its size; the acceleration of a mass is the net force divided by the mass. Each of these sentences is a differential equation, and solving it tells you the actual temperature, population or position at every time.

Most of this course is about four questions:

  1. What kind of equation is it? Order, linear or nonlinear, ordinary or partial. The answer decides which method can work.
  2. Does a solution exist, and is it unique? (Section 1.2.)
  3. How do we find it? (Chapters 2 to 4 and 13.)
  4. What does it mean? Reading the solution as a physical behaviour.

Why “linear” matters so much

For a linear equation, solutions can be added and scaled: if $y_1$ and $y_2$ solve the homogeneous equation, so does $c_1y_1+c_2y_2$. That single property is what makes the methods of Chapters 3 and 4 possible. Nonlinear equations have no such structure, and usually only special types (like separable equations) can be solved exactly.

To test linearity, look only at how $y$ and its derivatives appear. The variable $x$ can appear in any way at all. So $x^5y''+e^xy=\ln x$ is linear, while $yy'=x$ (product), $(y')^2=x$ (power) and $y''+\sin y=0$ (function of $y$) are not.

What “solution” really means

A solution is a function, together with an interval, that turns the equation into a true identity on that interval. Checking a proposed solution is always the same: differentiate it, substitute everything into the equation, and simplify until both sides agree. You can always check an answer this way, so you never need to wonder whether a solution is right.

What is a differential equation?

In calculus you learned that the derivative of $y=e^{0.1x^2}$ is $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=0.2xe^{0.1x^2}$. Notice that the right side is $0.2x$ times the original function, so

$$\frac{dy}{dx}=0.2xy.$$

Now turn the question around. If someone hands you this equation, can you find the function $y$ it came from? That is the central question of the course.

Definition 1.1.1 · Differential equation

An equation containing the derivatives of one or more unknown functions (dependent variables) with respect to one or more independent variables is a differential equation (DE).

Classification by type

If the unknown function depends on only one independent variable, the equation is an ordinary differential equation (ODE). For example,

$$\frac{dy}{dx}+5y=e^x,\qquad\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}-\frac{dy}{dx}+6y=0.$$

If it depends on two or more variables, the derivatives are partial derivatives and the equation is a partial differential equation (PDE), such as $\dfrac{\partial^2u}{\partial x^2}+\dfrac{\partial^2u}{\partial y^2}=0$. Chapters 1 to 4 are about ODEs. Chapter 13 is about PDEs.

We will use three notations: Leibniz $\dfrac{dy}{dx}$, prime $y'$, $y''$, $y'''$, $y^{(4)}$, and (for time derivatives in physics) dot $\dot x$, $\ddot x$.

Classification by order

The order of a DE is the order of the highest derivative in it. For example,

$$\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}+5\left(\frac{dy}{dx}\right)^3-4y=e^x$$

is second order. The cube on $\dfrac{dy}{dx}$ is a power, not a derivative, so it does not affect the order.

A general $n$th-order ODE can be written $F(x,y,y',\dots,y^{(n)})=0$. When we can solve for the highest derivative, we get the normal form $y^{(n)}=f(x,y,y',\dots,y^{(n-1)})$. For example, $4xy'+y=x$ has normal form $y'=\dfrac{x-y}{4x}$.

Classification by linearity

Linear ODE

An $n$th-order ODE is linear if it can be written

$$a_n(x)y^{(n)}+a_{n-1}(x)y^{(n-1)}+\cdots+a_1(x)y'+a_0(x)y=g(x).$$

Two things make an equation linear:

  1. $y$ and all its derivatives appear only to the first power.
  2. The coefficients $a_0,a_1,\dots,a_n$ depend at most on $x$, not on $y$.

Anything else is nonlinear: a product like $yy'$, a power like $y^2$ or $(y')^3$, or a function of $y$ like $\sin y$ or $e^y$.

Example 1Linear or nonlinear?

Classify (a) $(y-x)\,dx+4x\,dy=0$, (b) $y''-2y'+y=0$, (c) $x^3y'''+xy'-5y=e^x$, (d) $(1-y)y'+2y=e^x$, (e) $y''+\sin y=0$, (f) $y^{(4)}+y^2=0$.

  1. (a) Divide by $dx$: $4xy'+y=x$. The coefficients $4x$ and $1$ depend only on $x$. Linear, first order.
  2. (b) Constant coefficients, everything to the first power. Linear, second order.
  3. (c) Coefficients $x^3$, $x$, $-5$ depend only on $x$, and $e^x$ is just the right side $g(x)$. Linear, third order.
  4. (d) The coefficient of $y'$ is $1-y$, which depends on $y$. Nonlinear, first order.
  5. (e) $\sin y$ is a nonlinear function of $y$. Nonlinear, second order. (This is the pendulum equation.)
  6. (f) $y^2$ is a power of $y$. Nonlinear, fourth order.

Solutions

Definition 1.1.2 · Solution of an ODE

Any function $\phi$, defined on an interval $I$ and having at least $n$ derivatives there, which turns the $n$th-order ODE into an identity when substituted, is a solution of the equation on $I$.

The interval $I$ is called the interval of definition (or interval of existence). It can be open $(a,b)$, closed, or infinite. A solution has to be differentiable on all of $I$, so it cannot have a break or a vertical asymptote inside it.

Example 2Verifying a solution

Verify that (a) $y=\dfrac{1}{16}x^4$ solves $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=xy^{1/2}$ and (b) $y=xe^x$ solves $y''-2y'+y=0$, both on $(-\infty,\infty)$.

  1. (a) Left side. $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=\dfrac{4x^3}{16}=\dfrac{x^3}{4}$.
  2. (a) Right side. $xy^{1/2}=x\left(\dfrac{x^4}{16}\right)^{1/2}=x\cdot\dfrac{x^2}{4}=\dfrac{x^3}{4}$.
  3. (a) Compare. Both sides equal $\dfrac{x^3}{4}$ for every $x$, so it is a solution.
  4. (b) Differentiate. By the product rule $y'=xe^x+e^x$ and $y''=xe^x+2e^x$.
  5. (b) Substitute. $y''-2y'+y=(xe^x+2e^x)-2(xe^x+e^x)+xe^x=0$. Every term cancels, so it is a solution.

Note that $y=0$ also solves both equations. A solution that is identically zero on $I$ is called the trivial solution.

Example 3The interval of definition matters

Consider $y=\dfrac1x$ and the equation $xy'+y=0$.

  1. As a function, $y=\dfrac1x$ is defined for every $x\neq0$.
  2. As a solution, check: $y'=-\dfrac{1}{x^2}$, so $xy'+y=-\dfrac1x+\dfrac1x=0$. It works for $x\neq0$.
  3. But a solution must be differentiable on a whole interval, and $\dfrac1x$ blows up at $0$. So we must pick an interval that avoids $0$, such as $(0,\infty)$ or $(-\infty,0)$.

Explicit and implicit solutions

A solution written as $y=\phi(x)$ is explicit. Sometimes the best we can do is a relation $G(x,y)=0$ that defines one or more solutions without solving for $y$. That is an implicit solution.

Example 4An implicit solution

Show that $x^2+y^2=25$ is an implicit solution of $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=-\dfrac{x}{y}$ on $(-5,5)$.

  1. Differentiate implicitly. $\dfrac{d}{dx}(x^2)+\dfrac{d}{dx}(y^2)=0$ gives $2x+2y\dfrac{dy}{dx}=0$.
  2. Solve for $\dfrac{dy}{dx}$. $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=-\dfrac{x}{y}$, which is the equation.
  3. Explicit pieces. Solving the relation for $y$ gives two explicit solutions, $y=\sqrt{25-x^2}$ and $y=-\sqrt{25-x^2}$, each valid on $(-5,5)$. At $x=\pm5$, $y=0$ and the right side $-\dfrac{x}{y}$ is undefined.

Families of solutions

Solving a first-order equation usually involves one integration, which produces one arbitrary constant $c$. So the solution is a one-parameter family $G(x,y,c)=0$. An $n$th-order equation usually gives an $n$-parameter family. Picking values for the constants gives a particular solution.

For example, $y=cx-x\cos x$ solves $xy'-y=x^2\sin x$ on $(-\infty,\infty)$ for every $c$. Here $y'=c-\cos x+x\sin x$, so

$$\begin{aligned}xy'-y&=cx-x\cos x+x^2\sin x-cx+x\cos x\\&=x^2\sin x.\end{aligned}$$

Sometimes a solution exists that no choice of $c$ produces. It is called a singular solution. For example, $y=\left(\dfrac{x^2}{4}+c\right)^2$ solves $y'=xy^{1/2}$, and so does $y=0$, but no value of $c$ gives $y=0$.

Systems of differential equations

A system has two or more unknown functions, for example $\dfrac{dx}{dt}=f(t,x,y)$, $\dfrac{dy}{dt}=g(t,x,y)$. Predator and prey populations, and currents in coupled circuits, lead to systems. We will not solve them in this course.

Summary
  • ODE: one independent variable. PDE: several.
  • Order: the highest derivative present.
  • Linear: $y$ and its derivatives to the first power, coefficients depending only on $x$.
  • A solution satisfies the equation on an interval $I$, and must be differentiable on all of $I$.
  • First-order equations give one-parameter families of solutions.

More worked examples

Example 5Finding which exponentials solve an equation

For which values of $m$ is $y=e^{mx}$ a solution of $y''-5y'+6y=0$?

  1. Differentiate. $y'=me^{mx}$ and $y''=m^2e^{mx}$.
  2. Substitute. $m^2e^{mx}-5me^{mx}+6e^{mx}=e^{mx}(m^2-5m+6)$.
  3. Use that $e^{mx}\neq0$. The expression is zero for all $x$ only when $m^2-5m+6=0$.
  4. Solve. $(m-2)(m-3)=0$, so $m=2$ or $m=3$. Both $e^{2x}$ and $e^{3x}$ are solutions. This is exactly the idea behind Section 3.3.
Example 6Checking a solution with a constant

Show that $y=\dfrac{1}{x^2+c}$ is a one-parameter family of solutions of $y'+2xy^2=0$.

  1. Differentiate. Write $y=(x^2+c)^{-1}$. By the chain rule, $y'=-(x^2+c)^{-2}\cdot2x=-\dfrac{2x}{(x^2+c)^2}$.
  2. Compute $2xy^2$. $2x\cdot\dfrac{1}{(x^2+c)^2}=\dfrac{2x}{(x^2+c)^2}$.
  3. Add. $y'+2xy^2=-\dfrac{2x}{(x^2+c)^2}+\dfrac{2x}{(x^2+c)^2}=0$ for every $c$.
  4. Interval. The formula needs $x^2+c\neq0$. For $c>0$ that is all $x$. For $c<0$ the solution must stay on one side of $x=\pm\sqrt{-c}$. For example, with $c=-1$, valid intervals are $(-\infty,-1)$, $(-1,1)$ and $(1,\infty)$.
Common mistakes
  • Calling an equation nonlinear because of $x^2$ or $\sin x$ in a coefficient. Only the way $y$ appears matters.
  • Reading the order from the highest power instead of the highest derivative: $(y')^3+y=0$ is first order.
  • Forgetting the interval. A formula can be a solution on one interval and undefined on another.

Practice set · 12 problems

12 more problems on this section. Each one has an answer and a full step-by-step solution that explains why each step is taken. The numbers are fixed, so you can come back to the same problem.

Chapter 1 · Section 1.2

Initial value problems

A differential equation usually has a whole family of solutions. In applications we want the one that matches the situation, such as a starting position and velocity. Adding those conditions gives an initial value problem.

Solving a differential equation usually gives a whole family of solutions, because each integration introduces a constant. A first-order equation gives a family with one constant, $c$; a second-order equation gives two, $c_1$ and $c_2$. Geometrically, the family is a set of curves filling the plane.

An initial condition picks one curve: $y(x_0)=y_0$ says “the curve through the point $(x_0,y_0)$”. For a second-order equation, one point is not enough, because many curves pass through a point at different angles. So we also give the slope there, $y'(x_0)=y_1$. In general, an $n$th-order equation needs $n$ conditions, one for each constant.

Why existence and uniqueness is worth knowing

When an engineer models a system, they want the model to predict one outcome. Theorem 1.2.1 says when that is guaranteed for $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=f(x,y)$: if $f$ and $\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial y}$ are continuous in a rectangle around $(x_0,y_0)$, exactly one solution passes through that point, at least for a short interval.

The theorem is a sufficient condition. If its hypotheses fail, it says nothing: there might be no solution, one, or many. Example 4 showed two solutions through the same point, which happened because $\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial y}$ was not continuous there.

How to apply the theorem in three lines

  1. Write the equation as $y'=f(x,y)$ and identify $f$.
  2. Compute $\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial y}$, treating $x$ as a constant.
  3. Find where either one fails to be continuous (division by zero, square roots of negatives, logs of non-positives). If the initial point avoids those places, a unique solution exists near it.

What an IVP is

$n$th-order initial value problem $$\text{Solve}\quad\frac{d^ny}{dx^n}=f(x,y,y',\dots,y^{(n-1)})$$ $$\text{subject to}\quad y(x_0)=y_0,\ y'(x_0)=y_1,\ \dots,\ y^{(n-1)}(x_0)=y_{n-1}.$$

The values are all given at the same point $x_0$. They are called initial conditions. A first-order IVP has one condition, $y(x_0)=y_0$: geometrically we want the solution curve through the point $(x_0,y_0)$. A second-order IVP also fixes the slope there, $y'(x_0)=y_1$.

The reason an $n$th-order equation needs $n$ conditions: its general solution has $n$ constants, and we need $n$ equations to find them.

Example 1A first-order IVP

$y=ce^x$ is a one-parameter family of solutions of $y'=y$ on $(-\infty,\infty)$. Find the solution with (a) $y(0)=3$ and (b) $y(1)=-2$.

  1. (a) Put $x=0$, $y=3$: $3=ce^0=c$. So $y=3e^x$.
  2. (b) Put $x=1$, $y=-2$: $-2=ce$, so $c=-2e^{-1}$.
  3. (b) Simplify. $y=-2e^{-1}e^x=-2e^{x-1}$.
Example 2The interval of a solution of an IVP

$y=\dfrac{1}{x^2+c}$ solves $y'+2xy^2=0$. Find the solution of the IVP with $y(0)=-1$, and its interval of definition.

  1. Use the condition. $-1=\dfrac{1}{0+c}$, so $c=-1$ and $y=\dfrac{1}{x^2-1}$.
  2. Where is it defined? As a function it is defined except at $x=\pm1$, where the denominator is zero.
  3. Choose the interval. A solution of an IVP must be defined on an interval containing $x_0=0$, with no breaks. The largest such interval is $(-1,1)$.
Example 3A second-order IVP

$x=c_1\cos4t+c_2\sin4t$ solves $x''+16x=0$. Find the solution with $x\left(\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)=-2$ and $x'\left(\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)=1$.

  1. First condition. At $t=\dfrac{\pi}{2}$, $4t=2\pi$, so $\cos2\pi=1$ and $\sin2\pi=0$. Then $x\left(\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)=c_1=-2$.
  2. Differentiate. $x'=-4c_1\sin4t+4c_2\cos4t$.
  3. Second condition. $x'\left(\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)=4c_2=1$, so $c_2=\dfrac14$.
  4. Answer. $x=-2\cos4t+\dfrac14\sin4t$.

Existence and uniqueness

Two natural questions come up for any IVP. Does a solution exist? If so, is it the only one? The answer is not always yes.

Example 4An IVP with two solutions

Show that $y'=xy^{1/2}$, $y(0)=0$ has at least two solutions.

  1. The trivial solution. $y=0$ satisfies the equation ($0=0$) and the condition.
  2. Another one. From Example 2 of 1.1, $y=\dfrac{x^4}{16}$ also solves the equation, and $y(0)=0$.
  3. Conclusion. Two different curves pass through $(0,0)$. The IVP does not have a unique solution.

The following theorem says when this cannot happen.

Theorem 1.2.1 · Existence of a unique solution

Let $R$ be a rectangle $a\le x\le b$, $c\le y\le d$ containing $(x_0,y_0)$ in its interior. If $f(x,y)$ and $\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial y}$ are continuous on $R$, then there is some interval $I_0=(x_0-h,x_0+h)$, $h>0$, and a unique function $y(x)$ on $I_0$ that solves $y'=f(x,y)$, $y(x_0)=y_0$.

In Example 4, $f=xy^{1/2}$ and $\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial y}=\dfrac{x}{2y^{1/2}}$. The partial derivative is not continuous at $y=0$, so the theorem does not apply at $(0,0)$, which is why uniqueness failed. At a point like $(2,1)$, both are continuous in a rectangle around it, so $y'=xy^{1/2}$, $y(2)=1$ has exactly one solution.

Example 5Applying the theorem

Does $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=x^2+y^2$, $y(x_0)=y_0$ have a unique solution?

  1. Check $f$. $f(x,y)=x^2+y^2$ is a polynomial, so it is continuous everywhere.
  2. Check $\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial y}$. It is $2y$, also continuous everywhere.
  3. Conclusion. Through every point $(x_0,y_0)$ there is exactly one solution curve, at least on some small interval.
What the theorem does not say

It guarantees a solution only on some interval $I_0$, which may be much smaller than the rectangle. It also gives sufficient conditions, not necessary ones: when they fail, there might still be a unique solution.

Summary
  • An $n$th-order IVP gives $n$ conditions at one point $x_0$.
  • Substitute the conditions into the general solution to find the constants.
  • The interval of a solution of an IVP must contain $x_0$ and have no breaks.
  • If $f$ and $\partial f/\partial y$ are continuous near $(x_0,y_0)$, the solution exists and is unique.

More worked examples

Example 6Where uniqueness is guaranteed

For $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=\sqrt{y-x}$, find the region of the plane where Theorem 1.2.1 guarantees a unique solution through each point.

  1. Identify $f$. $f(x,y)=\sqrt{y-x}$. It is continuous where $y-x\ge0$.
  2. Partial derivative. $\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial y}=\dfrac{1}{2\sqrt{y-x}}$, which needs $y-x>0$.
  3. Both together. Both are continuous where $y>x$, the region above the line $y=x$.
  4. Conclusion. Any initial point with $y_0>x_0$, such as $(0,1)$, has a unique solution nearby. On the line $y=x$ the theorem does not apply.
Example 7Fitting two constants

$y=c_1\cos3x+c_2\sin3x$ solves $y''+9y=0$. Find the solution with $y(0)=2$ and $y'(0)=-6$.

  1. First condition. At $x=0$, $\cos0=1$ and $\sin0=0$, so $y(0)=c_1=2$.
  2. Differentiate. $y'=-3c_1\sin3x+3c_2\cos3x$.
  3. Second condition. $y'(0)=3c_2=-6$, so $c_2=-2$.
  4. Answer. $y=2\cos3x-2\sin3x$.
Common mistakes
  • Using the theorem backwards: failing its hypotheses does not prove that there is no solution or many solutions.
  • Taking $\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial x}$ instead of $\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial y}$.
  • Forgetting that the interval of a solution of an IVP must contain $x_0$.

Chapter 2 · Section 2.1

Direction fields and autonomous equations

Before solving anything, we can learn a lot about a first-order equation just by looking at it. A direction field shows the shape of every solution at once, and for autonomous equations a simple sign chart tells you where solutions go in the long run.

Most differential equations cannot be solved by a formula. Even so, the equation $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=f(x,y)$ tells you the slope of the solution curve at every point. Drawing short line segments with those slopes gives a direction field, and solution curves are the curves that follow the segments, like leaves floating on a stream.

Autonomous equations

When the right side does not depend on $x$, as in $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=f(y)$, the slope depends only on the height $y$. Then everything about the solutions can be read from one picture of $f(y)$:

  • Where $f(y)=0$, the slope is zero, so the constant $y=c$ is a solution. These are the critical points (equilibria).
  • Where $f(y)>0$, solutions increase; where $f(y)<0$, they decrease.
  • Solutions cannot cross an equilibrium (by uniqueness), so they move toward or away from it forever.

An equilibrium is asymptotically stable (an attractor) if solutions on both sides move toward it, unstable (a repeller) if they move away on both sides, and semi-stable if they approach from one side and leave on the other. A phase portrait is a vertical line with the critical points marked and arrows between them showing the direction of motion.

Slopes from the equation

The equation $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=f(x,y)$ tells you the slope of the solution curve at every point $(x,y)$. That is useful even when you cannot solve it: at each point you can draw a tiny line segment, called a lineal element, with slope $f(x,y)$.

Drawing these on a grid gives a direction field (or slope field). A solution curve is a curve that is tangent to the field at every point, so you can sketch solutions by following the arrows, like a leaf following the current in a stream.

Interactive · direction field
Example 1Reading a direction field

Describe the direction field of $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=0.2xy$.

  1. Where is the slope zero? $0.2xy=0$ when $x=0$ or $y=0$. The lineal elements along both axes are horizontal.
  2. Signs in each quadrant. In the first and third quadrants $xy>0$, so slopes are positive. In the second and fourth $xy<0$, so slopes are negative.
  3. Steepness. The slope grows with $|xy|$, so the segments get steeper far from the axes.
  4. Shape of solutions. Curves above the $x$-axis look like bells opening upward, $y=ce^{0.1x^2}$ with $c>0$. Below the axis they are their mirror images. The axis itself, $y=0$, is a solution.

Setting $f(x,y)=c$ gives a curve called an isocline, along which every lineal element has the same slope $c$. The isocline $f(x,y)=0$ is especially useful: solution curves are flat as they cross it.

Autonomous equations

A first-order equation in which the independent variable does not appear explicitly,

$$\frac{dy}{dx}=f(y),$$

is called autonomous. For example $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=1+y^2$ is autonomous, but $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=0.2xy$ is not. Many physical laws are autonomous because the rules do not change with time: population growth $\dfrac{dP}{dt}=kP$, Newton's cooling $\dfrac{dT}{dt}=k(T-T_m)$.

Since the slope depends only on $y$, every lineal element on a horizontal line has the same slope. The whole field is made of horizontal copies of one column.

Critical points

Critical point

A real number $c$ is a critical point (or equilibrium point, stationary point) of $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=f(y)$ if $f(c)=0$. Then the constant function $y=c$ is a solution, called an equilibrium solution.

Why: if $y=c$ then $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=0$, and $f(c)=0$, so both sides match. Between critical points $f(y)$ has a fixed sign, so solutions in that strip are always increasing or always decreasing.

Phase portraits

Draw a vertical line (the phase line) and mark the critical points on it. In each interval between them, put an arrow up if $f(y)>0$ and down if $f(y)<0$. This picture is the phase portrait.

Example 2Population with limited resources

Find the critical points and phase portrait of $\dfrac{dP}{dt}=P(a-bP)$, where $a$ and $b$ are positive.

  1. Critical points. $P(a-bP)=0$ when $P=0$ or $P=\dfrac{a}{b}$. So $P=0$ and $P=\dfrac ab$ are equilibrium solutions.
  2. Sign below $0$. Take $P=-1$: $(-1)(a+b)<0$. Arrow down.
  3. Sign between $0$ and $\dfrac ab$. Both factors are positive. Arrow up.
  4. Sign above $\dfrac ab$. $P>0$ but $a-bP<0$. Arrow down.
  5. Read the portrait. Arrows point toward $\dfrac ab$ from both sides, and away from $0$ on both sides. A population that starts anywhere above $0$ approaches $\dfrac ab$ as $t\to\infty$. This is the carrying capacity you will meet again in 2.8.

Stability

The arrows around a critical point $c$ tell you what happens to solutions that start near it:

  • Asymptotically stable (an attractor): arrows on both sides point toward $c$. Nearby solutions approach $y=c$.
  • Unstable (a repeller): arrows on both sides point away. Nearby solutions move away.
  • Semi-stable: one arrow toward and one away.
Example 3Classifying critical points

Find and classify the critical points of $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=(y-2)(y+1)^2$.

  1. Critical points. $y=2$ and $y=-1$.
  2. Test each interval. For $y<-1$, try $y=-2$: $(-4)(1)<0$, down. For $-1<y<2$, try $y=0$: $(-2)(1)<0$, down. For $y>2$, try $y=3$: $(1)(16)>0$, up.
  3. $y=2$. Below it the arrow points down (away), above it points up (away). Unstable (repeller).
  4. $y=-1$. Above it the arrow points down (toward), below it points down (away). Semi-stable. The squared factor never changes sign, which is why the arrows on both sides point the same way.
A quick test

If $f'(c)<0$ the critical point is an attractor. If $f'(c)>0$ it is a repeller. If $f'(c)=0$ the test says nothing and you must look at the signs. (In Example 2, $f'(P)=a-2bP$, so $f'(0)=a>0$ and $f'(a/b)=-a<0$.)

Summary
  • $y'=f(x,y)$ gives the slope at each point. Solutions follow the direction field.
  • Autonomous: $y'=f(y)$. Critical points solve $f(c)=0$ and give constant solutions.
  • Phase portrait: sign of $f$ between critical points.
  • Toward from both sides: attractor. Away from both: repeller. Mixed: semi-stable.

More worked examples

Example 4A full phase-portrait analysis

Classify the critical points of $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=y^2(y-3)$ and describe the solution with $y(0)=1$.

  1. Critical points. $y^2(y-3)=0$ gives $y=0$ and $y=3$.
  2. Sign on each interval. For $y<0$: $y^2>0$, $y-3<0$, so $f<0$ (decreasing). For $0<y<3$: again $f<0$. For $y>3$: $f>0$ (increasing).
  3. Classify $y=3$. Below it solutions decrease (move away), above it they increase (move away). Unstable.
  4. Classify $y=0$. Above it solutions decrease toward $0$; below it they decrease away from $0$. Semi-stable.
  5. The solution through $y(0)=1$. It starts between $0$ and $3$, where $f<0$, so it decreases and approaches $y=0$ as $x\to\infty$, without reaching it.
Common mistakes
  • Testing the sign of $f$ at the critical points themselves. Test a point between them.
  • A factor like $y^2$ does not change sign at $y=0$. That is what makes a semi-stable point.
  • Believing a solution can reach an equilibrium in finite time. For nice $f$, uniqueness prevents it.

Practice set · 10 problems

10 more problems on this section. Each one has an answer and a full step-by-step solution that explains why each step is taken. The numbers are fixed, so you can come back to the same problem.

Chapter 2 · Section 2.2

Separable equations

The simplest first-order equations to solve are those where the $x$ part and the $y$ part can be pulled apart onto different sides of the equation. Then you just integrate each side.

An equation is separable when the right side factors into a function of $x$ times a function of $y$: $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=g(x)h(y)$. Then all the $y$'s can be moved to one side and all the $x$'s to the other:

$$\frac{dy}{h(y)}=g(x)\,dx\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\int\frac{dy}{h(y)}=\int g(x)\,dx+c.$$

Why is this legal? Treating $dy$ and $dx$ as separate pieces is shorthand for the chain rule. If $H$ is an antiderivative of $\dfrac1h$, then $\dfrac{d}{dx}H(y(x))=\dfrac{y'}{h(y)}=g(x)$, and integrating both sides in $x$ gives the same result.

Three things to watch

  1. One constant is enough. Put $+c$ on one side only; two constants combine into one.
  2. Lost solutions. Dividing by $h(y)$ assumes $h(y)\neq0$. Every root $y=r$ of $h$ gives a constant solution $y=r$, which may not appear in the family. Check for it separately.
  3. Implicit answers are fine. If you cannot solve for $y$, leave the answer as $G(y)=F(x)+c$.

Typical algebra: $\ln|y|=x^2+c$ becomes $|y|=e^ce^{x^2}$, and then $y=Ae^{x^2}$ with $A=\pm e^c$. Allowing $A=0$ as well brings back the lost solution $y=0$.

Solution by integration

The very simplest case is $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=g(x)$, where the right side has no $y$ at all. Then $y=\int g(x)\,dx$. For example, $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=1+e^{2x}$ gives $y=x+\dfrac12e^{2x}+c$. Separable equations extend this idea.

Definition 2.2.1 · Separable equation

A first-order equation $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=g(x)\,h(y)$ is separable: the right side factors into a function of $x$ times a function of $y$.

For example, $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=y^2xe^{3x+4y}=(xe^{3x})(y^2e^{4y})$ is separable. But $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=y+\sin x$ is not: a sum cannot be written as a product of the two kinds of factor.

The method

Write $p(y)=\dfrac{1}{h(y)}$. Then the equation is $p(y)\dfrac{dy}{dx}=g(x)$. Integrate both sides with respect to $x$. On the left, $\int p(y)\dfrac{dy}{dx}\,dx=\int p(y)\,dy$ by substitution. So:

Steps
  1. Separate: $\dfrac{dy}{h(y)}=g(x)\,dx$.
  2. Integrate both sides: $\displaystyle\int\frac{dy}{h(y)}=\int g(x)\,dx$, giving $H(y)=G(x)+c$. One constant is enough.
  3. Solve for $y$ if you can (otherwise leave it implicit).
  4. Check for lost solutions: any $y=r$ with $h(r)=0$ is a constant solution that dividing by $h(y)$ may have thrown away.
Example 1Solving a separable equation

Solve $(1+x)\,dy-y\,dx=0$.

  1. Separate. Divide by $(1+x)y$: $\dfrac{dy}{y}=\dfrac{dx}{1+x}$.
  2. Integrate. $\ln|y|=\ln|1+x|+c_1$.
  3. Exponentiate to remove the logs: $|y|=e^{\ln|1+x|+c_1}=e^{c_1}|1+x|$.
  4. Remove the absolute values. $y=\pm e^{c_1}(1+x)$. Rename the constant $c=\pm e^{c_1}$: $y=c(1+x)$.
  5. Lost solutions. We divided by $y$, so check $y=0$: it does satisfy the equation, and it is the case $c=0$. Nothing is lost.
A shortcut with logarithms

When every integral gives a logarithm, write the constant as $\ln|c|$ instead of $c_1$. In Example 1: $\ln|y|=\ln|1+x|+\ln|c|=\ln|c(1+x)|$, so $y=c(1+x)$ directly.

Example 2A separable IVP

Solve $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=-\dfrac{x}{y}$, $y(4)=-3$.

  1. Separate. $y\,dy=-x\,dx$.
  2. Integrate. $\dfrac{y^2}{2}=-\dfrac{x^2}{2}+c_1$. Multiply by $2$: $x^2+y^2=c^2$ (a family of circles).
  3. Use the condition. $16+9=25=c^2$, so $x^2+y^2=25$.
  4. Pick the right branch. The point $(4,-3)$ has $y<0$, so $y=-\sqrt{25-x^2}$, valid on $(-5,5)$.
Example 3Losing a solution

Solve $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=y^2-4$.

  1. Separate. $\dfrac{dy}{y^2-4}=dx$.
  2. Partial fractions. $\dfrac{1}{(y-2)(y+2)}=\dfrac{1/4}{y-2}-\dfrac{1/4}{y+2}$.
  3. Integrate. $\dfrac14\ln|y-2|-\dfrac14\ln|y+2|=x+c_1$, so $\ln\left|\dfrac{y-2}{y+2}\right|=4x+4c_1$.
  4. Exponentiate. $\dfrac{y-2}{y+2}=ce^{4x}$, with $c=\pm e^{4c_1}$.
  5. Solve for $y$. $y-2=ce^{4x}(y+2)$, so $y(1-ce^{4x})=2+2ce^{4x}$ and $y=2\,\dfrac{1+ce^{4x}}{1-ce^{4x}}$.
  6. Check the constant solutions. $y^2-4=0$ at $y=\pm2$. $y=2$ is the case $c=0$. But $y=-2$ is a solution that no value of $c$ gives. It was lost when we divided by $y^2-4$, so it is a singular solution and must be listed separately.
Example 4An implicit solution

Solve $(e^{2y}-y)\cos x\,\dfrac{dy}{dx}=e^y\sin2x$, $y(0)=0$.

  1. Use $\sin2x=2\sin x\cos x$ and divide by $e^y\cos x$: $\dfrac{e^{2y}-y}{e^y}\,dy=\dfrac{2\sin x\cos x}{\cos x}\,dx$, that is $(e^y-ye^{-y})\,dy=2\sin x\,dx$.
  2. Integrate the left side. $\int e^y\,dy=e^y$. For $\int ye^{-y}\,dy$ use parts: $-ye^{-y}-e^{-y}$. So the left side is $e^y+ye^{-y}+e^{-y}$.
  3. Integrate the right side. $\int2\sin x\,dx=-2\cos x$.
  4. Together. $e^y+ye^{-y}+e^{-y}=-2\cos x+c$.
  5. Use $y(0)=0$. $1+0+1=-2+c$, so $c=4$: $$e^y+ye^{-y}+e^{-y}=4-2\cos x.$$ We cannot solve for $y$, so the answer stays implicit.
Be careful

Add the constant as soon as you integrate, not after you rearrange. $\ln y=x+c$ gives $y=ce^x$, but forgetting $c$ until later can give the wrong $y=e^x+c$.

Summary
  • Separable: $y'=g(x)h(y)$. Separate, integrate both sides, add one constant.
  • Use the initial condition to find $c$, and pick the correct branch of any square root.
  • Check whether a constant solution $h(r)=0$ was lost when you divided.

More worked examples

Example 5Separation with partial fractions

Solve $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=y^2-4$ with $y(0)=0$.

  1. Constant solutions first. $y^2-4=0$ gives $y=\pm2$. Note them before dividing.
  2. Separate. $\dfrac{dy}{y^2-4}=dx$.
  3. Partial fractions. $\dfrac{1}{(y-2)(y+2)}=\dfrac14\left(\dfrac1{y-2}-\dfrac1{y+2}\right)$.
  4. Integrate. $\dfrac14\ln\left|\dfrac{y-2}{y+2}\right|=x+c$, so $\dfrac{y-2}{y+2}=Ae^{4x}$.
  5. Initial condition. At $x=0$, $y=0$: $\dfrac{-2}{2}=A$, so $A=-1$.
  6. Solve for $y$. $y-2=-e^{4x}(y+2)$, so $y(1+e^{4x})=2-2e^{4x}$ and $$y=\frac{2(1-e^{4x})}{1+e^{4x}}.$$ As $x\to\infty$, $y\to-2$, the stable equilibrium.
Common mistakes
  • Trying to separate a sum: $y'=x+y$ is not separable.
  • Dropping the absolute value in $\ln|y|$ and then losing negative solutions.
  • Applying the initial condition before integrating.

Practice set · 12 problems

12 more problems on this section. Each one has an answer and a full step-by-step solution that explains why each step is taken. The numbers are fixed, so you can come back to the same problem.

Chapter 2 · Section 2.3

Linear equations

First-order linear equations come up constantly in circuits, mixing and cooling. There is a single method that solves every one of them: multiply by an integrating factor so that the left side becomes the derivative of a product.

A first-order linear equation in standard form is $y'+P(x)y=f(x)$. The left side looks almost like the result of the product rule. If we multiply by a cleverly chosen function $\mu(x)$, it becomes exactly a product-rule derivative:

$$\mu y'+\mu Py\overset{?}{=}(\mu y)'=\mu y'+\mu'y.$$

This works when $\mu'=\mu P$. That is a separable equation for $\mu$, with solution $\mu=e^{\int P\,dx}$. Once the left side is $(\mu y)'$, we integrate both sides and divide by $\mu$:

$$\big(\mu y\big)'=\mu f\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad y=\frac{1}{\mu}\left(\int\mu f\,dx+c\right).$$

So the integrating factor is not a trick to memorize. It is the one function that turns the left side into something you can integrate.

The solution always has two parts

The constant $c$ multiplies $\dfrac1\mu$, which solves the homogeneous equation $y'+Py=0$. The rest is one particular solution. So $y=y_c+y_p$, the same structure as in Chapter 3. In applications, $y_c$ often decays to zero (a transient), and $y_p$ describes the long-term behaviour.

Standard form

Definition 2.3.1 · Linear equation

A first-order equation of the form $a_1(x)\dfrac{dy}{dx}+a_0(x)y=g(x)$ is linear in $y$. Dividing by $a_1(x)$ gives the standard form

$$\frac{dy}{dx}+P(x)\,y=f(x). \tag{1}$$

When $g(x)=0$ the equation is homogeneous, otherwise nonhomogeneous. Always put the equation in standard form first, because $P(x)$ is read from it.

Where the integrating factor comes from

The left side of (1) looks almost like a product rule. If we multiply (1) by a function $\mu(x)$, we get $\mu y'+\mu Py=\mu f$. We want the left side to equal $\dfrac{d}{dx}[\mu y]=\mu y'+\mu'y$. Comparing, we need

$$\mu'=\mu P\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\frac{d\mu}{\mu}=P\,dx\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\mu=e^{\int P(x)\,dx}.$$

With this choice, (1) becomes $\dfrac{d}{dx}\big[\mu y\big]=\mu f$, and we simply integrate.

Method for $y'+P(x)y=f(x)$
  1. Put the equation in standard form and identify $P(x)$.
  2. Find the integrating factor $e^{\int P(x)\,dx}$ (no constant needed).
  3. Multiply the standard form by it. The left side becomes $\dfrac{d}{dx}\left[e^{\int P\,dx}y\right]$.
  4. Integrate both sides, and divide by the integrating factor.
Example 1Constant coefficient

Solve $\dfrac{dy}{dx}-3y=6$.

  1. Standard form already, with $P(x)=-3$ and $f(x)=6$.
  2. Integrating factor. $e^{\int(-3)\,dx}=e^{-3x}$.
  3. Multiply. $e^{-3x}y'-3e^{-3x}y=6e^{-3x}$, and the left side is $\dfrac{d}{dx}\big[e^{-3x}y\big]$.
  4. Integrate. $e^{-3x}y=\int6e^{-3x}\,dx=-2e^{-3x}+c$.
  5. Solve. Multiply by $e^{3x}$: $y=-2+ce^{3x}$, valid on $(-\infty,\infty)$.
Example 2Variable coefficient

Solve $x\dfrac{dy}{dx}-4y=x^6e^x$.

  1. Standard form. Divide by $x$: $\dfrac{dy}{dx}-\dfrac4xy=x^5e^x$. So $P(x)=-\dfrac4x$. We take $x>0$ because $P$ is not defined at $0$.
  2. Integrating factor. $e^{\int-4/x\,dx}=e^{-4\ln x}=e^{\ln x^{-4}}=x^{-4}$.
  3. Multiply. $x^{-4}y'-4x^{-5}y=xe^x$, which is $\dfrac{d}{dx}\big[x^{-4}y\big]=xe^x$.
  4. Integrate by parts. $\int xe^x\,dx=xe^x-e^x+c$.
  5. Solve. $x^{-4}y=xe^x-e^x+c$, so $y=x^5e^x-x^4e^x+cx^4$ on $(0,\infty)$.
Example 3A homogeneous equation

Solve $(x^2-9)\dfrac{dy}{dx}+xy=0$.

  1. Standard form. $\dfrac{dy}{dx}+\dfrac{x}{x^2-9}y=0$, so $P=\dfrac{x}{x^2-9}$. It is undefined at $x=\pm3$, so we work on an interval avoiding them, such as $(3,\infty)$.
  2. Integrating factor. $\int\dfrac{x}{x^2-9}\,dx=\dfrac12\ln|x^2-9|$, so the factor is $e^{\frac12\ln|x^2-9|}=\sqrt{x^2-9}$.
  3. Multiply and integrate. $\dfrac{d}{dx}\big[\sqrt{x^2-9}\,y\big]=0$, so $\sqrt{x^2-9}\,y=c$.
  4. Answer. $y=\dfrac{c}{\sqrt{x^2-9}}$ on $(3,\infty)$.
Example 4An IVP

Solve $\dfrac{dy}{dx}+y=x$, $y(0)=4$.

  1. Integrating factor. $P=1$, so $e^{x}$.
  2. Multiply. $\dfrac{d}{dx}\big[e^xy\big]=xe^x$.
  3. Integrate by parts. $e^xy=xe^x-e^x+c$, so $y=x-1+ce^{-x}$.
  4. Use $y(0)=4$. $4=0-1+c$, so $c=5$.
  5. Answer. $y=x-1+5e^{-x}$.
  6. Interpretation. The term $5e^{-x}$ dies out as $x\to\infty$; it is called a transient term. Every solution approaches $x-1$ for large $x$.
The structure of the answer

The general solution always has the form $y=y_c+y_p$: $y_c=ce^{-\int P\,dx}$ solves the homogeneous equation, and $y_p$ is one particular solution of the full equation. In Example 4, $y_c=ce^{-x}$ and $y_p=x-1$. The same structure appears for higher-order equations in Chapter 3.

Common mistakes
  • Reading $P(x)$ before dividing by the coefficient of $y'$.
  • Forgetting the sign of $P$: for $y'-3y$, $P=-3$.
  • Forgetting to divide the constant $c$ by the integrating factor at the end.
Summary
  • Standard form $y'+P(x)y=f(x)$. Integrating factor $e^{\int P\,dx}$.
  • After multiplying: $\frac{d}{dx}\left[e^{\int P}y\right]=e^{\int P}f$. Integrate and divide.
  • Solutions are valid on intervals where $P$ and $f$ are continuous.

More worked examples

Example 5A coefficient you must divide out

Solve $x\dfrac{dy}{dx}+2y=x^2$ for $x>0$, with $y(1)=1$.

  1. Standard form. Divide by $x$: $y'+\dfrac2xy=x$. So $P=\dfrac2x$ and $f=x$.
  2. Integrating factor. $\displaystyle\int\frac2x\,dx=2\ln x=\ln x^2$, so $\mu=e^{\ln x^2}=x^2$.
  3. Multiply. $x^2y'+2xy=x^3$, and the left side is $(x^2y)'$. Check: the product rule gives $x^2y'+2xy$.
  4. Integrate. $x^2y=\dfrac{x^4}{4}+c$, so $y=\dfrac{x^2}{4}+\dfrac{c}{x^2}$.
  5. Initial condition. $y(1)=\dfrac14+c=1$, so $c=\dfrac34$ and $y=\dfrac{x^2}{4}+\dfrac{3}{4x^2}$.
Common mistakes
  • Reading $P$ before dividing by the coefficient of $y'$.
  • Forgetting the sign: for $y'-3y=\dots$, $P=-3$ and $\mu=e^{-3x}$.
  • Adding the constant after dividing by $\mu$. It must be added before, so it gets divided too.

Practice set · 12 problems

12 more problems on this section. Each one has an answer and a full step-by-step solution that explains why each step is taken. The numbers are fixed, so you can come back to the same problem.

Chapter 2 · Section 2.7

Linear models

This section turns physical situations into first-order linear equations and solves them with the methods of 2.2 and 2.3. The skill to practise is setting up the equation: deciding what the unknown is, what its rate of change depends on, and what the initial condition is.

Every model in this section comes from one sentence of the form “the rate of change of a quantity equals something”. The work is translating that sentence into symbols, then solving with Section 2.2 or 2.3.

ModelSentenceEquation
Growth and decayrate proportional to the amount$\dfrac{dA}{dt}=kA$
Newton's law of coolingrate proportional to the temperature difference$\dfrac{dT}{dt}=k(T-T_m)$
Mixingrate in minus rate out$\dfrac{dA}{dt}=R_{in}-R_{out}$
Series circuitvoltage drops add up to the supply$L\dfrac{di}{dt}+Ri=E(t)$

In growth and decay, the constant $k$ is found from a second piece of data (a half-life, a doubling time, a later measurement). Always solve for $k$ exactly, as a logarithm, before rounding.

In mixing problems, each rate is (concentration) times (flow rate). The concentration going out is the tank's current concentration, $\dfrac{A(t)}{V(t)}$, which is why the equation involves $A$ itself.

Growth and decay

If a quantity grows or decays at a rate proportional to how much is present,

$$\frac{dx}{dt}=kx,\qquad x(t_0)=x_0,$$

then $x=x_0e^{k(t-t_0)}$ (usually $t_0=0$, so $x=x_0e^{kt}$). Growth has $k>0$, decay has $k<0$. The constant $k$ is usually found from one extra measurement.

Example 1Bacterial growth

A culture starts with $P_0$ bacteria. At $t=1$ h there are $\dfrac32P_0$. If the growth rate is proportional to the number present, when will the number have tripled?

  1. Model. $\dfrac{dP}{dt}=kP$, so $P=P_0e^{kt}$.
  2. Find $k$ from $P(1)$. $\dfrac32P_0=P_0e^{k}$, so $e^k=\dfrac32$ and $k=\ln\dfrac32\approx0.4055$.
  3. Set $P=3P_0$. $3P_0=P_0e^{kt}$, so $e^{kt}=3$ and $t=\dfrac{\ln3}{k}=\dfrac{\ln3}{\ln1.5}\approx2.71$ h.
  4. Note. The actual number $P_0$ cancelled. The time to triple does not depend on the starting amount.
Example 2Carbon dating

A fossilized bone contains $\dfrac{1}{1000}$ of the original amount of carbon-14. The half-life of C-14 is about $5730$ years. Estimate its age.

  1. Model. $A=A_0e^{kt}$.
  2. Find $k$ from the half-life. $\dfrac12A_0=A_0e^{5730k}$, so $k=-\dfrac{\ln2}{5730}\approx-0.00012097$.
  3. Set $A=\dfrac{A_0}{1000}$. $e^{kt}=\dfrac{1}{1000}$, so $kt=-\ln1000$.
  4. Solve. $t=\dfrac{5730\ln1000}{\ln2}\approx57{,}104$ years.

Newton's law of cooling

The rate at which an object's temperature changes is proportional to the difference between its temperature $T$ and the surrounding temperature $T_m$:

$$\frac{dT}{dt}=k(T-T_m),\qquad k<0.$$

This is linear (and separable). Its solution is $T=T_m+ce^{kt}$. The difference $T-T_m$ decays exponentially.

Example 3Cooling a cake

A cake comes out of the oven at $300^\circ$F into a room at $70^\circ$F. After $3$ minutes it is $200^\circ$F. How long until it reaches $75^\circ$F?

  1. Model. $T=70+ce^{kt}$.
  2. Initial condition. $T(0)=300$ gives $c=230$.
  3. Find $k$. $T(3)=200$: $130=230e^{3k}$, so $k=\dfrac13\ln\dfrac{13}{23}\approx-0.19018$.
  4. Answer. $T(t)=70+230e^{-0.19018t}$.
  5. Time to $75^\circ$. $5=230e^{kt}$ gives $t=\dfrac{\ln(5/230)}{k}\approx20.1$ min.
  6. Note. The model says $T$ never actually reaches $70^\circ$; it only approaches it. That is why we asked about $75^\circ$.

Mixtures

A tank holds a solution, and a solution of a different concentration flows in while the mixture flows out. If $A(t)$ is the amount of salt in the tank,

$$\frac{dA}{dt}=(\text{rate in})-(\text{rate out})=R_{\text{in}}-R_{\text{out}}.$$

Each rate is (concentration) times (flow rate). The concentration leaving is $\dfrac{A(t)}{\text{volume}}$, assuming the tank is well stirred.

Example 4A brine tank

A tank holds $300$ gal of brine containing $50$ lb of salt. Brine with $2$ lb/gal flows in at $3$ gal/min, and the well-mixed solution flows out at $3$ gal/min. Find $A(t)$.

  1. Rate in. $(2\ \text{lb/gal})(3\ \text{gal/min})=6$ lb/min.
  2. Rate out. In and out flows are equal, so the volume stays $300$ gal. Concentration out is $\dfrac{A}{300}$, so rate out is $\dfrac{A}{300}\cdot3=\dfrac{A}{100}$ lb/min.
  3. Equation. $\dfrac{dA}{dt}=6-\dfrac{A}{100}$, or $\dfrac{dA}{dt}+\dfrac{1}{100}A=6$, with $A(0)=50$.
  4. Integrating factor $e^{t/100}$: $\dfrac{d}{dt}\big[e^{t/100}A\big]=6e^{t/100}$, so $e^{t/100}A=600e^{t/100}+c$ and $A=600+ce^{-t/100}$.
  5. Initial condition. $50=600+c$, so $c=-550$: $A(t)=600-550e^{-t/100}$.
  6. Long run. $A\to600$ lb, which is $2$ lb/gal times $300$ gal: the tank ends up with the incoming concentration.
When the flow rates differ

If brine enters at $r_{\text{in}}$ and leaves at $r_{\text{out}}\neq r_{\text{in}}$, the volume changes: $V(t)=V_0+(r_{\text{in}}-r_{\text{out}})t$. The outflow concentration is then $\dfrac{A}{V(t)}$, and $P(t)$ in the linear equation is no longer constant.

Series circuits

In an LR circuit with inductance $L$, resistance $R$ and impressed voltage $E(t)$, Kirchhoff's law says the voltage drops add up to $E$:

$$L\frac{di}{dt}+Ri=E(t).$$

In an RC circuit with capacitance $C$, the charge $q$ satisfies $R\dfrac{dq}{dt}+\dfrac1Cq=E(t)$, and the current is $i=\dfrac{dq}{dt}$.

Example 5An LR circuit

A $12$-volt battery is connected to an LR circuit with $L=\dfrac12$ H and $R=10\ \Omega$. Find $i(t)$ if $i(0)=0$.

  1. Equation. $\dfrac12\dfrac{di}{dt}+10i=12$, or in standard form $\dfrac{di}{dt}+20i=24$.
  2. Integrating factor $e^{20t}$: $\dfrac{d}{dt}\big[e^{20t}i\big]=24e^{20t}$, so $e^{20t}i=\dfrac{6}{5}e^{20t}+c$.
  3. General solution. $i=\dfrac65+ce^{-20t}$.
  4. Initial condition. $0=\dfrac65+c$, so $c=-\dfrac65$ and $i(t)=\dfrac65-\dfrac65e^{-20t}$.
  5. Interpretation. $-\dfrac65e^{-20t}$ is the transient term. The steady-state current is $\dfrac65=\dfrac{E}{R}$ A.
Summary
  • Growth/decay: $x'=kx$, $x=x_0e^{kt}$. Find $k$ from one measurement.
  • Cooling: $T=T_m+ce^{kt}$.
  • Mixtures: $A'=$ rate in $-$ rate out, with concentration out $=A/V$.
  • Circuits: $Li'+Ri=E$ or $Rq'+q/C=E$.

More worked examples

Example 6Half-life

A radioactive substance has a half-life of 10 years. How long until only 20% remains?

  1. Model. $A=A_0e^{kt}$.
  2. Find $k$ from the half-life. $\dfrac12A_0=A_0e^{10k}$, so $k=\dfrac{\ln\frac12}{10}=-\dfrac{\ln2}{10}$.
  3. Set up the question. $0.2A_0=A_0e^{kt}$, so $t=\dfrac{\ln0.2}{k}=\dfrac{10\ln5}{\ln2}$.
  4. Answer. $t\approx23.2$ years. Note that $A_0$ cancels; the answer does not depend on the starting amount.
Common mistakes
  • Using the inflow concentration for the outflow in mixing problems.
  • Forgetting that the volume changes when the in and out flow rates differ.
  • Rounding $k$ early and carrying the error through.

Practice set · 12 problems

12 more problems on this section. Each one has an answer and a full step-by-step solution that explains why each step is taken. The numbers are fixed, so you can come back to the same problem.

Chapter 2 · Section 2.8

Nonlinear models

Exponential growth cannot go on forever: a population runs out of food and space. Adding a term that slows growth as the population gets large leads to the logistic equation, the most important nonlinear model in the course.

The model $\dfrac{dP}{dt}=kP$ predicts unlimited exponential growth, which cannot last: food, space or customers run out. The logistic equation fixes this by making the per-person growth rate fall as the population rises:

$$\frac{dP}{dt}=P(a-bP).$$

When $P$ is small, $a-bP\approx a$ and growth looks exponential. As $P$ approaches $\dfrac ab$, the bracket shrinks toward zero and growth stops. The number $K=\dfrac ab$ is the carrying capacity.

It is autonomous, so Section 2.1 gives the whole picture before solving: critical points at $P=0$ (unstable) and $P=K$ (stable). Every positive solution approaches $K$. Solutions starting below $\dfrac K2$ have an inflection point when they cross $\dfrac K2$, which is where growth is fastest. That is the familiar S-shaped curve.

Solving by separation and partial fractions gives

$$P(t)=\frac{aP_0}{bP_0+(a-bP_0)e^{-at}}.$$

The logistic equation

Write the growth model as $\dfrac{dP}{dt}=P\,f(P)$, where $f(P)$ is the growth rate per individual. For exponential growth $f$ is a constant. A more realistic choice is a rate that is largest for small populations and falls to zero at a carrying capacity $K$. The simplest such choice is a straight line $f(P)=a-bP$, which gives the logistic equation

$$\frac{dP}{dt}=P(a-bP).$$

From 2.1, its critical points are $0$ (a repeller) and $K=\dfrac ab$ (an attractor).

Solving it

The equation is separable. Use partial fractions:

$$\frac{dP}{P(a-bP)}=dt,\qquad\frac{1}{P(a-bP)}=\frac{1/a}{P}+\frac{b/a}{a-bP}.$$

Integrating gives $\dfrac1a\ln|P|-\dfrac1a\ln|a-bP|=t+c$, so $\ln\left|\dfrac{P}{a-bP}\right|=at+ac$ and $\dfrac{P}{a-bP}=c_1e^{at}$. Solving for $P$ and using $P(0)=P_0$:

Logistic solution $$P(t)=\frac{aP_0}{bP_0+(a-bP_0)e^{-at}}.$$

As $t\to\infty$, $e^{-at}\to0$ and $P\to\dfrac{aP_0}{bP_0}=\dfrac ab$. If $P_0<\dfrac{a}{2b}$, the graph is an S-shaped curve with an inflection point at $P=\dfrac{a}{2b}$, where the growth is fastest.

Interactive · logistic curve
Example 1The spread of flu

A student carrying a flu virus returns to a campus of $1000$ students. The rate of spread is proportional to the number infected $x$ and the number not infected $1000-x$. If $50$ are infected after $4$ days, how many after $6$ days?

  1. Model. $\dfrac{dx}{dt}=kx(1000-x)$, $x(0)=1$. This is logistic with $a=1000k$ and $b=k$.
  2. Use the formula. $x(t)=\dfrac{1000k\cdot1}{k+999ke^{-1000kt}}=\dfrac{1000}{1+999e^{-1000kt}}$.
  3. Find $k$ from $x(4)=50$. $50=\dfrac{1000}{1+999e^{-4000k}}$, so $1+999e^{-4000k}=20$ and $e^{-4000k}=\dfrac{19}{999}$.
  4. So $-1000k=\dfrac14\ln\dfrac{19}{999}\approx-0.9906$, and $x(t)=\dfrac{1000}{1+999e^{-0.9906t}}$.
  5. Evaluate at $t=6$. $x(6)=\dfrac{1000}{1+999e^{-5.9436}}\approx276$ students.

Other nonlinear models

Harvesting. If a population is harvested at a constant rate $h$, the model becomes $\dfrac{dP}{dt}=P(a-bP)-h$. Its critical points move, and if $h$ is too large there are none and the population dies out.

Chemical reactions. When $a$ grams of substance $A$ and $b$ grams of $B$ combine to form $X$, the rate is proportional to the amounts of $A$ and $B$ remaining: $\dfrac{dX}{dt}=k(\alpha-X)(\beta-X)$. This is separable and solved with partial fractions, just like the logistic equation.

Gompertz. $\dfrac{dP}{dt}=P(a-b\ln P)$ is used for tumour growth. Its carrying capacity is $e^{a/b}$.

Summary
  • Logistic: $P'=P(a-bP)$, carrying capacity $K=\frac ab$.
  • Solve by separation and partial fractions. $P\to\frac ab$ for any $P_0>0$.
  • Fastest growth at $P=\frac{a}{2b}$, the inflection point.

More worked examples

Example 2Reading the logistic curve

A population satisfies $\dfrac{dP}{dt}=P(0.4-0.002P)$ with $P(0)=50$. Find the carrying capacity, the population when growth is fastest, and the time that happens.

  1. Carrying capacity. $K=\dfrac{a}{b}=\dfrac{0.4}{0.002}=200$.
  2. Fastest growth. At $P=\dfrac K2=100$.
  3. Solution. $P=\dfrac{0.4\cdot50}{0.002\cdot50+(0.4-0.1)e^{-0.4t}}=\dfrac{20}{0.1+0.3e^{-0.4t}}=\dfrac{200}{1+3e^{-0.4t}}$.
  4. Time. $100=\dfrac{200}{1+3e^{-0.4t}}$ gives $3e^{-0.4t}=1$, so $t=\dfrac{\ln3}{0.4}\approx2.75$.

Chapter 3 · Section 3.1

Theory of linear equations

Before learning methods for second-order equations we need the theory that tells us what a complete answer looks like. The big result: for a linear equation, the general solution is a combination of a few basic solutions plus one particular solution.

This section has no new solving method. Instead it explains what a complete answer looks like for any linear equation, which is the frame for all of Chapters 3 and 4.

  1. Homogeneous equation ($g=0$), order $n$. You need exactly $n$ solutions that are linearly independent (none is a combination of the others). Then every solution is $y_c=c_1y_1+\dots+c_ny_n$. Such a set is a fundamental set.
  2. Nonhomogeneous equation. Find any one solution $y_p$. Then every solution is $y=y_c+y_p$. The difference of two solutions of the full equation always solves the homogeneous one, which is why this works.

So every problem in this chapter is two smaller problems: find $y_c$ (Sections 3.3 and 3.6) and find one $y_p$ (Sections 3.4, 3.5 and 3.6).

Testing independence with the Wronskian

For two functions, independence just means neither is a constant multiple of the other. For more functions, use the Wronskian, the determinant of the functions and their derivatives:

$$W(y_1,y_2)=\begin{vmatrix}y_1&y_2\\y_1'&y_2'\end{vmatrix}=y_1y_2'-y_2y_1'.$$

If $y_1,y_2$ are solutions of the same linear equation on an interval, then $W$ is either never zero there (independent) or always zero (dependent). One evaluation at a convenient point settles it.

Initial-value problems

An $n$th-order linear IVP is

$$a_n(x)y^{(n)}+a_{n-1}(x)y^{(n-1)}+\cdots+a_1(x)y'+a_0(x)y=g(x),$$ $$y(x_0)=y_0,\quad y'(x_0)=y_1,\quad\dots,\quad y^{(n-1)}(x_0)=y_{n-1}.$$
Theorem 3.1.1 · Existence of a unique solution

If $a_n,\dots,a_0$ and $g$ are continuous on an interval $I$, $a_n(x)\neq0$ for every $x$ in $I$, and $x_0$ is in $I$, then the IVP has exactly one solution on $I$.

The condition $a_n(x)\neq0$ matters. It is why Cauchy–Euler equations $ax^2y''+\cdots$ are solved on $x>0$: the leading coefficient vanishes at $0$.

Example 1Uniqueness in action

Check that $y=3e^{2x}+e^{-2x}-3x$ solves $y''-4y=12x$, $y(0)=4$, $y'(0)=1$, and explain why it is the only solution.

  1. Differentiate. $y'=6e^{2x}-2e^{-2x}-3$ and $y''=12e^{2x}+4e^{-2x}$.
  2. Substitute. $y''-4y=12e^{2x}+4e^{-2x}-12e^{2x}-4e^{-2x}+12x=12x$. It works.
  3. Initial conditions. $y(0)=3+1-0=4$ and $y'(0)=6-2-3=1$. Both hold.
  4. Uniqueness. The coefficients $1$, $0$, $-4$ and $g=12x$ are continuous everywhere and $a_2=1\neq0$, so Theorem 3.1.1 says this is the only solution on $(-\infty,\infty)$.

Boundary-value problems are different

If the conditions are given at two different points, such as $y(a)=y_0$ and $y(b)=y_1$, we have a boundary-value problem (BVP). The theorem above does not apply, and a BVP can have many solutions, exactly one, or none.

Example 2A BVP with many solutions

$x=c_1\cos4t+c_2\sin4t$ solves $x''+16x=0$. Solve it with (a) $x(0)=0$, $x\left(\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)=0$ and (b) $x(0)=0$, $x\left(\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)=1$.

  1. First condition (both parts). $x(0)=c_1=0$, so $x=c_2\sin4t$.
  2. (a) $x\left(\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)=c_2\sin2\pi=0$ for every $c_2$. So $x=c_2\sin4t$ is a solution for any $c_2$: infinitely many.
  3. (b) $x\left(\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)=c_2\sin2\pi=0$, which can never equal $1$: no solution.

Homogeneous equations and superposition

The equation is homogeneous when $g(x)=0$. For homogeneous equations, solutions can be added and scaled.

Theorem 3.1.2 · Superposition principle

If $y_1,y_2,\dots,y_k$ solve a homogeneous linear equation on $I$, so does $y=c_1y_1+c_2y_2+\cdots+c_ky_k$ for any constants.

Why: each derivative of a sum is the sum of derivatives, and constants come out. So the left side applied to $c_1y_1+c_2y_2$ is $c_1\cdot0+c_2\cdot0=0$. For example, $y_1=x^2$ and $y_2=x^2\ln x$ both solve $x^3y'''-2xy'+4y=0$, so $c_1x^2+c_2x^2\ln x$ does too.

Linear independence and the Wronskian

To build every solution we need solutions that are genuinely different, not just multiples of one another.

Definition 3.1.1 · Linear dependence

Functions $f_1,\dots,f_n$ are linearly dependent on $I$ if there are constants $c_1,\dots,c_n$, not all zero, with $c_1f_1+\cdots+c_nf_n=0$ for every $x$ in $I$. Otherwise they are linearly independent.

For two functions this just says one is a constant multiple of the other. For instance $\sin2x$ and $\sin x\cos x$ are dependent, since $\sin2x=2\sin x\cos x$. For more functions, or to be sure, use the Wronskian.

Wronskian $$W(f_1,f_2)=\begin{vmatrix}f_1&f_2\\f_1'&f_2'\end{vmatrix}=f_1f_2'-f_2f_1'.$$

(For $n$ functions, the $n\times n$ determinant with rows $f$, $f'$, $\dots$, $f^{(n-1)}$.)

Theorem 3.1.3 · Criterion for independent solutions

If $y_1,\dots,y_n$ are solutions of a homogeneous linear $n$th-order equation on $I$, they are linearly independent if and only if $W(y_1,\dots,y_n)\neq0$ for every $x$ in $I$.

Example 3A fundamental set

$y_1=e^{3x}$ and $y_2=e^{-3x}$ both solve $y''-9y=0$. Show they form a fundamental set and write the general solution.

  1. Wronskian. $W=\begin{vmatrix}e^{3x}&e^{-3x}\\3e^{3x}&-3e^{-3x}\end{vmatrix}=e^{3x}(-3e^{-3x})-e^{-3x}(3e^{3x})=-3-3=-6$.
  2. Conclusion. $W=-6\neq0$ for every $x$, so the solutions are independent on $(-\infty,\infty)$.
  3. General solution. $y=c_1e^{3x}+c_2e^{-3x}$.
Fundamental set and general solution

Any $n$ linearly independent solutions $y_1,\dots,y_n$ of a homogeneous $n$th-order linear equation form a fundamental set. Every solution is then $y=c_1y_1+\cdots+c_ny_n$, the general solution.

Nonhomogeneous equations

For $g(x)\neq0$, let $y_p$ be any one solution (a particular solution), and let $y_c=c_1y_1+\cdots+c_ny_n$ be the general solution of the homogeneous equation (the complementary function). Then

Theorem 3.1.5 · General solution, nonhomogeneous $$y=y_c+y_p=c_1y_1+\cdots+c_ny_n+y_p.$$

Why: if $y$ is any solution, then $y-y_p$ solves the homogeneous equation (the $g$'s cancel), so $y-y_p=y_c$.

Example 4Putting it together

Given that $y_p=-\dfrac{11}{12}-\dfrac12x$ solves $y'''-6y''+11y'-6y=3x$, and $y_1=e^x$, $y_2=e^{2x}$, $y_3=e^{3x}$ solve the homogeneous equation, write the general solution.

  1. Check $y_p$. $y_p'=-\dfrac12$, $y_p''=y_p'''=0$. Then $11\left(-\dfrac12\right)-6\left(-\dfrac{11}{12}-\dfrac12x\right)=-\dfrac{11}{2}+\dfrac{11}{2}+3x=3x$. Good.
  2. $y_c$. The three exponentials have different rates, so they are independent: $y_c=c_1e^x+c_2e^{2x}+c_3e^{3x}$.
  3. General solution. $y=c_1e^x+c_2e^{2x}+c_3e^{3x}-\dfrac{11}{12}-\dfrac12x$.
Superposition for nonhomogeneous equations

If $y_{p_1}$ solves the equation with right side $g_1$ and $y_{p_2}$ solves it with $g_2$, then $y_{p_1}+y_{p_2}$ solves it with $g_1+g_2$. This lets you split a complicated right side into simpler pieces, which is used constantly in 3.4.

Reduction of order (Zill 3.2)

If you know one solution $y_1$ of $y''+P(x)y'+Q(x)y=0$, a second is $y_2=y_1\displaystyle\int\frac{e^{-\int P\,dx}}{y_1^2}\,dx$. This is how the $xe^{mx}$ and $x^m\ln x$ solutions for repeated roots are found.

Summary
  • Continuous coefficients and $a_n\neq0$: an IVP has exactly one solution.
  • BVPs can have zero, one or infinitely many solutions.
  • $W\neq0$ means independent. $n$ independent solutions form a fundamental set.
  • General solution $y=y_c+y_p$.

More worked examples

Example 5A Wronskian that decides independence

Show that $y_1=e^{2x}$ and $y_2=xe^{2x}$ form a fundamental set for $y''-4y'+4y=0$.

  1. Both are solutions. For $y_2$: $y_2'=(1+2x)e^{2x}$, $y_2''=(4+4x)e^{2x}$, and $(4+4x)-4(1+2x)+4x=0$. The check for $y_1$ is similar.
  2. Wronskian. $$W=\begin{vmatrix}e^{2x}&xe^{2x}\\2e^{2x}&(1+2x)e^{2x}\end{vmatrix}=(1+2x)e^{4x}-2xe^{4x}=e^{4x}.$$
  3. Conclusion. $e^{4x}\neq0$ for every $x$, so the solutions are independent and $y=c_1e^{2x}+c_2xe^{2x}$ is the general solution.
Common mistakes
  • Writing $y=y_p$ alone for a nonhomogeneous equation. The answer is $y_c+y_p$.
  • Applying initial conditions to $y_c$ before adding $y_p$.
  • Sign of the Wronskian: it is $y_1y_2'-y_2y_1'$, top-left times bottom-right first.

Chapter 3 · Section 3.3

Homogeneous linear equations with constant coefficients

When the coefficients are constants, we can always find the general solution. The idea is to guess an exponential, which turns the differential equation into a polynomial equation.

The equation $ay''+by'+cy=0$ asks for a function whose derivatives combine to zero. The exponential $e^{mx}$ is the natural guess because differentiating it only multiplies by $m$. Substituting turns the differential equation into the ordinary quadratic $am^2+bm+c=0$, so all the calculus disappears.

A quadratic has three kinds of roots, and each gives a different kind of solution:

  • Two real roots: two exponentials. Growth, decay, or one of each.
  • A repeated root: only one exponential, so we need a second solution. It is $xe^{mx}$, found by reduction of order.
  • Complex roots $\alpha\pm i\beta$: Euler's formula $e^{i\beta x}=\cos\beta x+i\sin\beta x$ turns the complex exponentials into real oscillations, $e^{\alpha x}\cos\beta x$ and $e^{\alpha x}\sin\beta x$. Here $\alpha$ controls growth or decay and $\beta$ is the frequency.

For higher-order equations the idea is the same: a polynomial of degree $n$, $n$ roots counted with multiplicity, and one solution per root (multiplied by $x,x^2,\dots$ when roots repeat).

Why try $y=e^{mx}$

The first-order equation $y'+ay=0$ has solution $y=e^{-ax}$. In general, an exponential has the special property that each derivative is a constant times itself: $(e^{mx})'=me^{mx}$, $(e^{mx})''=m^2e^{mx}$. So in

$$ay''+by'+cy=0$$

every term will be a multiple of $e^{mx}$. Substituting $y=e^{mx}$:

$$am^2e^{mx}+bme^{mx}+ce^{mx}=e^{mx}(am^2+bm+c)=0.$$

Since $e^{mx}$ is never zero, we need

Auxiliary equation $$am^2+bm+c=0.$$

Its roots are $m=\dfrac{-b\pm\sqrt{b^2-4ac}}{2a}$, and there are three cases depending on the discriminant.

Case I · Distinct real roots ($b^2-4ac>0$)

We get two exponentials $e^{m_1x}$ and $e^{m_2x}$ with different rates, which are independent:

$$y=c_1e^{m_1x}+c_2e^{m_2x}.$$

Case II · Repeated real root ($b^2-4ac=0$)

Now $m_1=m_2=-\dfrac{b}{2a}$ and we only get one exponential. Reduction of order supplies the second solution $xe^{m_1x}$ (you can check by substitution that it works because $2am_1+b=0$):

$$y=c_1e^{m_1x}+c_2xe^{m_1x}.$$

Case III · Complex roots ($b^2-4ac<0$)

The roots are $m=\alpha\pm i\beta$ with $\beta>0$. Formally the solutions are $e^{(\alpha+i\beta)x}$ and $e^{(\alpha-i\beta)x}$, but we want real functions. Euler's formula

$$e^{i\theta}=\cos\theta+i\sin\theta$$

gives $e^{(\alpha\pm i\beta)x}=e^{\alpha x}(\cos\beta x\pm i\sin\beta x)$. Adding and subtracting these two solutions (superposition) and dividing by $2$ or $2i$ gives the real solutions $e^{\alpha x}\cos\beta x$ and $e^{\alpha x}\sin\beta x$:

$$y=e^{\alpha x}\big(c_1\cos\beta x+c_2\sin\beta x\big).$$
Interactive · how the roots shape the solution
Example 1One of each case

Solve (a) $2y''-5y'-3y=0$, (b) $y''-10y'+25y=0$, (c) $y''+4y'+7y=0$.

  1. (a) Auxiliary equation. $2m^2-5m-3=(2m+1)(m-3)=0$, so $m_1=-\dfrac12$, $m_2=3$. Case I.
  2. (a) Solution. $y=c_1e^{-x/2}+c_2e^{3x}$.
  3. (b) Auxiliary equation. $m^2-10m+25=(m-5)^2=0$, so $m=5$ twice. Case II.
  4. (b) Solution. $y=c_1e^{5x}+c_2xe^{5x}$.
  5. (c) Auxiliary equation. $m^2+4m+7=0$ gives $m=\dfrac{-4\pm\sqrt{16-28}}{2}=\dfrac{-4\pm2\sqrt3\,i}{2}=-2\pm\sqrt3\,i$. Case III with $\alpha=-2$, $\beta=\sqrt3$.
  6. (c) Solution. $y=e^{-2x}\big(c_1\cos\sqrt3\,x+c_2\sin\sqrt3\,x\big)$.
Example 2An IVP

Solve $4y''+4y'+17y=0$, $y(0)=-1$, $y'(0)=2$.

  1. Roots. $4m^2+4m+17=0$ gives $m=\dfrac{-4\pm\sqrt{16-272}}{8}=\dfrac{-4\pm16i}{8}=-\dfrac12\pm2i$.
  2. General solution. $y=e^{-x/2}\big(c_1\cos2x+c_2\sin2x\big)$.
  3. First condition. $y(0)=c_1=-1$.
  4. Differentiate. $y'=-\dfrac12e^{-x/2}(c_1\cos2x+c_2\sin2x)+e^{-x/2}(-2c_1\sin2x+2c_2\cos2x)$.
  5. Second condition. $y'(0)=-\dfrac12c_1+2c_2=\dfrac12+2c_2=2$, so $c_2=\dfrac34$.
  6. Answer. $y=e^{-x/2}\left(-\cos2x+\dfrac34\sin2x\right)$.
Two equations worth remembering

$y''+k^2y=0$ has $y=c_1\cos kx+c_2\sin kx$. $y''-k^2y=0$ has $y=c_1e^{kx}+c_2e^{-kx}$, or equivalently $y=c_1\cosh kx+c_2\sinh kx$. The hyperbolic form is often easier for boundary-value problems on a finite interval.

Higher-order equations

For $a_ny^{(n)}+\cdots+a_1y'+a_0y=0$ the auxiliary equation is the degree-$n$ polynomial $a_nm^n+\cdots+a_1m+a_0=0$. Each root contributes as above, and a root repeated $k$ times contributes $e^{mx},xe^{mx},\dots,x^{k-1}e^{mx}$.

Example 3A third-order equation

Solve $y'''+3y''-4y=0$.

  1. Auxiliary equation. $m^3+3m^2-4=0$.
  2. Find a root by inspection. $m=1$: $1+3-4=0$. So $m-1$ is a factor.
  3. Divide. $m^3+3m^2-4=(m-1)(m^2+4m+4)=(m-1)(m+2)^2$.
  4. Roots. $m=1$ and $m=-2$ (twice).
  5. Solution. $y=c_1e^{x}+c_2e^{-2x}+c_3xe^{-2x}$.
Example 4Repeated complex roots

Solve $y^{(4)}+2y''+y=0$.

  1. Auxiliary equation. $m^4+2m^2+1=(m^2+1)^2=0$, so $m=\pm i$, each twice.
  2. Solution. The pair $\pm i$ gives $\cos x$, $\sin x$. Its repeat multiplies by $x$: $y=c_1\cos x+c_2\sin x+c_3x\cos x+c_4x\sin x$.
Summary
  • $y=e^{mx}$ turns $ay''+by'+cy=0$ into $am^2+bm+c=0$.
  • Distinct real: $c_1e^{m_1x}+c_2e^{m_2x}$. Repeated: $(c_1+c_2x)e^{mx}$. Complex: $e^{\alpha x}(c_1\cos\beta x+c_2\sin\beta x)$.
  • Higher order: factor the polynomial; repeated roots pick up powers of $x$.

More worked examples

Example 5Complex roots with initial conditions

Solve $y''+2y'+5y=0$, $y(0)=1$, $y'(0)=3$.

  1. Auxiliary equation. $m^2+2m+5=0$, so $m=\dfrac{-2\pm\sqrt{4-20}}{2}=-1\pm2i$.
  2. General solution. $\alpha=-1$, $\beta=2$: $y=e^{-x}(c_1\cos2x+c_2\sin2x)$.
  3. $y(0)=1$. $c_1=1$.
  4. $y'(0)=3$. Differentiating, $y'(0)=-c_1+2c_2=3$, so $c_2=2$.
  5. Answer. $y=e^{-x}(\cos2x+2\sin2x)$: oscillations that die away.
Common mistakes
  • Writing $e^{(\alpha\pm i\beta)x}$ in the final answer instead of the real form.
  • Forgetting the factor $x$ for a repeated root.
  • Using $\beta$ with its sign or doubling it: for $m=-1\pm2i$, the frequency is $2$.

Chapter 3 · Section 3.4

Undetermined coefficients

To solve a nonhomogeneous equation we need one particular solution $y_p$. When the coefficients are constant and the right side is a polynomial, exponential, sine, cosine, or a product of these, we can guess the form of $y_p$ with unknown coefficients and then find them.

Undetermined coefficients rests on one observation: for some right sides, the left side $ay''+by'+cy$ can only produce more of the same kind of function. Differentiate a polynomial and you get a polynomial; an exponential, an exponential; a sine, sines and cosines. So the particular solution must be of the same family as $g(x)$, with unknown coefficients that we find by substituting.

$g(x)$ containsTry $y_p=$
a polynomial of degree $n$a full polynomial of degree $n$
$e^{rx}$$Ae^{rx}$
$\sin kx$ or $\cos kx$$A\cos kx+B\sin kx$ (always both)
products of thesethe product of the forms, e.g. $(Ax+B)e^{rx}$

The duplication rule

If any term of the guess already solves the homogeneous equation, it produces zero on the left and can never match $g$. Multiply that part of the guess by $x$ (or $x^2$ if needed) until no term duplicates $y_c$. This is why you always find $y_c$ first.

The method only works for constant coefficients and for these families of $g$. For $\tan x$, $\sec x$, $\ln x$ or $\dfrac1x$, use variation of parameters (Section 3.5).

The idea

For $ay''+by'+cy=g(x)$, the general solution is $y=y_c+y_p$. We find $y_c$ by 3.3. For $y_p$ we use the fact that derivatives of polynomials, exponentials, sines and cosines are again functions of the same kind. So if $g(x)$ is one of these, $y_p$ should be too.

$g(x)$Form of $y_p$
$1$ (any constant)$A$
$5x+7$$Ax+B$
$3x^2-2$$Ax^2+Bx+C$
$\sin4x$ or $\cos4x$$A\cos4x+B\sin4x$
$e^{5x}$$Ae^{5x}$
$(9x-2)e^{5x}$$(Ax+B)e^{5x}$
$x^2e^{5x}$$(Ax^2+Bx+C)e^{5x}$
$e^{3x}\sin4x$$Ae^{3x}\cos4x+Be^{3x}\sin4x$
$xe^{3x}\cos4x$$(Ax+B)e^{3x}\cos4x+(Cx+E)e^{3x}\sin4x$

Notice: a polynomial of degree $n$ needs the full polynomial of degree $n$, and a sine needs both sine and cosine.

Example 1Polynomial right side

Solve $y''+4y'-2y=2x^2-3x+6$.

  1. Complementary function. $m^2+4m-2=0$ gives $m=-2\pm\sqrt6$, so $y_c=c_1e^{-(2+\sqrt6)x}+c_2e^{(-2+\sqrt6)x}$.
  2. Guess. $g$ is a quadratic, so $y_p=Ax^2+Bx+C$. Then $y_p'=2Ax+B$ and $y_p''=2A$.
  3. Substitute.$$\begin{aligned}&2A+4(2Ax+B)-2(Ax^2+Bx+C)\\&\quad=-2Ax^2+(8A-2B)x+(2A+4B-2C).\end{aligned}$$
  4. Match coefficients with $2x^2-3x+6$: $-2A=2$, $8A-2B=-3$, $2A+4B-2C=6$.
  5. Solve from the top down. $A=-1$. Then $-8-2B=-3$ gives $B=-\dfrac52$. Then $-2-10-2C=6$ gives $C=-9$.
  6. Answer. $y=c_1e^{-(2+\sqrt6)x}+c_2e^{(-2+\sqrt6)x}-x^2-\dfrac52x-9$.
Example 2Trigonometric right side

Find a particular solution of $y''-y'+y=2\sin3x$.

  1. Guess both sine and cosine. $y_p=A\cos3x+B\sin3x$. (Just $A\sin3x$ fails, because $y'$ produces a cosine that nothing else can cancel.)
  2. Derivatives. $y_p'=-3A\sin3x+3B\cos3x$ and $y_p''=-9A\cos3x-9B\sin3x$.
  3. Substitute and collect. $\cos3x$: $-9A-3B+A=-8A-3B$. $\sin3x$: $-9B+3A+B=3A-8B$.
  4. Match. $-8A-3B=0$ and $3A-8B=2$.
  5. Solve. From the first, $B=-\dfrac83A$. Then $3A+\dfrac{64}{3}A=2$, so $\dfrac{73}{3}A=2$, $A=\dfrac{6}{73}$ and $B=-\dfrac{16}{73}$.
  6. Answer. $y_p=\dfrac{6}{73}\cos3x-\dfrac{16}{73}\sin3x$.
Example 3Superposition of right sides

Find a particular solution of $y''-2y'-3y=4x-5+6xe^{2x}$.

  1. Split the right side. Find $y_{p_1}$ for $4x-5$ and $y_{p_2}$ for $6xe^{2x}$, then add.
  2. $y_{p_1}=Ax+B$. Substituting: $-2A-3Ax-3B=4x-5$. So $-3A=4$, $A=-\dfrac43$, and $-2A-3B=-5$, so $\dfrac83-3B=-5$, $B=\dfrac{23}{9}$.
  3. $y_{p_2}=(Cx+E)e^{2x}$. Its derivatives are $(2Cx+2E+C)e^{2x}$ and $(4Cx+4E+4C)e^{2x}$.
  4. Substitute $y_{p_2}$. The bracket is $4Cx+4E+4C-2(2Cx+2E+C)-3(Cx+E)=-3Cx+2C-3E$. Matching $6x$: $-3C=6$, $C=-2$, and $2C-3E=0$, $E=-\dfrac43$.
  5. Add. $y_p=-\dfrac43x+\dfrac{23}{9}-2xe^{2x}-\dfrac43e^{2x}$.

The glitch: when the guess solves the homogeneous equation

Example 4Duplicate with $y_c$

Find a particular solution of $y''-5y'+4y=8e^x$.

  1. The obvious guess fails. Try $y_p=Ae^x$: $Ae^x-5Ae^x+4Ae^x=0$, which can never equal $8e^x$.
  2. Why. $m^2-5m+4=(m-1)(m-4)$, so $y_c=c_1e^x+c_2e^{4x}$. Our guess $Ae^x$ is already a homogeneous solution, so it is sent to $0$.
  3. Fix: multiply by $x$. Try $y_p=Axe^x$. Then $y_p'=A(1+x)e^x$ and $y_p''=A(2+x)e^x$.
  4. Substitute. $A(2+x)-5A(1+x)+4Ax=-3A$, times $e^x$. So $-3A=8$ and $A=-\dfrac83$.
  5. Answer. $y_p=-\dfrac83xe^x$.
Multiplication rule

If any term of your guessed $y_p$ duplicates a term of $y_c$, multiply that guess by $x^n$, where $n$ is the smallest positive integer that removes every duplication. For a repeated root this can mean $x^2$.

Example 5An IVP with a duplication

Solve $y''+y=4x+10\sin x$, $y(\pi)=0$, $y'(\pi)=2$.

  1. $y_c$. $m=\pm i$, so $y_c=c_1\cos x+c_2\sin x$.
  2. Guess. For $4x$: $Ax+B$. For $10\sin x$: normally $C\cos x+E\sin x$, but those duplicate $y_c$, so multiply by $x$: $Cx\cos x+Ex\sin x$.
  3. Polynomial part. $(Ax+B)''+(Ax+B)=Ax+B=4x$, so $A=4$, $B=0$.
  4. Trig part. For $Cx\cos x+Ex\sin x$, the second derivative plus itself is $-2C\sin x+2E\cos x$. Matching $10\sin x$: $C=-5$, $E=0$.
  5. General solution. $y=c_1\cos x+c_2\sin x+4x-5x\cos x$.
  6. $y(\pi)=0$. $-c_1+4\pi+5\pi=0$, so $c_1=9\pi$.
  7. $y'(\pi)=2$. $y'=-c_1\sin x+c_2\cos x+4-5\cos x+5x\sin x$, so $y'(\pi)=-c_2+4+5=2$ and $c_2=7$.
  8. Answer. $y=9\pi\cos x+7\sin x+4x-5x\cos x$.
When the method does not apply

Undetermined coefficients only works for constant coefficients and right sides like those in the table. For $\ln x$, $\dfrac1x$, $\tan x$, $\sec x$ and similar, use variation of parameters (3.5).

Summary
  • Find $y_c$ first. Then guess $y_p$ of the same type as $g$, with full polynomials and both sine and cosine.
  • Substitute, collect like terms, match coefficients.
  • If the guess duplicates $y_c$, multiply by $x$ (or $x^2$).
  • Split sums on the right side and add the particular solutions.

More worked examples

Example 6Exponential times polynomial

Find a particular solution of $y''-y=xe^{2x}$.

  1. $y_c$. $m^2-1=0$, so $y_c=c_1e^x+c_2e^{-x}$. Nothing with $e^{2x}$, so no duplication.
  2. Guess. Polynomial of degree 1 times $e^{2x}$: $y_p=(Ax+B)e^{2x}$.
  3. Derivatives. $y_p'=(2Ax+A+2B)e^{2x}$ and $y_p''=(4Ax+4A+4B)e^{2x}$.
  4. Substitute. $y_p''-y_p=(3Ax+4A+3B)e^{2x}=xe^{2x}$.
  5. Match. $3A=1$ and $4A+3B=0$, so $A=\dfrac13$ and $B=-\dfrac49$.
  6. Answer. $y_p=\left(\dfrac13x-\dfrac49\right)e^{2x}$.
Common mistakes
  • Guessing only $A\sin kx$ for $\sin kx$. Both sine and cosine are needed.
  • Guessing $Ax^2$ for $x^2$. Use the full polynomial $Ax^2+Bx+C$.
  • Checking duplication against $g$ instead of $y_c$.

Practice set · 15 problems

15 more problems on this section. Each one has an answer and a full step-by-step solution that explains why each step is taken. The numbers are fixed, so you can come back to the same problem.

Chapter 3 · Section 3.5

Variation of parameters

Undetermined coefficients only works for a few kinds of right side. Variation of parameters works for any continuous right side, and even for variable coefficients, as long as we know the complementary function.

The homogeneous solution is $y_c=c_1y_1+c_2y_2$ with constant $c_1,c_2$. Variation of parameters asks: what if we let the constants become functions, $y_p=u_1(x)y_1+u_2(x)y_2$? Two unknown functions but only one equation leaves room for one extra condition. Choosing $u_1'y_1+u_2'y_2=0$ keeps the second derivative simple, and substituting gives a pair of linear equations for $u_1'$ and $u_2'$. Cramer's rule solves them:

$$u_1'=-\frac{y_2f}{W},\qquad u_2'=\frac{y_1f}{W},\qquad W=y_1y_2'-y_2y_1'.$$

The method always works, for any continuous $f$, and even for variable coefficients once $y_1,y_2$ are known. The price is two integrals, which can be hard.

The two rules that prevent most errors

  1. Standard form first. The $f$ in the formulas is the right side after dividing by the coefficient of $y''$.
  2. No constants when integrating. They would only add multiples of $y_1$ and $y_2$, which are already in $y_c$.

The idea

Put the equation in standard form

$$y''+P(x)y'+Q(x)y=f(x),$$

and suppose $y_c=c_1y_1+c_2y_2$ is known. The idea is to replace the constants by functions and look for

$$y_p=u_1(x)\,y_1(x)+u_2(x)\,y_2(x).$$

We have two unknown functions but only one equation, so we are free to impose one extra condition to make life easier.

Deriving the formulas

  1. By the product rule, $y_p'=u_1y_1'+u_2y_2'+\big(u_1'y_1+u_2'y_2\big)$.
  2. Extra condition: set $u_1'y_1+u_2'y_2=0$, so that $y_p'=u_1y_1'+u_2y_2'$ has no $u'$ terms.
  3. Then $y_p''=u_1y_1''+u_2y_2''+u_1'y_1'+u_2'y_2'$.
  4. Substitute into the equation. The terms with $u_1$ collect to $u_1(y_1''+Py_1'+Qy_1)=0$ because $y_1$ is a homogeneous solution, and the same for $u_2$. What remains is $u_1'y_1'+u_2'y_2'=f(x)$.

So $u_1'$ and $u_2'$ satisfy the linear system

$$\begin{aligned}y_1u_1'+y_2u_2'&=0\\y_1'u_1'+y_2'u_2'&=f(x).\end{aligned}$$

Cramer's rule solves it, and the determinant of the system is the Wronskian $W=y_1y_2'-y_2y_1'$.

Variation of parameters $$u_1'=-\frac{y_2f(x)}{W},\qquad u_2'=\frac{y_1f(x)}{W},\qquad y_p=u_1y_1+u_2y_2.$$
Steps
  1. Put the equation in standard form (coefficient of $y''$ equal to $1$) to read off $f(x)$.
  2. Find $y_1$, $y_2$ and $W$.
  3. Compute $u_1'$, $u_2'$ and integrate (no constants needed).
  4. $y_p=u_1y_1+u_2y_2$, and $y=y_c+y_p$.
Example 1A repeated root

Solve $y''-4y'+4y=(x+1)e^{2x}$.

  1. $y_c$. $(m-2)^2=0$, so $y_1=e^{2x}$, $y_2=xe^{2x}$.
  2. Wronskian. $W=\begin{vmatrix}e^{2x}&xe^{2x}\\2e^{2x}&(2x+1)e^{2x}\end{vmatrix}=(2x+1)e^{4x}-2xe^{4x}=e^{4x}$.
  3. $f(x)$. Already standard form, so $f=(x+1)e^{2x}$.
  4. $u_1'$ and $u_2'$. $u_1'=-\dfrac{xe^{2x}(x+1)e^{2x}}{e^{4x}}=-x^2-x$ and $u_2'=\dfrac{e^{2x}(x+1)e^{2x}}{e^{4x}}=x+1$.
  5. Integrate. $u_1=-\dfrac{x^3}{3}-\dfrac{x^2}{2}$ and $u_2=\dfrac{x^2}{2}+x$.
  6. Assemble. $y_p=\left(-\dfrac{x^3}{3}-\dfrac{x^2}{2}\right)e^{2x}+\left(\dfrac{x^2}{2}+x\right)xe^{2x}=\left(\dfrac{x^3}{6}+\dfrac{x^2}{2}\right)e^{2x}$.
  7. Answer. $y=c_1e^{2x}+c_2xe^{2x}+\dfrac16x^3e^{2x}+\dfrac12x^2e^{2x}$.
Example 2A right side undetermined coefficients cannot handle

Solve $4y''+36y=\csc3x$.

  1. Standard form. Divide by $4$: $y''+9y=\dfrac14\csc3x$, so $f(x)=\dfrac14\csc3x$. Forgetting this step is the most common error.
  2. $y_c$. $m=\pm3i$, so $y_1=\cos3x$, $y_2=\sin3x$, and $W=\cos3x\cdot3\cos3x-\sin3x\cdot(-3\sin3x)=3$.
  3. $u_1'$. $-\dfrac{\sin3x\cdot\dfrac14\csc3x}{3}=-\dfrac{1}{12}$, so $u_1=-\dfrac{x}{12}$.
  4. $u_2'$. $\dfrac{\cos3x\cdot\dfrac14\csc3x}{3}=\dfrac{1}{12}\cdot\dfrac{\cos3x}{\sin3x}$, so $u_2=\dfrac{1}{36}\ln|\sin3x|$.
  5. Answer. $y=c_1\cos3x+c_2\sin3x-\dfrac{1}{12}x\cos3x+\dfrac{1}{36}\sin3x\,\ln|\sin3x|$, on an interval like $\left(0,\dfrac{\pi}{6}\right)$ where $\csc3x$ is continuous.
Example 3Integrals that cannot be done

Solve $y''-y=\dfrac1x$.

  1. $y_c$. $y_1=e^x$, $y_2=e^{-x}$, and $W=e^x(-e^{-x})-e^{-x}e^x=-2$.
  2. $u_1'$ and $u_2'$. $u_1'=-\dfrac{e^{-x}(1/x)}{-2}=\dfrac{e^{-x}}{2x}$ and $u_2'=\dfrac{e^x(1/x)}{-2}=-\dfrac{e^x}{2x}$.
  3. These have no elementary antiderivative. Write them as definite integrals from some $x_0>0$: $u_1=\dfrac12\displaystyle\int_{x_0}^{x}\frac{e^{-t}}{t}\,dt$, $u_2=-\dfrac12\displaystyle\int_{x_0}^{x}\frac{e^{t}}{t}\,dt$.
  4. Answer. $y=c_1e^x+c_2e^{-x}+\dfrac12e^x\displaystyle\int_{x_0}^{x}\frac{e^{-t}}{t}\,dt-\dfrac12e^{-x}\displaystyle\int_{x_0}^{x}\frac{e^{t}}{t}\,dt$.
Higher-order equations

For $y'''+P_2y''+P_1y'+P_0y=f$ the same idea gives $u_k'=\dfrac{W_k}{W}$, where $W_k$ is the Wronskian with its $k$th column replaced by $(0,0,f)$. For second order this reduces to the formulas above.

Summary
  • Standard form first: $f(x)$ is the right side after dividing by the coefficient of $y''$.
  • $u_1'=-\frac{y_2f}{W}$, $u_2'=\frac{y_1f}{W}$, $y_p=u_1y_1+u_2y_2$.
  • Works for any continuous $f$ and for variable coefficients, given $y_1$, $y_2$.

More worked examples

Example 4A secant right side

Solve $y''+y=\sec x$ on $-\dfrac\pi2<x<\dfrac\pi2$.

  1. $y_c$. $y_1=\cos x$, $y_2=\sin x$, and $W=\cos^2x+\sin^2x=1$.
  2. $u_1'$. $-\dfrac{\sin x\sec x}{1}=-\tan x$, so $u_1=\ln|\cos x|$.
  3. $u_2'$. $\dfrac{\cos x\sec x}{1}=1$, so $u_2=x$.
  4. Answer. $y=c_1\cos x+c_2\sin x+\cos x\ln|\cos x|+x\sin x$. Undetermined coefficients could not do this, because $\sec x$ has infinitely many independent derivatives.
Common mistakes
  • Swapping the formulas: the minus sign and $y_2$ go with $u_1'$.
  • Not dividing by the leading coefficient.
  • Forgetting to multiply $u_1$ by $y_1$ and $u_2$ by $y_2$ at the end.

Practice set · 12 problems

12 more problems on this section. Each one has an answer and a full step-by-step solution that explains why each step is taken. The numbers are fixed, so you can come back to the same problem.

Chapter 3 · Section 3.6 · on Test 2

Cauchy–Euler equations

Most linear equations with variable coefficients cannot be solved in closed form. The Cauchy–Euler equation is the big exception. Its solutions are always built from powers of $x$, logarithms, sines and cosines, and the method looks almost exactly like the one for constant coefficients in Section 3.3.

Before any formulas, it helps to see why this kind of equation is special and what its solutions look like.

The pattern that makes it work

In Section 3.3 we solved $ay''+by'+cy=0$ by guessing $y=e^{mx}$. That guess worked for one reason: every derivative of $e^{mx}$ is a constant times $e^{mx}$. After substituting, every term contained the same factor $e^{mx}$, we cancelled it, and a plain quadratic in $m$ was left.

A Cauchy–Euler equation is built so that the same thing happens with powers of $x$. Differentiating $x^m$ once lowers the power by one, $\dfrac{d}{dx}x^m=mx^{m-1}$. Multiplying by $x$ raises it back: $x\cdot mx^{m-1}=mx^m$. Differentiating twice lowers the power by two, and the factor $x^2$ puts both back: $x^2\cdot m(m-1)x^{m-2}=m(m-1)x^m$. So in $ax^2y''+bxy'+cy$ every term turns into a number times $x^m$. That is the whole trick, and it only works because the power of $x$ in front of each derivative equals the order of the derivative.

A quick test on any equation: in each term, add the power of $x$ in the coefficient to minus the order of the derivative. For $x^2y''$ that is $2-2=0$, for $xy'$ it is $1-1=0$, and for $y$ it is $0-0=0$. If every term gives the same number, powers of $x$ will work. In $x^2y''+y'+y=0$ the middle term gives $0-1=-1$, so that equation is not Cauchy–Euler and $y=x^m$ fails.

A dictionary between the two methods

Constant coefficients (3.3)Cauchy–Euler (3.6)
Equation$ay''+by'+cy=0$$ax^2y''+bxy'+cy=0$
Guess$y=e^{mx}$$y=x^m$
Auxiliary equation$am^2+bm+c=0$$am(m-1)+bm+c=0$
Distinct real roots$c_1e^{m_1x}+c_2e^{m_2x}$$c_1x^{m_1}+c_2x^{m_2}$
Repeated root$c_1e^{m_1x}+c_2\,x\,e^{m_1x}$$c_1x^{m_1}+c_2x^{m_1}\ln x$
Complex roots $\alpha\pm i\beta$$e^{\alpha x}(c_1\cos\beta x+c_2\sin\beta x)$$x^{\alpha}\big(c_1\cos(\beta\ln x)+c_2\sin(\beta\ln x)\big)$
Intervalall $x$$x>0$

Read the table column by column and you will notice the rule: replace $x$ by $\ln x$ inside the constant-coefficient answers.
