Since $e^{m\ln x}=x^m$, the exponential $e^{mx}$ becomes $x^m$, the extra factor $x$ becomes $\ln x$, and $\cos\beta x$ becomes $\cos(\beta\ln x)$. This is not a coincidence. The substitution $x=e^t$ (explained later in this section) turns a Cauchy–Euler equation into a constant-coefficient one in the variable $t=\ln x$.

What the solutions look like

  • $x^m$ with $m>0$ starts at $0$ when $x=0$ and grows. $x^m$ with $m<0$ blows up as $x\to0^+$ and decays as $x\to\infty$. That blow-up at $x=0$ is why we keep to $x>0$.
  • $\ln x$ is $0$ at $x=1$, negative for $0<x<1$, and grows very slowly. Multiplying by it changes $x^m$ only gently.
  • $\cos(\beta\ln x)$ oscillates, but the oscillations stretch out as $x$ grows. Its zeros are where $\beta\ln x=\dfrac{\pi}{2}+k\pi$, that is $x=e^{(\pi/2+k\pi)/\beta}$. Consecutive zeros are a fixed ratio apart, not a fixed distance apart. Near $x=0$ it oscillates infinitely often.

Why $x=1$ is the natural place for initial conditions

The point $x=0$ is not allowed, because the leading coefficient $ax^2$ vanishes there. The point $x=1$ is the friendliest choice: $1^m=1$ for every $m$, and $\ln1=0$, so $\cos(\beta\ln1)=1$ and $\sin(\beta\ln1)=0$. Almost every textbook and test problem puts its initial conditions at $x=1$ for this reason.

The plan for every Cauchy–Euler problem

  1. Check the form: $x^2$ with $y''$, $x$ with $y'$, constant with $y$.
  2. Substitute $y=x^m$ (or use $am(m-1)+bm+c=0$) and simplify to a quadratic in $m$.
  3. Find the roots and decide which of the three cases you are in.
  4. Write $y_c$ from the table.
  5. If the equation has a right side $g(x)$, find $y_p$: guess $Ax^k$ when $g$ is a power of $x$, otherwise divide by $ax^2$ and use variation of parameters.
  6. Apply the initial or boundary conditions last.

What makes an equation Cauchy–Euler

A linear differential equation of the form

$$a_nx^n\frac{d^ny}{dx^n}+a_{n-1}x^{n-1}\frac{d^{n-1}y}{dx^{n-1}}+\cdots+a_1x\frac{dy}{dx}+a_0y=g(x),$$

where $a_n,a_{n-1},\dots,a_0$ are constants, is a Cauchy–Euler equation. The thing to look for is that the power of $x$ in each coefficient matches the order of the derivative it multiplies:

  • $x^2$ multiplies $y''$,
  • $x^1$ multiplies $y'$,
  • $x^0=1$ multiplies $y$.

We will work mostly with the second-order homogeneous equation

$$ax^2y''+bxy'+cy=0. \tag{1}$$

Higher-order equations are handled the same way. The nonhomogeneous equation $ax^2y''+bxy'+cy=g(x)$ is solved by variation of parameters once we know the complementary function $y_c$ of (1).

Why we work on $x>0$

The leading coefficient $ax^2$ is zero at $x=0$. The existence and uniqueness theorem (Theorem 3.1.1) needs the leading coefficient to be nonzero, so we look for solutions on the interval $(0,\infty)$. That is also why $\ln x$ appears freely in the answers below.

The method: try $y=x^m$

For constant coefficients we tried $y=e^{mx}$, because differentiating an exponential gives back the same exponential. Here we try $y=x^m$, because each derivative lowers the power by one, and the extra power of $x$ in front puts it right back:

$$\begin{aligned}x^k\frac{d^ky}{dx^k}&=x^k\,m(m-1)(m-2)\cdots(m-k+1)\,x^{m-k}\\&=m(m-1)\cdots(m-k+1)\,x^m.\end{aligned}$$

So every term becomes a number times $x^m$. Substituting $y=x^m$, $y'=mx^{m-1}$, $y''=m(m-1)x^{m-2}$ into (1):

$$ax^2\cdot m(m-1)x^{m-2}+bx\cdot mx^{m-1}+c\,x^m=x^m\big(am(m-1)+bm+c\big).$$

Since $x^m\neq0$ for $x>0$, $y=x^m$ is a solution exactly when $m$ solves the auxiliary equation

Auxiliary equation $$am(m-1)+bm+c=0, \tag{2}$$

which is the same as $am^2+(b-a)m+c=0$.

The most common mistake

The coefficient $a$ multiplies $m(m-1)$, not $m^2$. Writing $am^2+bm+c=0$ (the Section 3.3 form) gives the wrong roots. Until it is automatic, substitute $y=x^m$ and differentiate each time instead of memorizing (2).

A quadratic has three kinds of roots, so there are three cases.

Case I: distinct real roots

If $m_1\neq m_2$ are real, then $y_1=x^{m_1}$ and $y_2=x^{m_2}$ are not multiples of each other, so they form a fundamental set and

Distinct real roots$$y=c_1x^{m_1}+c_2x^{m_2}. \tag{3}$$
Example 1Distinct roots

Solve $x^2y''-2xy'-4y=0$.

  1. Assume a power. Let $y=x^m$, so $y'=mx^{m-1}$ and $y''=m(m-1)x^{m-2}$. We differentiate rather than quote (2) so the $m(m-1)$ appears naturally.
  2. Substitute. $$\begin{aligned}&x^2\cdot m(m-1)x^{m-2}-2x\cdot mx^{m-1}-4x^m\\&\quad=x^m\big(m(m-1)-2m-4\big)=x^m(m^2-3m-4).\end{aligned}$$
  3. Set the bracket to zero. $x^m$ is never zero for $x>0$, so we need $m^2-3m-4=0$.
  4. Factor. $(m+1)(m-4)=0$, so $m_1=-1$ and $m_2=4$. They are real and different, so this is Case I.
  5. Write the solution with (3). $y=c_1x^{-1}+c_2x^{4}$, valid on $(0,\infty)$.

Case II: repeated real root

If the roots are equal, $m_1=m_2$, the substitution only gives one solution, $y_1=x^{m_1}$. The discriminant is zero, so by the quadratic formula $m_1=-\dfrac{b-a}{2a}$. A second solution comes from reduction of order (Section 3.2), and it turns out to be $x^{m_1}\ln x$. The logarithm plays exactly the role that the extra factor $x$ played in $xe^{mx}$ for constant coefficients.

Repeated root$$y=c_1x^{m_1}+c_2x^{m_1}\ln x. \tag{4}$$
Optional: where $\ln x$ comes from

Divide (1) by $ax^2$ to get the standard form $y''+\dfrac{b}{ax}y'+\dfrac{c}{ax^2}y=0$, so $P(x)=\dfrac{b}{ax}$ and $\displaystyle\int P\,dx=\frac{b}{a}\ln x$. The reduction-of-order formula gives

$$y_2=x^{m_1}\int\frac{e^{-(b/a)\ln x}}{x^{2m_1}}\,dx=x^{m_1}\int x^{-b/a}\,x^{-2m_1}\,dx.$$

Because $m_1=\dfrac{a-b}{2a}$, the exponent is $-\dfrac{b}{a}-2m_1=-\dfrac{b}{a}-\dfrac{a-b}{a}=-1$. So the integrand is $\dfrac1x$, the integral is $\ln x$, and $y_2=x^{m_1}\ln x$.

Example 2Repeated roots

Solve $4x^2y''+8xy'+y=0$.

  1. Substitute $y=x^m$. $4x^2\,m(m-1)x^{m-2}+8x\,mx^{m-1}+x^m=x^m\big(4m(m-1)+8m+1\big)$.
  2. Simplify the bracket. $4m^2-4m+8m+1=4m^2+4m+1$.
  3. Solve. $4m^2+4m+1=(2m+1)^2=0$, so $m=-\dfrac12$ is a repeated root. This is Case II.
  4. Use (4). $y=c_1x^{-1/2}+c_2x^{-1/2}\ln x$.

For higher-order equations, a root $m_1$ of multiplicity $k$ gives the $k$ solutions $x^{m_1},\;x^{m_1}\ln x,\;x^{m_1}(\ln x)^2,\;\dots,\;x^{m_1}(\ln x)^{k-1}$.

Case III: complex conjugate roots

If the roots are $m=\alpha\pm i\beta$ with $\beta>0$, then $x^{\alpha+i\beta}$ and $x^{\alpha-i\beta}$ are solutions, but they are complex. We want real solutions. The trick is to write $x$ as $e^{\ln x}$:

$$x^{i\beta}=\left(e^{\ln x}\right)^{i\beta}=e^{i\beta\ln x}=\cos(\beta\ln x)+i\sin(\beta\ln x)$$

by Euler’s formula. Adding and subtracting this and its conjugate gives the real functions $x^\alpha\cos(\beta\ln x)$ and $x^\alpha\sin(\beta\ln x)$. Their Wronskian is $\beta x^{2\alpha-1}\neq0$ on $(0,\infty)$, so they form a fundamental set.

Complex roots $\alpha\pm i\beta$$$y=x^{\alpha}\big[c_1\cos(\beta\ln x)+c_2\sin(\beta\ln x)\big]. \tag{5}$$

Compare with Section 3.3, where complex roots gave $e^{\alpha x}(c_1\cos\beta x+c_2\sin\beta x)$. Here $e^{\alpha x}$ becomes $x^\alpha$, and $\beta x$ becomes $\beta\ln x$.

Example 3An initial-value problem

Solve $4x^2y''+17y=0$, $y(1)=-1$, $y'(1)=-\dfrac12$.

  1. Substitute $y=x^m$. There is no $y'$ term, but the method is the same: $4x^2\,m(m-1)x^{m-2}+17x^m=x^m(4m^2-4m+17)$.
  2. Solve the auxiliary equation. $4m^2-4m+17=0$ gives $m=\dfrac{4\pm\sqrt{16-272}}{8}=\dfrac{4\pm16i}{8}=\dfrac12\pm2i$.
  3. Read off $\alpha$ and $\beta$. $\alpha=\dfrac12$, $\beta=2$. By (5), $y=x^{1/2}\big[c_1\cos(2\ln x)+c_2\sin(2\ln x)\big]$.
  4. First condition. At $x=1$, $\ln1=0$, so $\cos0=1$ and $\sin0=0$. Then $y(1)=c_1=-1$.
  5. Differentiate. By the product and chain rules, $$\begin{aligned}y'&=\dfrac12x^{-1/2}\big[c_1\cos(2\ln x)+c_2\sin(2\ln x)\big]\\&\quad+x^{1/2}\Big[-\frac{2c_1}{x}\sin(2\ln x)+\frac{2c_2}{x}\cos(2\ln x)\Big].\end{aligned}$$
  6. Second condition. At $x=1$: $y'(1)=\dfrac12c_1+2c_2=-\dfrac12$. With $c_1=-1$ this gives $2c_2=0$, so $c_2=0$.
  7. Answer. $y=-x^{1/2}\cos(2\ln x)$. It oscillates, and the oscillations grow without bound as $x\to\infty$ because of the factor $x^{1/2}$.
Example 4Third-order equation

Solve $x^3y'''+5x^2y''+7xy'+8y=0$.

  1. Derivatives of $x^m$. $y'=mx^{m-1}$, $y''=m(m-1)x^{m-2}$, $y'''=m(m-1)(m-2)x^{m-3}$.
  2. Substitute. Each term becomes a multiple of $x^m$: $x^m\big[m(m-1)(m-2)+5m(m-1)+7m+8\big]$.
  3. Expand. $m^3-3m^2+2m+5m^2-5m+7m+8=m^3+2m^2+4m+8$.
  4. Factor by grouping. $m^2(m+2)+4(m+2)=(m+2)(m^2+4)$, so $m_1=-2$, $m_{2,3}=\pm2i$.
  5. Combine the cases. The real root gives $x^{-2}$. The pair $0\pm2i$ has $\alpha=0$, $\beta=2$ and gives $\cos(2\ln x)$, $\sin(2\ln x)$. So $y=c_1x^{-2}+c_2\cos(2\ln x)+c_3\sin(2\ln x)$.

Nonhomogeneous equations

Undetermined coefficients does not work for variable-coefficient equations in general, because there is no reason for $y_p$ to look like $g(x)$. Use variation of parameters (Section 3.5). The formulas $u_1'=-\dfrac{y_2f}{W}$, $u_2'=\dfrac{y_1f}{W}$ were derived for the standard form $y''+P(x)y'+Q(x)y=f(x)$, so you must divide by $ax^2$ first.

Divide first

For $ax^2y''+bxy'+cy=g(x)$ the right side to use is $f(x)=\dfrac{g(x)}{ax^2}$, not $g(x)$. Forgetting this is the second most common mistake in this section.

Example 5Variation of parameters

Solve $x^2y''-3xy'+3y=2x^4e^x$.

  1. Homogeneous part. With $y=x^m$: $m(m-1)-3m+3=m^2-4m+3=(m-1)(m-3)=0$. So $y_c=c_1x+c_2x^3$, with $y_1=x$, $y_2=x^3$.
  2. Standard form. Divide by $x^2$: $y''-\dfrac3xy'+\dfrac3{x^2}y=2x^2e^x$. So $f(x)=2x^2e^x$.
  3. Wronskian. $W=\begin{vmatrix}x&x^3\\1&3x^2\end{vmatrix}=3x^3-x^3=2x^3$.
  4. Find $u_1'$. $u_1'=-\dfrac{y_2f}{W}=-\dfrac{x^3\cdot2x^2e^x}{2x^3}=-x^2e^x$.
  5. Find $u_2'$. $u_2'=\dfrac{y_1f}{W}=\dfrac{x\cdot2x^2e^x}{2x^3}=e^x$.
  6. Integrate. $u_2=e^x$ directly. For $u_1$, integrate by parts twice: $\displaystyle\int x^2e^x\,dx=(x^2-2x+2)e^x$, so $u_1=-(x^2-2x+2)e^x$.
  7. Build $y_p=u_1y_1+u_2y_2$. $-(x^2-2x+2)e^x\cdot x+e^x\cdot x^3=(-x^3+2x^2-2x+x^3)e^x=2x^2e^x-2xe^x$.
  8. General solution. $y=c_1x+c_2x^3+2x^2e^x-2xe^x$.

Reduction to constant coefficients

The likeness between Cauchy–Euler solutions and constant-coefficient solutions is not a coincidence. The substitution $x=e^t$ (so $t=\ln x$) turns any Cauchy–Euler equation into one with constant coefficients. By the chain rule,

$$\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac1x\frac{dy}{dt},\qquad\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}=\frac1{x^2}\left(\frac{d^2y}{dt^2}-\frac{dy}{dt}\right),$$

so $x^2y''+bxy'+cy$ becomes $\dfrac{d^2y}{dt^2}+(b-1)\dfrac{dy}{dt}+cy$. Solve in $t$ with Section 3.3 or 3.4 methods, then put $t=\ln x$ back. This is especially handy when $g(x)$ involves $\ln x$, because then undetermined coefficients works in the variable $t$.

Example 6Changing to constant coefficients

Solve $x^2y''-xy'+y=\ln x$.

  1. Substitute $t=\ln x$. Using the formulas above with $b=-1$, $c=1$: $\dfrac{d^2y}{dt^2}-2\dfrac{dy}{dt}+y=t$.
  2. Homogeneous part. $m^2-2m+1=(m-1)^2=0$, so $y_c=c_1e^t+c_2te^t$.
  3. Particular part. The right side is $t$, a first-degree polynomial, so try $y_p=A+Bt$. Then $y_p'=B$, $y_p''=0$ and $0-2B+A+Bt=t$.
  4. Match coefficients. $t$: $B=1$. Constant: $A-2B=0$, so $A=2$. Thus $y_p=2+t$.
  5. Return to $x$. $e^t=x$ and $t=\ln x$: $y=c_1x+c_2x\ln x+2+\ln x$.
Optional: the shifted equation and solutions for $x<0$

The equation $a(x-x_0)^2y''+b(x-x_0)y'+cy=0$ is solved the same way with $y=(x-x_0)^m$. To solve on $x<0$, substitute $t=-x$; the equation keeps its form in $t$ and you can replace $x^m$ by $|x|^m$ in the answers.

Summary for $ax^2y''+bxy'+cy=0$
  • Solve $am(m-1)+bm+c=0$.
  • Distinct real $m_1,m_2$: $y=c_1x^{m_1}+c_2x^{m_2}$.
  • Repeated $m_1$: $y=c_1x^{m_1}+c_2x^{m_1}\ln x$.
  • Complex $\alpha\pm i\beta$: $y=x^\alpha\big[c_1\cos(\beta\ln x)+c_2\sin(\beta\ln x)\big]$.
  • Nonhomogeneous: divide by $ax^2$, then variation of parameters.

Particular solutions by guessing a power

Variation of parameters always works, but when the right side is a power of $x$ there is a much faster way. It rests on one identity. If $P(m)=am(m-1)+bm+c$ is the auxiliary polynomial, then substituting $y=x^k$ gives

$$ax^2(x^k)''+bx(x^k)'+c\,x^k=P(k)\,x^k.$$

A power of $x$ goes in, and the same power comes out, multiplied by the number $P(k)$. So if the right side is $g(x)=Cx^k$ and $P(k)\neq0$, the guess $y_p=Ax^k$ gives $P(k)A=C$, and

Guessing a power$$ax^2y''+bxy'+cy=Cx^k\quad\Longrightarrow\quad y_p=\frac{C}{P(k)}\,x^k\qquad\text{when }P(k)\neq0.$$

For a sum of powers, treat each power on its own and add the results (superposition).

When $P(k)=0$, the power $x^k$ is already a solution of the homogeneous equation, so $Ax^k$ produces $0$ and can never match $Cx^k$. This is the same resonance problem as in Section 3.4, where $e^{rx}$ forcing at a root needed an extra factor $x$. In the Cauchy–Euler world the extra factor is $\ln x$:

Resonant power

If $k$ is a simple root of $P$, try $y_p=Ax^k\ln x$. Substituting gives $P'(k)\,A\,x^k$, so $A=\dfrac{C}{P'(k)}$, where for $a=1$, $P'(k)=2k+b-1$.

If $k$ is a repeated root, both $x^k$ and $x^k\ln x$ are homogeneous solutions. Try $y_p=Ax^k(\ln x)^2$; substituting gives $2aAx^k$, so $A=\dfrac{C}{2a}$.

Why the logarithm appears

Think of $P(m)x^m$ as a function of $m$. Differentiate the identity $L[x^m]=P(m)x^m$ with respect to $m$. Since $\dfrac{\partial}{\partial m}x^m=x^m\ln x$, we get $L[x^m\ln x]=P'(m)x^m+P(m)x^m\ln x$. At a root, $P(k)=0$, so $L[x^k\ln x]=P'(k)x^k$, which is exactly a multiple of the right side. Differentiating once more explains the $(\ln x)^2$ for a repeated root. This is the same reason $xe^{rx}$ appears in Section 3.4.

Example 7A repeated root with initial conditions

Solve $x^2y''+3xy'+y=0$, $y(1)=2$, $y'(1)=-1$.

  1. Identify the type. $x^2$ goes with $y''$, $x$ with $y'$, and a constant with $y$, so this is Cauchy–Euler with $a=1$, $b=3$, $c=1$. We look for solutions on $x>0$.
  2. Auxiliary equation. $m(m-1)+3m+1=m^2+2m+1=(m+1)^2=0$. The only root is $m=-1$, and it is repeated. The discriminant is zero, so this is Case II.
  3. General solution. A repeated root gives $x^{-1}$ and $x^{-1}\ln x$: $$y=c_1x^{-1}+c_2x^{-1}\ln x=\frac{c_1+c_2\ln x}{x}.$$
  4. Use $y(1)=2$. At $x=1$, $\ln1=0$ and $\dfrac11=1$, so $y(1)=c_1$. Hence $c_1=2$.
  5. Differentiate. Use the quotient rule on $\dfrac{c_1+c_2\ln x}{x}$: the numerator's derivative is $\dfrac{c_2}{x}$, so $$y'=\frac{\dfrac{c_2}{x}\cdot x-(c_1+c_2\ln x)\cdot1}{x^2}=\frac{c_2-c_1-c_2\ln x}{x^2}.$$
  6. Use $y'(1)=-1$. $y'(1)=c_2-c_1=-1$. With $c_1=2$, $c_2=1$.
  7. Answer. $$y=\frac{2+\ln x}{x}.$$ As a check, $y(1)=2$ and $y'(1)=\dfrac{1-2-0}{1}=-1$. As $x\to\infty$ the solution tends to $0$, because $\ln x$ grows more slowly than $x$.
Example 8Guessing a power, with and without resonance

Find a particular solution of (a) $x^2y''-2xy'+2y=6x^3$ and (b) $x^2y''-2xy'+2y=4x^2$.

  1. The homogeneous equation first. $P(m)=m(m-1)-2m+2=m^2-3m+2=(m-1)(m-2)$. The roots are $1$ and $2$, so $y_c=c_1x+c_2x^2$. We need these roots to know whether a guess is resonant.
  2. (a) Is $x^3$ resonant? $P(3)=9-9+2=2\neq0$. The power $3$ is not a root, so the plain guess $y_p=Ax^3$ works.
  3. (a) Substitute. $y_p'=3Ax^2$ and $y_p''=6Ax$. Then $$x^2(6Ax)-2x(3Ax^2)+2Ax^3=(6-6+2)Ax^3=2Ax^3.$$ The bracket is exactly $P(3)=2$, as the identity promised.
  4. (a) Match. $2A=6$, so $A=3$ and $y_p=3x^3$.
  5. (b) Is $x^2$ resonant? $P(2)=0$: $x^2$ is part of $y_c$. Substituting $Ax^2$ would give $0$, so we must use $y_p=Ax^2\ln x$.
  6. (b) Derivatives. By the product rule, $y_p'=A(2x\ln x+x)$ and $y_p''=A(2\ln x+2+1)=A(2\ln x+3)$.
  7. (b) Substitute and collect. $$\begin{aligned}x^2y_p''&=A(2x^2\ln x+3x^2)\\-2xy_p'&=A(-4x^2\ln x-2x^2)\\2y_p&=A(2x^2\ln x).\end{aligned}$$ The $\ln x$ terms add to $(2-4+2)Ax^2\ln x=0$. They must cancel, because $P(2)=0$. What is left is $(3-2)Ax^2=Ax^2$.
  8. (b) Match. $A=4$, so $y_p=4x^2\ln x$. The shortcut agrees: $P'(k)=2k-3$ gives $P'(2)=1$ and $A=\dfrac{4}{1}=4$.
  9. General solutions. (a) $y=c_1x+c_2x^2+3x^3$. (b) $y=c_1x+c_2x^2+4x^2\ln x$.
Example 9Variation of parameters with complex roots

Solve $x^2y''+xy'+y=\sec(\ln x)$ on $1\le x<e^{\pi/2}$.

  1. Why not guess? The right side is not a power of $x$, and $\sec$ has no finite family of derivatives. Variation of parameters is the method.
  2. Homogeneous solutions. $m(m-1)+m+1=m^2+1=0$, so $m=\pm i$: $\alpha=0$, $\beta=1$. Then $y_1=\cos(\ln x)$ and $y_2=\sin(\ln x)$.
  3. Standard form. Divide the whole equation by $x^2$. The right side becomes $$f(x)=\frac{\sec(\ln x)}{x^2}.$$ Using $\sec(\ln x)$ itself here would be wrong.
  4. Wronskian. $y_1'=-\dfrac{\sin(\ln x)}{x}$ and $y_2'=\dfrac{\cos(\ln x)}{x}$. So $$W=\cos(\ln x)\cdot\frac{\cos(\ln x)}{x}+\sin(\ln x)\cdot\frac{\sin(\ln x)}{x}=\frac1x.$$ This matches the general formula $W=\beta x^{2\alpha-1}$.
  5. $u_1'$. $$u_1'=-\frac{y_2f}{W}=-\sin(\ln x)\cdot\frac{\sec(\ln x)}{x^2}\cdot x=-\frac{\tan(\ln x)}{x}.$$
  6. $u_2'$. $$u_2'=\frac{y_1f}{W}=\cos(\ln x)\cdot\frac{\sec(\ln x)}{x^2}\cdot x=\frac{1}{x}.$$
  7. Integrate. Substitute $\theta=\ln x$, $d\theta=\dfrac{dx}{x}$. Then $u_1=-\displaystyle\int\tan\theta\,d\theta=\ln|\cos\theta|=\ln|\cos(\ln x)|$, and $u_2=\ln x$.
  8. Assemble. $$y_p=u_1y_1+u_2y_2=\cos(\ln x)\ln|\cos(\ln x)|+\ln x\,\sin(\ln x).$$
  9. General solution and interval. $y=c_1\cos(\ln x)+c_2\sin(\ln x)+\cos(\ln x)\ln|\cos(\ln x)|+\ln x\,\sin(\ln x)$. The logarithm of $\cos(\ln x)$ needs $\cos(\ln x)\neq0$, which holds for $0\le\ln x<\dfrac{\pi}{2}$. That is why the interval stops at $e^{\pi/2}\approx4.81$.
Example 10Superposition of two powers

Find a particular solution of $x^2y''+xy'-4y=5x^3-8$.

  1. Auxiliary polynomial. $P(m)=m(m-1)+m-4=m^2-4$. Its roots are $\pm2$.
  2. Split the right side. $5x^3-8=5x^3+(-8)x^0$. The powers are $3$ and $0$. Neither is a root, since $P(3)=5$ and $P(0)=-4$.
  3. First power. $Ax^3$ produces $P(3)Ax^3=5Ax^3$. Matching $5x^3$ gives $A=1$.
  4. Second power. A constant $B=Bx^0$ produces $P(0)B=-4B$. Matching $-8$ gives $B=2$. (Check directly: $y=2$ has $y'=y''=0$, so the left side is $-4\cdot2=-8$.)
  5. Add. $y_p=x^3+2$, and $y=c_1x^2+c_2x^{-2}+x^3+2$.

Test-day checklist

Before you write anything
  • Confirm the powers of $x$ match the orders of the derivatives. If not, the equation is not Cauchy–Euler.
  • Write the auxiliary equation with $m(m-1)$, then expand. Do not write $am^2+bm+c$.
  • Decide the case from the roots: two real, one repeated, or complex.
Mistakes that cost marks
  • $am^2+bm+c=0$ instead of $am(m-1)+bm+c=0$. This gives the wrong roots and everything after is wrong.
  • Exponentials in the answer. $e^{mx}$ is for constant coefficients. Cauchy–Euler answers use $x^m$.
  • $\cos\beta x$ instead of $\cos(\beta\ln x)$. The argument is always $\beta\ln x$.
  • Forgetting the chain rule. $\dfrac{d}{dx}\cos(\beta\ln x)=-\dfrac{\beta}{x}\sin(\beta\ln x)$. The factor $\dfrac1x$ is easy to drop.
  • Not dividing by $ax^2$ before variation of parameters. The formulas for $u_1'$ and $u_2'$ use $f(x)=\dfrac{g(x)}{ax^2}$.
  • Guessing $Ax^k$ when $k$ is a root. Check $P(k)$ first. If it is zero, multiply the guess by $\ln x$.
  • Using $x=0$ for initial conditions. Solutions live on $x>0$, and $x=1$ is the usual point.

Practice set · 36 problems

36 more problems on this section, weighted toward the kinds that show up on Test 2. Each one has an answer and a full step-by-step solution that explains why each step is taken. The numbers are fixed, so you can come back to the same problem.

Chapter 3 · Section 3.8 · on Test 2

Linear models: initial-value problems

A mass on a spring and an LRC circuit obey the same second-order linear equation. This section builds that equation from physics and then reads its three kinds of solutions as three kinds of motion: free, damped and driven.

Almost every problem in this section comes down to one equation,

$$m\frac{d^2x}{dt^2}+\beta\frac{dx}{dt}+kx=f(t),$$

and to reading its solution as a physical motion. Each term is a force acting on the mass.

  • $m\,x''$ is mass times acceleration, the left side of Newton's law $F=ma$, moved over to join the others.
  • $kx$ is the spring's restoring force. It always points back toward equilibrium: when $x>0$ (below) it pulls up, and when $x<0$ it pushes down.
  • $\beta x'$ is damping, a friction-like force from air or a dashpot. It opposes the velocity, so it is proportional to $x'$ with a positive constant $\beta$.
  • $f(t)$ is any outside driving force, such as a motor shaking the support.

Gravity does not appear, because measuring $x$ from the equilibrium position cancels it, as shown below. That is why the problem always tells you how far the weight stretches the spring: you use the stretch to find $k$, and then you never think about gravity again.

Turning a word problem into an IVP

The problem saysYou write
“A weight of $W$ lb”$m=\dfrac{W}{32}$ slug
“A mass of $m$ kg”use $m$ directly; weight is $9.8m$ N
“stretches the spring $s$”$k=\dfrac{W}{s}=\dfrac{mg}{s}$ (convert inches to feet)
“damping force numerically equal to $c$ times the instantaneous velocity”$\beta=c$
“released from rest”$x'(0)=0$
“released from equilibrium”$x(0)=0$
“$d$ units below equilibrium”$x(0)=d$
“$d$ units above equilibrium”$x(0)=-d$
“with a downward velocity $v$”$x'(0)=v$
“with an upward velocity $v$”$x'(0)=-v$
“driven by an external force $f(t)$”put $f(t)$ on the right side

Divide by $m$ to read the behaviour

Dividing by $m$ gives Zill's standard form

$$x''+2\lambda x'+\omega^2x=\frac{f(t)}{m},\qquad 2\lambda=\frac{\beta}{m},\quad\omega^2=\frac{k}{m}.$$

The auxiliary equation $r^2+2\lambda r+\omega^2=0$ has roots $r=-\lambda\pm\sqrt{\lambda^2-\omega^2}$. The sign of $\lambda^2-\omega^2$ is a contest between damping ($\lambda$) and the spring ($\omega$):

  • $\lambda^2>\omega^2$, overdamped. Two negative real roots. The damping wins: the mass creeps back to equilibrium and crosses it at most once.
  • $\lambda^2=\omega^2$, critically damped. One repeated root $-\lambda$. This is the borderline; the slightest decrease in damping would allow oscillation. It also crosses equilibrium at most once.
  • $\lambda^2<\omega^2$, underdamped. Complex roots $-\lambda\pm i\sqrt{\omega^2-\lambda^2}$. The spring wins: the mass oscillates, and the oscillations shrink like $e^{-\lambda t}$.

With no damping ($\lambda=0$) the motion is a pure sine wave forever. With a forcing term, the solution is $x=x_c+x_p$. When there is damping, $x_c$ dies out (the transient) and $x_p$ remains (the steady state). The steady state oscillates at the frequency of the forcing, not the natural frequency of the spring.

Resonance in one sentence

If an undamped spring with natural frequency $\omega$ is pushed by $F_0\sin\gamma t$ with $\gamma=\omega$, every push arrives at the right moment to add energy, and the amplitude grows without bound, like $t\cos\omega t$. Mathematically, the forcing duplicates a term of $x_c$, and undetermined coefficients must multiply the guess by $t$.

Spring/mass systems: free undamped motion

Hooke’s law. A spring stretched (or compressed) by $s$ pushes back with a force proportional to $s$: $F=ks$. The constant $k$ is the spring constant. For example, if a 10-lb weight stretches a spring $\dfrac12$ ft, then $10=k\cdot\dfrac12$, so $k=20$ lb/ft.

Equilibrium. Hang a mass $m$ on the spring. It stretches by $s$ and comes to rest where the spring force balances gravity:

$$mg=ks\qquad\text{or}\qquad mg-ks=0.$$

Newton’s second law. Now pull the mass $x$ units below equilibrium and let it go. The net force is gravity minus the spring force on the total stretch $s+x$:

$$m\frac{d^2x}{dt^2}=mg-k(s+x)=\underbrace{mg-ks}_{0}-kx=-kx.$$

The equilibrium balance cancels gravity completely. What is left is

Free undamped motion (simple harmonic motion) $$\frac{d^2x}{dt^2}+\omega^2x=0,\qquad\omega^2=\frac km. \tag{1}$$

Its general solution is $x(t)=c_1\cos\omega t+c_2\sin\omega t$.

Conventions and units
  • $x$ is measured from the equilibrium position, and down is positive. A mass released “2 ft above equilibrium” has $x(0)=-2$. An “upward velocity” is a negative $x'(0)$.
  • Weight $W$ and mass are related by $W=mg$. In the US system $g=32$ ft/s², so a 2-lb weight has mass $m=\dfrac{2}{32}=\dfrac1{16}$ slug. In SI, $g=9.8$ m/s² and masses are given in kg.
  • Convert inches to feet before using Hooke’s law: 6 in $=\dfrac12$ ft.

Reading the solution. The period is $T=\dfrac{2\pi}{\omega}$, the time for one full cycle. The frequency is $f=\dfrac1T=\dfrac{\omega}{2\pi}$, the number of cycles per unit time. The circular frequency is $\omega$ itself, measured in radians per second.

Example 1Free undamped motion

A mass weighing 2 pounds stretches a spring 6 inches. At $t=0$ the mass is released from a point 8 inches below the equilibrium position with an upward velocity of $\dfrac43$ ft/s. Find the equation of motion.

  1. Convert units. We work in feet: 6 in $=\dfrac12$ ft and 8 in $=\dfrac23$ ft.
  2. Find the mass. $m=\dfrac Wg=\dfrac{2}{32}=\dfrac1{16}$ slug.
  3. Find $k$ from Hooke’s law. The 2-lb weight stretches the spring $\dfrac12$ ft: $2=k\cdot\dfrac12$, so $k=4$ lb/ft.
  4. Write the equation. $\dfrac1{16}x''=-4x$, so $x''+64x=0$ and $\omega=8$.
  5. Initial conditions. Below equilibrium is positive: $x(0)=\dfrac23$. Upward velocity is negative: $x'(0)=-\dfrac43$.
  6. General solution. $x=c_1\cos8t+c_2\sin8t$. At $t=0$: $x(0)=c_1=\dfrac23$.
  7. Use the velocity. $x'=-8c_1\sin8t+8c_2\cos8t$, so $x'(0)=8c_2=-\dfrac43$ and $c_2=-\dfrac16$.
  8. Answer. $x(t)=\dfrac23\cos8t-\dfrac16\sin8t$. The period is $\dfrac{2\pi}{8}=\dfrac\pi4$ s.

Amplitude: the alternative form

A sum $c_1\cos\omega t+c_2\sin\omega t$ is always a single shifted sine wave:

Amplitude–phase form $$x(t)=A\sin(\omega t+\phi),\qquad A=\sqrt{c_1^2+c_2^2},$$ $$\sin\phi=\frac{c_1}{A},\qquad\cos\phi=\frac{c_2}{A},\qquad\tan\phi=\frac{c_1}{c_2}.$$

This comes from the identity $A\sin(\omega t+\phi)=A\sin\phi\cos\omega t+A\cos\phi\sin\omega t$ and matching coefficients. $A$ is the amplitude (largest displacement) and $\phi$ is the phase angle.

Pick the right quadrant

$\tan^{-1}$ only returns angles in $\left(-\dfrac\pi2,\dfrac\pi2\right)$. Look at the signs of $\sin\phi=\dfrac{c_1}{A}$ and $\cos\phi=\dfrac{c_2}{A}$ to decide which quadrant $\phi$ is in, and add $\pi$ if needed.

Example 2Example 1 in amplitude form

Write $x(t)=\dfrac23\cos8t-\dfrac16\sin8t$ as $A\sin(8t+\phi)$.

  1. Amplitude. $A=\sqrt{\left(\dfrac23\right)^2+\left(-\dfrac16\right)^2}=\sqrt{\dfrac{16}{36}+\dfrac1{36}}=\dfrac{\sqrt{17}}{6}\approx0.69$ ft.
  2. Signs. $\sin\phi=\dfrac{c_1}{A}>0$ and $\cos\phi=\dfrac{c_2}{A}<0$, so $\phi$ is in the second quadrant.
  3. Angle. $\tan\phi=\dfrac{2/3}{-1/6}=-4$. A calculator gives $\tan^{-1}(-4)=-1.326$, which is in the fourth quadrant, so add $\pi$: $\phi=\pi-1.326=1.816$ rad.
  4. Answer. $x(t)=\dfrac{\sqrt{17}}6\sin(8t+1.816)$.
Interactive · spring–mass lab

Free damped motion

Real springs slow down. A simple model is a damping force proportional to velocity, $-\beta\dfrac{dx}{dt}$, with damping constant $\beta>0$. The minus sign means it always opposes the motion. Newton’s law becomes

$$m\frac{d^2x}{dt^2}=-kx-\beta\frac{dx}{dt}.$$

Divide by $m$ and name the constants $2\lambda=\dfrac\beta m$, $\omega^2=\dfrac km$. (Writing $2\lambda$ makes the roots tidier.)

Free damped motion $$\frac{d^2x}{dt^2}+2\lambda\frac{dx}{dt}+\omega^2x=0,\qquad m^2+2\lambda m+\omega^2=0,$$ $$m_{1,2}=-\lambda\pm\sqrt{\lambda^2-\omega^2}.$$

The sign of $\lambda^2-\omega^2$ decides the kind of motion:

CaseConditionSolutionWhat you see
Overdamped$\lambda^2-\omega^2>0$$e^{-\lambda t}\left(c_1e^{\sqrt{\lambda^2-\omega^2}\,t}+c_2e^{-\sqrt{\lambda^2-\omega^2}\,t}\right)$Slow return, no oscillation, crosses equilibrium at most once
Critically damped$\lambda^2-\omega^2=0$$e^{-\lambda t}(c_1+c_2t)$Fastest return without oscillating
Underdamped$\lambda^2-\omega^2<0$$e^{-\lambda t}\left(c_1\cos\sqrt{\omega^2-\lambda^2}\,t+c_2\sin\sqrt{\omega^2-\lambda^2}\,t\right)$Oscillates with shrinking amplitude

Equivalently, compare $\beta^2$ with $4mk$: overdamped if $\beta^2>4mk$, critical if equal, underdamped if less. In every case the factor $e^{-\lambda t}$ makes $x(t)\to0$.

Example 3Overdamped motion

Solve $x''+5x'+4x=0$, $x(0)=1$, $x'(0)=1$, and describe the motion.

  1. Auxiliary equation. $m^2+5m+4=(m+1)(m+4)=0$, so $m=-1,-4$. Two real roots: overdamped.
  2. General solution. $x=c_1e^{-t}+c_2e^{-4t}$.
  3. Conditions. $x(0)=c_1+c_2=1$ and $x'(0)=-c_1-4c_2=1$. Adding gives $-3c_2=2$, so $c_2=-\dfrac23$ and $c_1=\dfrac53$.
  4. Answer. $x(t)=\dfrac53e^{-t}-\dfrac23e^{-4t}$.
  5. Interpretation. The mass starts 1 unit below equilibrium moving down. It reaches its lowest point when $x'=0$: $-\dfrac53e^{-t}+\dfrac83e^{-4t}=0$ gives $e^{3t}=\dfrac85$, $t=\dfrac13\ln\dfrac85\approx0.157$ s, where $x\approx1.069$. Then it creeps back toward equilibrium without ever passing it.
Example 4Critically damped motion

An 8-lb weight stretches a spring 2 ft. A damping force numerically equal to 2 times the instantaneous velocity acts on the system. Find the equation of motion if the weight is released from equilibrium with an upward velocity of 3 ft/s.

  1. Constants. $m=\dfrac8{32}=\dfrac14$ slug. Hooke: $8=k\cdot2$, so $k=4$. Damping: $\beta=2$.
  2. Equation. $\dfrac14x''+2x'+4x=0$. Multiply by 4: $x''+8x'+16x=0$.
  3. Roots. $m^2+8m+16=(m+4)^2=0$, a repeated root $m=-4$: critically damped.
  4. General solution. $x=c_1e^{-4t}+c_2te^{-4t}$.
  5. Conditions. Released from equilibrium: $x(0)=c_1=0$. Upward velocity: $x'(0)=-3$. Since $x'=c_2(1-4t)e^{-4t}$ when $c_1=0$, $x'(0)=c_2=-3$.
  6. Answer. $x(t)=-3te^{-4t}$. It reaches its highest point when $x'=0$, at $t=\dfrac14$ s, where $x=-\dfrac34e^{-1}\approx-0.276$ ft (about 3.3 in above equilibrium), and then returns.
Example 5Underdamped motion

A 16-lb weight is attached to a 5-ft spring. At equilibrium the spring measures 8.2 ft. The weight is pushed up and released from rest 2 ft above equilibrium. The medium offers a damping force numerically equal to the instantaneous velocity. Find $x(t)$.

  1. Stretch. The spring went from 5 ft to 8.2 ft, so $s=3.2$ ft. Hooke: $16=k(3.2)$, so $k=5$ lb/ft.
  2. Mass and damping. $m=\dfrac{16}{32}=\dfrac12$ slug, $\beta=1$.
  3. Equation. $\dfrac12x''+x'+5x=0$, or $x''+2x'+10x=0$.
  4. Roots. $m=\dfrac{-2\pm\sqrt{4-40}}{2}=-1\pm3i$: underdamped.
  5. General solution. $x=e^{-t}(c_1\cos3t+c_2\sin3t)$.
  6. Conditions. 2 ft above: $x(0)=c_1=-2$. From rest: $x'(0)=-c_1+3c_2=0$, so $c_2=\dfrac{c_1}{3}=-\dfrac23$.
  7. Answer. $x(t)=e^{-t}\left(-2\cos3t-\dfrac23\sin3t\right)$. It oscillates with “quasi period” $\dfrac{2\pi}3$ s while the amplitude shrinks like $e^{-t}$.
Optional: amplitude form for damped motion

An underdamped solution can be written $x(t)=Ae^{-\lambda t}\sin\left(\sqrt{\omega^2-\lambda^2}\,t+\phi\right)$ with $A=\sqrt{c_1^2+c_2^2}$ and $\tan\phi=\dfrac{c_1}{c_2}$. The factor $Ae^{-\lambda t}$ is the damped amplitude, and $\dfrac{2\pi}{\sqrt{\omega^2-\lambda^2}}$ is the quasi period.

Driven motion

Now let an external force $f(t)$ act, for example the support moving up and down. Adding it to Newton’s law and dividing by $m$:

Driven motion with damping $$\frac{d^2x}{dt^2}+2\lambda\frac{dx}{dt}+\omega^2x=F(t),\qquad F(t)=\frac{f(t)}{m}.$$

This is nonhomogeneous, so $x=x_c+x_p$. Use undetermined coefficients (or the Laplace transform from Chapter 4) for $x_p$.

Example 6Interpreting and solving a driven IVP

Interpret and solve $\dfrac15x''+1.2x'+2x=5\cos4t$, $x(0)=\dfrac12$, $x'(0)=0$.

  1. Interpret. Mass $m=\dfrac15$ slug, damping $\beta=1.2$, spring constant $k=2$, driving force $5\cos4t$. The mass starts at rest $\dfrac12$ unit below equilibrium.
  2. Clear the fraction. Multiply by 5: $x''+6x'+10x=25\cos4t$.
  3. Homogeneous part. $m^2+6m+10=0$ gives $m=-3\pm i$, so $x_c=e^{-3t}(c_1\cos t+c_2\sin t)$.
  4. Guess $x_p$. The right side is a cosine, so try $x_p=A\cos4t+B\sin4t$. Then $x_p''=-16x_p$ and $x_p'=-4A\sin4t+4B\cos4t$.
  5. Substitute. Collecting terms: $(-6A+24B)\cos4t+(-24A-6B)\sin4t=25\cos4t$.
  6. Solve for $A,B$. $-24A-6B=0$ gives $B=-4A$. Then $-6A-96A=25$, so $A=-\dfrac{25}{102}$ and $B=\dfrac{50}{51}$.
  7. Apply the ICs to the whole $x$. $x(0)=c_1-\dfrac{25}{102}=\dfrac12$ gives $c_1=\dfrac{38}{51}$. $x'(0)=-3c_1+c_2+4\cdot\dfrac{50}{51}=0$ gives $c_2=-\dfrac{86}{51}$.
  8. Answer. $x(t)=e^{-3t}\left(\dfrac{38}{51}\cos t-\dfrac{86}{51}\sin t\right)-\dfrac{25}{102}\cos4t+\dfrac{50}{51}\sin4t$.

Transient and steady state. In Example 6, $x_c$ dies out because of $e^{-3t}$. It is called the transient term. What remains for large $t$, $x_p$, is the steady-state solution. With damping, the long-run motion is always set by the driving force, whatever the initial conditions.

Example 7Transient and steady state

The solution of $x''+2x'+2x=4\cos t+2\sin t$, $x(0)=0$, $x'(0)=x_1$ is $x=(x_1-2)e^{-t}\sin t+2\sin t$.

  1. Transient. $(x_1-2)e^{-t}\sin t\to0$ as $t\to\infty$.
  2. Steady state. $2\sin t$ remains. Every starting velocity $x_1$ ends up following the same curve.

Undamped forced motion and resonance

With no damping there is no transient. When the driving frequency $\gamma$ approaches the natural frequency $\omega$, the amplitude grows without bound. This is resonance.

Example 8Undamped forced motion

Solve $x''+\omega^2x=F_0\sin\gamma t$, $x(0)=0$, $x'(0)=0$, with $\gamma\neq\omega$.

  1. Complementary. $x_c=c_1\cos\omega t+c_2\sin\omega t$.
  2. Particular. Try $x_p=A\cos\gamma t+B\sin\gamma t$ (allowed because $\gamma\neq\omega$). Substituting: $A(\omega^2-\gamma^2)\cos\gamma t+B(\omega^2-\gamma^2)\sin\gamma t=F_0\sin\gamma t$, so $A=0$, $B=\dfrac{F_0}{\omega^2-\gamma^2}$.
  3. First IC. $x(0)=c_1=0$.
  4. Second IC. $x'(0)=c_2\omega+\dfrac{F_0\gamma}{\omega^2-\gamma^2}=0$, so $c_2=-\dfrac{F_0\gamma}{\omega(\omega^2-\gamma^2)}$.
  5. Answer. $x(t)=\dfrac{F_0}{\omega(\omega^2-\gamma^2)}\left(-\gamma\sin\omega t+\omega\sin\gamma t\right)$.
  6. Pure resonance. As $\gamma\to\omega$ this is $\dfrac00$. By L’Hôpital’s rule in $\gamma$: $x(t)=\dfrac{F_0}{2\omega^2}\sin\omega t-\dfrac{F_0}{2\omega}t\cos\omega t$. The factor $t$ makes the swings grow without limit.

Series circuit analogue

For an inductor $L$, resistor $R$ and capacitor $C$ in series with voltage $E(t)$, Kirchhoff’s law says the voltage drops add up to $E(t)$: $L\dfrac{di}{dt}+Ri+\dfrac1Cq=E(t)$. Since $i=\dfrac{dq}{dt}$,

LRC series circuit $$L\frac{d^2q}{dt^2}+R\frac{dq}{dt}+\frac1Cq=E(t).$$
SpringCircuit
mass $m$inductance $L$
damping $\beta$resistance $R$
spring constant $k$$\dfrac1C$
force $f(t)$voltage $E(t)$
position $x$, velocity $x'$charge $q$, current $i=q'$

The same three cases appear: overdamped if $R^2-\dfrac{4L}{C}>0$, critically damped if $=0$, underdamped if $<0$.

Example 9Underdamped series circuit

Find $q(t)$ when $L=0.25$ h, $R=10\ \Omega$, $C=0.001$ F, $E(t)=0$, $q(0)=q_0$ C, $i(0)=0$.

  1. Equation. $\dfrac14q''+10q'+1000q=0$, or $q''+40q'+4000q=0$.
  2. Roots. $m=\dfrac{-40\pm\sqrt{1600-16000}}{2}=-20\pm60i$: underdamped.
  3. General solution. $q=e^{-20t}(c_1\cos60t+c_2\sin60t)$.
  4. Conditions. $q(0)=c_1=q_0$. $i(0)=q'(0)=-20c_1+60c_2=0$, so $c_2=\dfrac{q_0}3$.
  5. Answer. $q(t)=q_0e^{-20t}\left(\cos60t+\dfrac13\sin60t\right)=\dfrac{q_0\sqrt{10}}{3}e^{-20t}\sin(60t+1.249)$.
Example 10Steady-state current

Find the steady-state charge and current when $E(t)=E_0\sin\gamma t$.

  1. Guess. The steady state is the particular solution $q_p=A\sin\gamma t+B\cos\gamma t$.
  2. Solve for $A,B$. Substituting into $Lq''+Rq'+\dfrac qC=E_0\sin\gamma t$ and matching sines and cosines gives, with the reactance $X=L\gamma-\dfrac1{C\gamma}$, $A=-\dfrac{E_0X}{\gamma(X^2+R^2)}$, $B=-\dfrac{E_0R}{\gamma(X^2+R^2)}$.
  3. Current. $i_p=q_p'=\dfrac{E_0}{Z}\left(\dfrac RZ\sin\gamma t-\dfrac XZ\cos\gamma t\right)$, where the impedance is $Z=\sqrt{X^2+R^2}$.

More worked examples

Example 11From words to amplitude form, underdamped

An 8-lb weight stretches a spring 2 ft. The surrounding medium gives a damping force numerically equal to the instantaneous velocity. The weight is released from rest 1 ft below equilibrium. Find $x(t)$, write it in amplitude form, and give the quasi period.

  1. Mass. $W=8$ lb and $g=32$ ft/s², so $m=\dfrac{8}{32}=\dfrac14$ slug.
  2. Spring constant. Hooke's law at equilibrium: $8=k\cdot2$, so $k=4$ lb/ft.
  3. Damping constant. “Numerically equal to the instantaneous velocity” means $\beta=1$.
  4. Initial conditions. 1 ft below equilibrium: $x(0)=1$. Released from rest: $x'(0)=0$.
  5. The equation. $\dfrac14x''+x'+4x=0$. Multiply by $4$ to clear the fraction: $x''+4x'+16x=0$. So $2\lambda=4$ and $\omega^2=16$.
  6. Classify. $\lambda=2$ and $\omega=4$. Since $\lambda^2=4<16=\omega^2$, the motion is underdamped; we expect decaying oscillations.
  7. Roots. $r^2+4r+16=0$ gives $$r=\frac{-4\pm\sqrt{16-64}}{2}=-2\pm2\sqrt3\,i.$$
  8. General solution. $x=e^{-2t}\big(c_1\cos2\sqrt3t+c_2\sin2\sqrt3t\big)$.
  9. Apply $x(0)=1$. At $t=0$, $e^0=1$, $\cos0=1$ and $\sin0=0$, so $c_1=1$.
  10. Apply $x'(0)=0$. By the product rule, $x'(0)=-2c_1+2\sqrt3\,c_2=0$, so $c_2=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt3}=\dfrac{\sqrt3}{3}$.
  11. Equation of motion. $$x(t)=e^{-2t}\left(\cos2\sqrt3t+\frac{\sqrt3}{3}\sin2\sqrt3t\right).$$
  12. Amplitude. $A=\sqrt{c_1^2+c_2^2}=\sqrt{1+\dfrac13}=\dfrac{2}{\sqrt3}$.
  13. Phase. For the form $Ae^{-\lambda t}\sin(\omega_1t+\phi)$ we need $\sin\phi=\dfrac{c_1}{A}=\dfrac{\sqrt3}{2}>0$ and $\cos\phi=\dfrac{c_2}{A}=\dfrac12>0$. Both are positive, so $\phi$ is in the first quadrant: $\phi=\dfrac{\pi}{3}$.
  14. Amplitude form. $$x(t)=\frac{2}{\sqrt3}e^{-2t}\sin\!\left(2\sqrt3t+\frac{\pi}{3}\right).$$ The “amplitude” $\dfrac{2}{\sqrt3}e^{-2t}$ shrinks over time.
  15. Quasi period. The oscillating part has frequency $2\sqrt3$, so the time between successive maxima is $\dfrac{2\pi}{2\sqrt3}=\dfrac{\pi}{\sqrt3}\approx1.81$ s.
Example 12Deciding the damping before solving

A 1-kg mass is attached to a spring with constant $k=16$ N/m, in a medium with damping constant $\beta$. For which $\beta$ is the motion overdamped, critically damped, or underdamped?

  1. Equation. $x''+\beta x'+16x=0$, so $2\lambda=\beta$ and $\omega^2=16$.
  2. Compare. The discriminant of $r^2+\beta r+16$ is $\beta^2-64$. Its sign decides everything.
  3. Answer. Overdamped when $\beta>8$, critically damped when $\beta=8$, underdamped when $0<\beta<8$. (The critical value is $\beta=2\sqrt{km}$ in general.)
  4. Meaning. A car's shock absorbers are designed to be close to critical: just enough damping that the car does not bounce, but not so much that it feels stiff.
Example 13A force that dies away

A 1-kg mass hangs on a spring with $k=4$ N/m. There is no damping. Starting at rest at equilibrium, it is driven by $f(t)=5e^{-t}$. Find $x(t)$ and describe the long-term motion.

  1. IVP. $x''+4x=5e^{-t}$, $x(0)=0$, $x'(0)=0$.
  2. Complementary function. $r^2+4=0$, $r=\pm2i$, so $x_c=c_1\cos2t+c_2\sin2t$.
  3. Guess. The right side is an exponential that does not duplicate $x_c$, so try $x_p=Ae^{-t}$. Then $x_p''=Ae^{-t}$.
  4. Match. $Ae^{-t}+4Ae^{-t}=5Ae^{-t}=5e^{-t}$, so $A=1$.
  5. General solution. $x=c_1\cos2t+c_2\sin2t+e^{-t}$.
  6. Initial conditions. $x(0)=c_1+1=0$, so $c_1=-1$. $x'(0)=2c_2-1=0$, so $c_2=\dfrac12$.
  7. Answer. $$x(t)=-\cos2t+\frac12\sin2t+e^{-t}.$$
  8. Long-term motion. The force fades, and so does $e^{-t}$. What remains is free undamped motion, $-\cos2t+\dfrac12\sin2t$, with amplitude $\sqrt{1+\dfrac14}=\dfrac{\sqrt5}{2}\approx1.12$ m. The push left the system with energy it never loses, because there is no damping.
Example 14Resonance from first principles

Solve $x''+9x=6\sin3t$, $x(0)=0$, $x'(0)=0$, and explain the behaviour.

  1. Natural frequency. $x_c=c_1\cos3t+c_2\sin3t$, so $\omega=3$. The forcing frequency is also $3$. This is the resonance case.
  2. Why the usual guess fails. $A\cos3t+B\sin3t$ is already in $x_c$, so it produces $0$ on the left. Multiply the guess by $t$: $x_p=t(A\cos3t+B\sin3t)$.
  3. Derivatives. $x_p'=A\cos3t+B\sin3t+t(-3A\sin3t+3B\cos3t)$ and $x_p''=-6A\sin3t+6B\cos3t+t(-9A\cos3t-9B\sin3t)$.
  4. Substitute. In $x_p''+9x_p$ the terms with $t$ cancel, leaving $-6A\sin3t+6B\cos3t=6\sin3t$.
  5. Match. $-6A=6$ and $6B=0$, so $A=-1$, $B=0$, and $x_p=-t\cos3t$.
  6. Initial conditions. $x=c_1\cos3t+c_2\sin3t-t\cos3t$. $x(0)=c_1=0$. $x'(0)=3c_2-1=0$, so $c_2=\dfrac13$.
  7. Answer. $$x(t)=\frac13\sin3t-t\cos3t.$$
  8. Behaviour. The term $-t\cos3t$ oscillates between $-t$ and $t$, so the swings grow linearly in time. A real spring would break or stop obeying Hooke's law. Any damping, however small, keeps the amplitude bounded.

Test-day checklist

Setting up
  • Find $m$ from the weight ($m=\dfrac{W}{g}$), then $k$ from the stretch ($k=\dfrac{W}{s}$). Convert inches to feet.
  • Write $mx''+\beta x'+kx=f(t)$ and then divide by $m$.
  • Translate the initial position and velocity with down as positive.
  • Classify with $\lambda^2-\omega^2$ before solving, so you know what shape the answer should have.
Mistakes that cost marks
  • Using the weight as the mass. An 8-lb weight has mass $\dfrac14$ slug, not $8$.
  • Sign of the initial conditions. “Above” and “upward” are negative.
  • Forgetting to divide by $m$ before reading $2\lambda$ and $\omega^2$.
  • Wrong quadrant for the phase angle. Check the signs of $\sin\phi=\dfrac{c_1}{A}$ and $\cos\phi=\dfrac{c_2}{A}$; $\tan^{-1}$ alone only gives quadrants I and IV.
  • Missing resonance. When the forcing frequency equals $\omega$ and there is no damping, multiply the guess by $t$.
  • Circuits: $L\leftrightarrow m$, $R\leftrightarrow\beta$, $\dfrac1C\leftrightarrow k$, $E(t)\leftrightarrow f(t)$, charge $q\leftrightarrow x$. The current is $i=q'$.

Practice set · 25 problems

25 more problems on this section, weighted toward the kinds that show up on Test 2. Each one has an answer and a full step-by-step solution that explains why each step is taken. The numbers are fixed, so you can come back to the same problem.

Chapter 3 · Section 3.9 · on Test 2

Linear models: boundary-value problems

In an initial-value problem all the conditions are at one point. In a boundary-value problem (BVP) they are at two points, typically the two ends of a beam or a string. That small change has a big effect: a BVP can have one solution, none, or infinitely many.

Initial values versus boundary values

An initial-value problem gives all its conditions at one point, like $y(0)$ and $y'(0)$. Think of throwing a ball: knowing where it starts and how fast it moves fixes its whole path. That is the existence and uniqueness theorem: exactly one solution.

A boundary-value problem splits its conditions between two points, like $y(0)$ and $y(L)$. Now we are asking whether a path that starts at one place can be made to land at another. Sometimes exactly one path works. Sometimes none does. Sometimes infinitely many do. Example 2 in Section 3.1 showed all three.

For $y''+\lambda y=0$, $y(0)=0$, $y(L)=0$, the zero function always works. The interesting question is: for which $\lambda$ is there also a nonzero solution? Those special $\lambda$ are the eigenvalues, and the nonzero solutions are the eigenfunctions.

A picture to keep in mind

Hold a guitar string fixed at both ends. It can vibrate in only certain shapes: one hump, two humps, three humps, and so on, which are $\sin\dfrac{\pi x}{L}$, $\sin\dfrac{2\pi x}{L}$, $\sin\dfrac{3\pi x}{L}$. A shape with one and a half humps is impossible, because it would not be zero at the far end. The eigenvalue problem is the mathematics of “which shapes fit”. Exactly the same eigenfunctions return in Chapter 13, where they build the solutions of the heat and wave equations.

Why there are always three cases

The auxiliary equation of $y''+\lambda y=0$ is $m^2=-\lambda$. The form of $y$ depends on the sign of $\lambda$:

CaseRootsGeneral solutionTypical outcome
$\lambda=0$$m=0,0$$y=c_1+c_2x$a line; only survives conditions on $y'$ alone
$\lambda=-\alpha^2<0$$m=\pm\alpha$$y=c_1\cosh\alpha x+c_2\sinh\alpha x$no oscillation, so almost never fits two zero conditions
$\lambda=\alpha^2>0$$m=\pm i\alpha$$y=c_1\cos\alpha x+c_2\sin\alpha x$oscillates, so it fits when $\alpha$ is just right

The hyperbolic form is the convenient choice for $\lambda<0$ because $\cosh0=1$, $\sinh0=0$, and the derivatives swap cleanly: $(\cosh\alpha x)'=\alpha\sinh\alpha x$, $(\sinh\alpha x)'=\alpha\cosh\alpha x$. The key facts are that $\cosh$ is never zero, and $\sinh\alpha x$ is zero only at $x=0$.

You must check all three cases, because a test marks the reasoning, and because the case $\lambda=0$ really is an eigenvalue for some conditions (for example $y'(0)=0$, $y'(L)=0$).

The beam: what each derivative means

For a beam bending under load, $y(x)$ is the deflection (how far the centre line sags). Each derivative has a physical meaning, and every boundary condition is a statement about one of them:

QuantityFormulaZero means
Deflection$y$the beam is held at that height
Slope$y'$the beam is held level (cannot rotate)
Bending moment$M=EIy''$nothing twists the end
Shear force$EIy'''$nothing pushes the end up or down

An embedded (clamped) end is held in place and held level: $y=0$, $y'=0$. A simply supported end rests on a pin: it cannot move, but it can rotate freely, so $y=0$ and $y''=0$. A free end has nothing acting on it, so the moment and the shear force are both zero: $y''=0$, $y'''=0$. Every beam gets exactly two conditions at each end, four in total, one for each constant from integrating $EIy^{(4)}=w$ four times.

Deflection of a beam

Think of a horizontal beam of length $L$ sagging under a load $w(x)$ (force per unit length). Let $y(x)$ be the deflection, positive downward. Beam theory says the bending moment is $M(x)=EI\,y''$, where $E$ is Young’s modulus of the material and $I$ is the moment of inertia of the cross-section ($EI$ is the flexural rigidity). Differentiating twice and using $M''=w$ gives

Beam equation $$EI\frac{d^4y}{dx^4}=w(x).$$

A fourth-order equation needs four conditions, two at each end. Each type of support gives two:

End of the beamConditionsWhy
Embedded (clamped)$y=0,\ y'=0$No deflection and no slope
Free$y''=0,\ y'''=0$No bending moment and no shear force
Simply supported (hinged)$y=0,\ y''=0$No deflection, free to rotate
Interactive · beam deflection
Example 1An embedded beam

A beam of length $L$ is embedded at both ends. Find the deflection under a constant load $w(x)=w_0$.

  1. Set up. $EI\,y^{(4)}=w_0$ with $y(0)=0$, $y'(0)=0$, $y(L)=0$, $y'(L)=0$ (embedded at both ends).
  2. Integrate four times. $y^{(4)}=\dfrac{w_0}{EI}$ is a constant, so $y=c_1+c_2x+c_3x^2+c_4x^3+\dfrac{w_0}{24EI}x^4$. (The polynomial part is $y_c$; the last term is $y_p$.)
  3. Left end. $y(0)=c_1=0$ and $y'(0)=c_2=0$.
  4. Right end. $y(L)=c_3L^2+c_4L^3+\dfrac{w_0L^4}{24EI}=0$ and $y'(L)=2c_3L+3c_4L^2+\dfrac{w_0L^3}{6EI}=0$.
  5. Solve the pair. Multiply the first by $\dfrac2L$ and subtract from the second: $c_4L^2+\dfrac{w_0L^3}{12EI}=0$, so $c_4=-\dfrac{w_0L}{12EI}$. Then $c_3=\dfrac{w_0L^2}{24EI}$.
  6. Answer. $y(x)=\dfrac{w_0}{24EI}\left(x^4-2Lx^3+L^2x^2\right)=\dfrac{w_0}{24EI}x^2(x-L)^2$. The maximum sag is at the middle, $y\!\left(\dfrac L2\right)=\dfrac{w_0L^4}{384EI}$.

Eigenvalues and eigenfunctions

A homogeneous BVP always has the trivial solution $y=0$. Often the equation contains a parameter $\lambda$, and the question is: for which $\lambda$ does the BVP have a nontrivial solution? Those values are eigenvalues, and the matching nontrivial solutions are eigenfunctions. This is the idea behind buckling columns, vibrating strings and, in Chapter 13, every PDE we solve.

Example 2Nontrivial solutions of a BVP

Solve $y''+\lambda y=0$, $y(0)=0$, $y(L)=0$.

  1. Why three cases. The roots of $m^2+\lambda=0$ are real, zero or imaginary depending on the sign of $\lambda$, so the form of $y$ changes. We must check all three.
  2. Case $\lambda=0$. $y''=0$ gives $y=c_1+c_2x$. Then $y(0)=c_1=0$ and $y(L)=c_2L=0$, so $c_2=0$. Only the trivial solution.
  3. Case $\lambda<0$. Write $\lambda=-\alpha^2$ with $\alpha>0$. Then $y''-\alpha^2y=0$ and $y=c_1\cosh\alpha x+c_2\sinh\alpha x$. (We use $\cosh$ and $\sinh$ rather than exponentials because $\cosh0=1$, $\sinh0=0$ make the condition at $0$ easy.) $y(0)=c_1=0$. $y(L)=c_2\sinh\alpha L=0$, and $\sinh\alpha L\neq0$ for $\alpha L\neq0$, so $c_2=0$. Only the trivial solution.
  4. Case $\lambda>0$. Write $\lambda=\alpha^2$. Then $y=c_1\cos\alpha x+c_2\sin\alpha x$. $y(0)=c_1=0$. Now $y(L)=c_2\sin\alpha L=0$.
  5. The key step. To get a nontrivial solution we need $c_2\neq0$, so we need $\sin\alpha L=0$. That happens when $\alpha L=n\pi$, that is $\alpha=\dfrac{n\pi}L$, $n=1,2,3,\dots$
  6. Answer. Eigenvalues $\lambda_n=\dfrac{n^2\pi^2}{L^2}$, eigenfunctions $y_n=\sin\dfrac{n\pi x}{L}$ (times any nonzero constant), $n=1,2,3,\dots$ For any other $\lambda$, only $y=0$ works.
Interactive · eigenfunctions
Example 3Example 2 revisited

With $L=\pi$, the eigenvalues of $y''+\lambda y=0$, $y(0)=0$, $y(\pi)=0$ are $\lambda_n=n^2$.

  1. So $y''+10y=0$, $y(0)=0$, $y(\pi)=0$ has only the trivial solution, because $10$ is not a perfect square.
  2. But $y''+9y=0$ with the same conditions has the nontrivial solutions $y=c\sin3x$.
How to attack any eigenvalue problem
  1. Treat $\lambda=0$, $\lambda=-\alpha^2<0$ and $\lambda=\alpha^2>0$ separately.
  2. In each case write the general solution and apply the first boundary condition to kill one constant.
  3. Apply the second condition. Ask: can the remaining constant be nonzero? If yes, the equation it leaves (like $\sin\alpha L=0$) gives the eigenvalues.

Other boundary conditions give other eigenfunctions. For instance $y'(0)=0$, $y'(L)=0$ gives $\cos\dfrac{n\pi x}{L}$ and also allows $\lambda=0$ with $y=1$.

Buckling of a thin vertical column

Euler studied a long slender column of length $L$ pushed down by a load $P$. If $y(x)$ is its sideways deflection, beam theory gives $EI\,y''=-Py$, that is

$$EI\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}+Py=0.$$
Example 4The Euler load

Find the deflection of a column hinged at both ends: $EIy''+Py=0$, $y(0)=0$, $y(L)=0$.

  1. Recognize the problem. Divide by $EI$: $y''+\lambda y=0$ with $\lambda=\dfrac P{EI}$. This is exactly Example 2.
  2. Eigenvalues. Nontrivial deflections need $\dfrac{P}{EI}=\dfrac{n^2\pi^2}{L^2}$, so $P_n=\dfrac{n^2\pi^2EI}{L^2}$, with shapes $y_n=c\sin\dfrac{n\pi x}L$.
  3. Meaning. For loads below $P_1=\dfrac{\pi^2EI}{L^2}$, the only solution is $y=0$: the column stays straight. $P_1$ is the critical load or Euler load, where the column first buckles into one arch.
Optional: the rotating string

A string of length $L$, density $\rho$ and tension $T$, fixed at both ends and spun at angular speed $\omega$, satisfies $Ty''+\rho\omega^2y=0$, $y(0)=y(L)=0$. Nontrivial shapes need $\dfrac{\rho\omega^2}{T}=\dfrac{n^2\pi^2}{L^2}$, so the string only bulges out at the critical speeds $\omega_n=\dfrac{n\pi}{L}\sqrt{\dfrac T\rho}$.

More worked examples

Example 5A cantilever under uniform load

A beam of length $L$ is embedded at $x=0$ and free at $x=L$ (a cantilever, like a diving board). It carries a constant load $w_0$. Find the deflection and the largest deflection.

  1. Equation. $EI\dfrac{d^4y}{dx^4}=w_0$, so $y^{(4)}=\dfrac{w_0}{EI}$, a constant.
  2. Integrate four times. Each integration adds a constant: $$\begin{aligned}y'''&=\frac{w_0}{EI}x+6c_3,\qquad y''=\frac{w_0}{2EI}x^2+6c_3x+2c_2,\\ y&=\frac{w_0}{24EI}x^4+c_3x^3+c_2x^2+c_1x+c_0.\end{aligned}$$ (Naming the constants $6c_3$ and $2c_2$ keeps $y$ tidy.)
  3. Conditions at the clamped end. $y(0)=c_0=0$ and $y'(0)=c_1=0$.
  4. Free end, shear. $y'''(L)=\dfrac{w_0L}{EI}+6c_3=0$, so $c_3=-\dfrac{w_0L}{6EI}$.
  5. Free end, moment. $y''(L)=\dfrac{w_0L^2}{2EI}+6c_3L+2c_2=0$. Substituting $c_3$: $\dfrac{w_0L^2}{2EI}-\dfrac{w_0L^2}{EI}+2c_2=0$, so $c_2=\dfrac{w_0L^2}{4EI}$.
  6. Deflection. $$y(x)=\frac{w_0}{24EI}\left(x^4-4Lx^3+6L^2x^2\right).$$
  7. Largest deflection. The sag increases toward the free end, so evaluate at $x=L$: $$y(L)=\frac{w_0}{24EI}(1-4+6)L^4=\frac{w_0L^4}{8EI}.$$ Doubling the length multiplies the sag by $16$, which is why long diving boards are so bouncy.
Example 6A simply supported beam

The same uniform load $w_0$ acts on a beam of length $L$ that is simply supported at both ends. Find $y(x)$ and the sag at the middle.

  1. Start from the same integrated form. $y=\dfrac{w_0}{24EI}x^4+c_3x^3+c_2x^2+c_1x+c_0$.
  2. At $x=0$. $y(0)=c_0=0$ and $y''(0)=2c_2=0$.
  3. At $x=L$, moment. $y''(L)=\dfrac{w_0L^2}{2EI}+6c_3L=0$, so $c_3=-\dfrac{w_0L}{12EI}$.
  4. At $x=L$, deflection. $y(L)=\dfrac{w_0L^4}{24EI}-\dfrac{w_0L^4}{12EI}+c_1L=0$, so $c_1=\dfrac{w_0L^3}{24EI}$.
  5. Deflection. $$y(x)=\frac{w_0}{24EI}\left(x^4-2Lx^3+L^3x\right).$$
  6. Sag at the middle. At $x=\dfrac L2$: $$y\!\left(\frac L2\right)=\frac{w_0}{24EI}\left(\frac{1}{16}-\frac{1}{4}+\frac12\right)L^4=\frac{5w_0L^4}{384EI}.$$ This is only about a tenth of the cantilever's sag $\dfrac{w_0L^4}{8EI}$, because both ends are supported.
Example 7When $\lambda=0$ is an eigenvalue

Find the eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of $y''+\lambda y=0$, $y'(0)=0$, $y'(\pi)=0$.

  1. Case $\lambda=0$. $y=c_1+c_2x$, so $y'=c_2$. Both conditions say $c_2=0$, but nothing restricts $c_1$. So $y=c_1$ is a nonzero solution: $\lambda_0=0$ is an eigenvalue, with eigenfunction $y_0=1$.
  2. Case $\lambda=-\alpha^2<0$. $y=c_1\cosh\alpha x+c_2\sinh\alpha x$ and $y'=\alpha(c_1\sinh\alpha x+c_2\cosh\alpha x)$. Then $y'(0)=\alpha c_2=0$ gives $c_2=0$, and $y'(\pi)=\alpha c_1\sinh\alpha\pi=0$. Since $\sinh\alpha\pi\neq0$, $c_1=0$. Only the zero solution, so no negative eigenvalues.
  3. Case $\lambda=\alpha^2>0$. $y=c_1\cos\alpha x+c_2\sin\alpha x$ and $y'=-\alpha c_1\sin\alpha x+\alpha c_2\cos\alpha x$. Then $y'(0)=\alpha c_2=0$, so $c_2=0$.
  4. The second condition. $y'(\pi)=-\alpha c_1\sin\alpha\pi=0$. To keep $c_1\neq0$ we need $\sin\alpha\pi=0$, so $\alpha=n$ for $n=1,2,3,\dots$
  5. Answer. $\lambda_0=0$, $y_0=1$, and $\lambda_n=n^2$, $y_n=\cos nx$ for $n=1,2,3,\dots$ These are the functions of a Fourier cosine series (Section 13.2).
Example 8Mixed conditions

Find the eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of $y''+\lambda y=0$, $y(0)=0$, $y'(1)=0$.

  1. $\lambda=0$. $y=c_1+c_2x$; $y(0)=c_1=0$ and $y'(1)=c_2=0$. Not an eigenvalue.
  2. $\lambda=-\alpha^2$. $y(0)=c_1=0$ leaves $y=c_2\sinh\alpha x$, and $y'(1)=\alpha c_2\cosh\alpha=0$. Since $\cosh\alpha\neq0$, $c_2=0$. No eigenvalues.
  3. $\lambda=\alpha^2$. $y(0)=c_1=0$ leaves $y=c_2\sin\alpha x$, and $y'(1)=\alpha c_2\cos\alpha=0$ needs $\cos\alpha=0$.
  4. Solve $\cos\alpha=0$. $\alpha=\dfrac{\pi}{2},\dfrac{3\pi}{2},\dfrac{5\pi}{2},\dots$, that is $\alpha=\dfrac{(2n-1)\pi}{2}$.
  5. Answer. $$\lambda_n=\frac{(2n-1)^2\pi^2}{4},\qquad y_n=\sin\frac{(2n-1)\pi x}{2},\quad n=1,2,3,\dots$$ The first shape is a quarter of a sine wave: zero at the left and flat at the right.
Which condition gives which eigenvalues on $[0,L]$
ConditionsEigenvaluesEigenfunctions
$y(0)=0,\ y(L)=0$$\left(\dfrac{n\pi}{L}\right)^2$$\sin\dfrac{n\pi x}{L}$
$y'(0)=0,\ y'(L)=0$$0$ and $\left(\dfrac{n\pi}{L}\right)^2$$1$ and $\cos\dfrac{n\pi x}{L}$
$y(0)=0,\ y'(L)=0$$\left(\dfrac{(2n-1)\pi}{2L}\right)^2$$\sin\dfrac{(2n-1)\pi x}{2L}$
$y'(0)=0,\ y(L)=0$$\left(\dfrac{(2n-1)\pi}{2L}\right)^2$$\cos\dfrac{(2n-1)\pi x}{2L}$

Use the table to check answers, but on a test show the three cases.

Test-day checklist

Mistakes that cost marks
  • Skipping a case. Write out $\lambda=0$, $\lambda<0$ and $\lambda>0$ every time, even when the first two give nothing.
  • Including $n=0$ for sines. $\sin0=0$ is the zero function, which is never an eigenfunction.
  • Forgetting $\lambda=0$ when both conditions are on $y'$. Then $y=1$ works.
  • Mixing up beam supports. Embedded: $y,y'$. Simply supported: $y,y''$. Free: $y'',y'''$.
  • Forgetting to divide by $EI$ before integrating, or losing the factorials ($24=4!$ for $x^4$, $120=5!$ for $x^5$).
  • Buckling: the critical (Euler) load is the smallest eigenvalue times $EI$: $P_1=\dfrac{\pi^2EI}{L^2}$ for pinned ends.

Practice set · 36 problems

36 more problems on this section, weighted toward the kinds that show up on Test 2. Each one has an answer and a full step-by-step solution that explains why each step is taken. The numbers are fixed, so you can come back to the same problem.

Chapter 4 · Section 4.1 · on Test 2

Definition of the Laplace transform

In calculus you met operations that turn one function into another: differentiation and integration. The Laplace transform is another such operation. It takes a function of time $t$ and produces a function of a new variable $s$. Its great value is that it turns a linear differential equation with initial conditions into an algebra problem.

Turning calculus into algebra

Chapter 3 solved $ay''+by'+cy=g(t)$ in several stages: find $y_c$, guess or vary parameters for $y_p$, then fit the constants to the initial conditions. The Laplace transform does all of that in one pass, and it handles right sides that switch on and off, which the earlier methods handle badly.

The plan has three steps:

  1. Transform. Apply $\mathcal L$ to both sides. Derivatives of $y(t)$ become multiplication by $s$ (Section 4.2), so the differential equation becomes an algebra equation for $Y(s)=\mathcal L\{y\}$, with the initial conditions already built in.
  2. Solve. Solve that algebra equation for $Y(s)$. This is usually just division.
  3. Transform back. Find the function $y(t)$ whose transform is $Y(s)$, usually with partial fractions and a table.

Section 4.1 builds the tool: what the transform is, how to compute it, and which functions have one.

Reading the definition

$$\mathcal L\{f(t)\}=\int_0^\infty e^{-st}f(t)\,dt=F(s).$$
  • The input is a function of $t$ (time). The output is a function of a new variable $s$. You integrate $t$ away, so $t$ never appears in $F(s)$.
  • The weight $e^{-st}$ decays fast when $s>0$. It tames $f(t)$ so that the improper integral can converge. That is why answers come with conditions like “$s>3$”: the weight must decay faster than $f$ grows.
  • The integral is improper, so it really means $\displaystyle\lim_{b\to\infty}\int_0^b$. In practice you evaluate at $b$, and the term with $e^{-sb}$ goes to $0$ as $b\to\infty$ when $s$ is large enough.
  • Only values for $t\ge0$ matter. The transform cannot see what $f$ does for $t<0$.

Three techniques you need

  1. Direct integration for exponentials and constants: $\displaystyle\int_0^\infty e^{-(s-a)t}\,dt=\frac{1}{s-a}$ for $s>a$.
  2. Integration by parts for $t^n$, $\sin kt$, $\cos kt$. With the $e^{-st}$ factor, the boundary terms vanish at both ends (at $0$ because of the $t$ or $\sin$ factor, at $\infty$ because of $e^{-st}$), which leaves a simpler transform.
  3. Linearity and the table. Once a few transforms are known, $\mathcal L\{\alpha f+\beta g\}=\alpha F+\beta G$ gives everything else. On a test, use the table unless the question says “use the definition”.

Which functions have a transform?

Enough for this course: $f$ must be piecewise continuous (finitely many jumps on each finite interval, no vertical asymptotes) and of exponential order, meaning $|f(t)|\le Me^{ct}$ for large $t$. Polynomials, exponentials, sines and cosines all qualify. A function like $e^{t^2}$ does not, because it eventually outgrows every $e^{ct}$, so no choice of $s$ makes the integral converge. A function like $\dfrac1t$ fails for a different reason: it blows up at $t=0$.

One more fact is worth remembering: if $f$ qualifies, then $F(s)\to0$ as $s\to\infty$. So something like $F(s)=s$ or $F(s)=\dfrac{s^2}{s+1}$ cannot be the transform of an ordinary function. This is a quick check on your answers.

Integral transforms

If $f(x,y)$ is a function of two variables, integrating over one of them leaves a function of the other. For example, $\displaystyle\int_1^2 2xy^2\,dx=3y^2$. In the same way, a definite integral such as

$$\int_a^b K(s,t)\,f(t)\,dt$$

turns a function $f(t)$ into a function of $s$. This is called an integral transform, and $K(s,t)$ is its kernel. We are interested in the case where the interval is $[0,\infty)$. Then the integral is improper and is defined as a limit:

$$\int_0^{\infty}K(s,t)f(t)\,dt=\lim_{b\to\infty}\int_0^{b}K(s,t)f(t)\,dt.$$

If the limit exists we say the integral converges. Otherwise it diverges. The Laplace transform uses the kernel $K(s,t)=e^{-st}$.

Definition 4.1.1 · Laplace transform

Let $f$ be a function defined for $t\ge0$. Then the integral

$$\mathcal{L}\{f(t)\}=\int_0^{\infty}e^{-st}f(t)\,dt \tag{1}$$

is the Laplace transform of $f$, provided the integral converges. The result is a function of $s$, written $F(s)$.

Notation: lowercase letters are functions of $t$ and the matching capital letters are their transforms, so $\mathcal{L}\{f(t)\}=F(s)$, $\mathcal{L}\{g(t)\}=G(s)$ and $\mathcal{L}\{y(t)\}=Y(s)$.

Interactive · the transform is an area

Pick $f$ and slide $s$. The shaded area under $e^{-st}f(t)$ is the number $F(s)$. Larger $s$ makes the weight $e^{-st}$ die faster, so the area shrinks. You will see below that $F(s)\to0$ as $s\to\infty$ for every transform we meet.

Computing transforms from the definition

Every example below follows the same three steps:

  1. Replace $\infty$ by $b$ and evaluate the ordinary integral $\int_0^b$.
  2. Let $b\to\infty$.
  3. Note which values of $s$ make the limit exist.

The one limit you need over and over is this: if $s>0$, then $e^{-sb}\to0$ as $b\to\infty$. If $s\le0$ it does not go to zero.

Example 1The transform of $1$

Evaluate $\mathcal{L}\{1\}$.

  1. Write the definition with $f(t)=1$. $\mathcal{L}\{1\}=\displaystyle\int_0^{\infty}e^{-st}\cdot1\,dt=\lim_{b\to\infty}\int_0^{b}e^{-st}\,dt$.
  2. Integrate. An antiderivative of $e^{-st}$ with respect to $t$ is $-\dfrac{e^{-st}}{s}$, because differentiating it brings down $-s$. So $\displaystyle\int_0^{b}e^{-st}\,dt=\left[-\frac{e^{-st}}{s}\right]_0^{b}=\frac{-e^{-sb}+1}{s}$.
  3. Take the limit. For $s>0$ the term $e^{-sb}$ goes to $0$ as $b\to\infty$, leaving $\dfrac1s$.
  4. Note when it fails. For $s<0$ the term $e^{-sb}$ blows up, so the integral diverges. (For $s=0$ the integral is $\int_0^\infty1\,dt$, which also diverges.)
  5. Answer. $\mathcal{L}\{1\}=\dfrac1s$ for $s>0$.

From now on we will use the shorthand $\big[\ \cdot\ \big]_0^{\infty}$ for "evaluate at $b$ and let $b\to\infty$." For example $\left[-\dfrac{e^{-st}}{s}\right]_0^{\infty}=\dfrac1s$ when $s>0$.

Example 2The transform of $t$, by parts

Evaluate $\mathcal{L}\{t\}$.

  1. Set up. $\mathcal{L}\{t\}=\displaystyle\int_0^{\infty}e^{-st}\,t\,dt$. The integrand is a product, so use integration by parts.
  2. Choose the parts. Let $u=t$ (it gets simpler when differentiated) and $dv=e^{-st}dt$. Then $du=dt$ and $v=-\dfrac{e^{-st}}{s}$.
  3. Apply $\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du$. $\displaystyle\int_0^{\infty}te^{-st}\,dt=\left[-\frac{te^{-st}}{s}\right]_0^{\infty}+\frac1s\int_0^{\infty}e^{-st}\,dt$.
  4. The boundary term is zero. At $t=0$ it is $0$. As $t\to\infty$, $te^{-st}\to0$ for $s>0$ because the exponential decays faster than $t$ grows (L'Hôpital's rule on $\dfrac{t}{e^{st}}$).
  5. Recognize what is left. The remaining integral is exactly $\mathcal{L}\{1\}=\dfrac1s$ from Example 1.
  6. Answer. $\mathcal{L}\{t\}=\dfrac1s\cdot\dfrac1s=\dfrac{1}{s^2}$ for $s>0$.

Repeating this argument shows $\mathcal{L}\{t^n\}=\dfrac{n}{s}\mathcal{L}\{t^{n-1}\}$, which builds up to $\mathcal{L}\{t^n\}=\dfrac{n!}{s^{n+1}}$.

Example 3An exponential

Evaluate $\mathcal{L}\{e^{-3t}\}$.

  1. Combine the exponentials. $\mathcal{L}\{e^{-3t}\}=\displaystyle\int_0^{\infty}e^{-st}e^{-3t}\,dt=\int_0^{\infty}e^{-(s+3)t}\,dt$. Adding the exponents is the key move.
  2. Integrate. This is Example 1 with $s$ replaced by $s+3$: $\left[-\dfrac{e^{-(s+3)t}}{s+3}\right]_0^{\infty}$.
  3. Take the limit. The exponential dies when $s+3>0$, that is $s>-3$. Then the result is $\dfrac{1}{s+3}$.
  4. Answer. $\mathcal{L}\{e^{-3t}\}=\dfrac{1}{s+3}$ for $s>-3$. In general $\mathcal{L}\{e^{at}\}=\dfrac{1}{s-a}$ for $s>a$.
Example 4A sine, by parts twice

Evaluate $\mathcal{L}\{\sin2t\}$.

  1. First integration by parts. Take $u=\sin2t$, $dv=e^{-st}dt$, so $du=2\cos2t\,dt$, $v=-\dfrac{e^{-st}}{s}$. Then $\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\{\sin2t\}=\left[-\frac{e^{-st}\sin2t}{s}\right]_0^{\infty}+\frac2s\int_0^{\infty}e^{-st}\cos2t\,dt$.
  2. The boundary term vanishes. $\sin0=0$ at the bottom, and $e^{-st}\sin2t\to0$ at the top for $s>0$ because $|\sin2t|\le1$. So $\mathcal{L}\{\sin2t\}=\dfrac2s\displaystyle\int_0^{\infty}e^{-st}\cos2t\,dt$.
  3. Second integration by parts. Now take $u=\cos2t$, $du=-2\sin2t\,dt$: $$\begin{aligned}\int_0^{\infty}e^{-st}\cos2t\,dt&=\left[-\frac{e^{-st}\cos2t}{s}\right]_0^{\infty}-\frac2s\int_0^{\infty}e^{-st}\sin2t\,dt\\&=\frac1s-\frac2s\mathcal{L}\{\sin2t\}.\end{aligned}$$ (At $t=0$, $\cos0=1$, which gives the $\dfrac1s$.)
  4. The original integral came back. Substitute: $\mathcal{L}\{\sin2t\}=\dfrac2s\left(\dfrac1s-\dfrac2s\mathcal{L}\{\sin2t\}\right)=\dfrac{2}{s^2}-\dfrac{4}{s^2}\mathcal{L}\{\sin2t\}$.
  5. Solve for it like an unknown. $\left(1+\dfrac{4}{s^2}\right)\mathcal{L}\{\sin2t\}=\dfrac{2}{s^2}$. Multiply both sides by $s^2$: $(s^2+4)\mathcal{L}\{\sin2t\}=2$.
  6. Answer. $\mathcal{L}\{\sin2t\}=\dfrac{2}{s^2+4}$ for $s>0$. The same work with $k$ in place of $2$ gives $\mathcal{L}\{\sin kt\}=\dfrac{k}{s^2+k^2}$ and, from step 3, $\mathcal{L}\{\cos kt\}=\dfrac{s}{s^2+k^2}$.

$\mathcal{L}$ is a linear transform

An integral of a sum is the sum of the integrals, and constants come out of integrals. So whenever both transforms exist,

Linearity $$\mathcal{L}\{\alpha f(t)+\beta g(t)\}=\alpha\,\mathcal{L}\{f(t)\}+\beta\,\mathcal{L}\{g(t)\}=\alpha F(s)+\beta G(s). \tag{2}$$

This is why we almost never use the definition directly. We break a function into pieces we already know.

Example 5Using linearity

Find (a) $\mathcal{L}\{1+5t\}$ and (b) $\mathcal{L}\{4e^{-3t}-10\sin2t\}$.

  1. (a) Split the sum. $\mathcal{L}\{1+5t\}=\mathcal{L}\{1\}+5\mathcal{L}\{t\}$.
  2. (a) Use Examples 1 and 2. $=\dfrac1s+\dfrac{5}{s^2}$, valid for $s>0$.
  3. (b) Split. $\mathcal{L}\{4e^{-3t}-10\sin2t\}=4\mathcal{L}\{e^{-3t}\}-10\mathcal{L}\{\sin2t\}$.
  4. (b) Use Examples 3 and 4. $=\dfrac{4}{s+3}-\dfrac{20}{s^2+4}$.
  5. (b) Where it is valid. The first piece needs $s>-3$, the second needs $s>0$. Both hold when $s>0$, so that is the range for the sum.

The basic table

Combining the examples above gives the transforms you will use constantly (Zill's Theorem 4.1.1). Learn these by heart.

$f(t)$$F(s)$valid for
$1$$\dfrac1s$$s>0$
$t^n$, $n=1,2,3,\dots$$\dfrac{n!}{s^{n+1}}$$s>0$
$e^{at}$$\dfrac{1}{s-a}$$s>a$
$\sin kt$$\dfrac{k}{s^2+k^2}$$s>0$
$\cos kt$$\dfrac{s}{s^2+k^2}$$s>0$
$\sinh kt$$\dfrac{k}{s^2-k^2}$$s>|k|$
$\cosh kt$$\dfrac{s}{s^2-k^2}$$s>|k|$

A quick way to remember the sine and cosine pair: sine has $k$ on top, cosine has $s$ on top. The hyperbolic versions are the same with a minus sign in the denominator.

Example 6Using the table

Find (a) $\mathcal{L}\{t^3\}$, (b) $\mathcal{L}\{\sinh3t\}$ and (c) $\mathcal{L}\{\sin^2t\}$.

  1. (a) With $n=3$: $\mathcal{L}\{t^3\}=\dfrac{3!}{s^4}=\dfrac{6}{s^4}$.
  2. (b) With $k=3$: $\mathcal{L}\{\sinh3t\}=\dfrac{3}{s^2-9}$, valid for $s>3$.
  3. (c) $\sin^2t$ is not in the table. It is a product, and $\mathcal{L}\{fg\}$ is not $F(s)G(s)$. So first rewrite it with the half-angle identity $\sin^2t=\dfrac{1-\cos2t}{2}$.
  4. (c) Transform each piece. $\mathcal{L}\{\sin^2t\}=\dfrac12\mathcal{L}\{1\}-\dfrac12\mathcal{L}\{\cos2t\}=\dfrac{1}{2s}-\dfrac12\cdot\dfrac{s}{s^2+4}$.
  5. (c) Combine over a common denominator. $\dfrac{(s^2+4)-s^2}{2s(s^2+4)}=\dfrac{2}{s(s^2+4)}$.
Example 7A piecewise function

Evaluate $\mathcal{L}\{f(t)\}$ for $f(t)=\begin{cases}0,&0\le t<3\\ 2,&t\ge3.\end{cases}$

  1. Split the integral where the formula changes. $\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\{f\}=\int_0^{3}e^{-st}\cdot0\,dt+\int_3^{\infty}e^{-st}\cdot2\,dt$.
  2. The first piece is zero because the integrand is zero.
  3. Integrate the second. $\displaystyle\int_3^{\infty}2e^{-st}\,dt=\left[-\frac{2e^{-st}}{s}\right]_3^{\infty}=0-\left(-\frac{2e^{-3s}}{s}\right)$, for $s>0$.
  4. Answer. $\mathcal{L}\{f\}=\dfrac{2e^{-3s}}{s}$. The factor $e^{-3s}$ is the signature of something switching on at $t=3$. Section 4.3 explains this pattern.

When does the transform exist?

The integral (1) does not converge for every function. For example, $f(t)=e^{t^2}$ grows so fast that no amount of $e^{-st}$ can tame it. Two conditions together guarantee existence.

Piecewise continuous. On every finite interval, $f$ has at most finitely many jumps, and it is continuous between them. Step functions, square waves and anything smooth qualify.

Definition 4.1.2 · Exponential order

A function $f$ is of exponential order $c$ if there are constants $c$, $M>0$ and $T>0$ such that

$$|f(t)|\le Me^{ct}\qquad\text{for all }t>T.$$

In words: eventually $f$ grows no faster than some exponential.

The functions $t$, $e^{-t}$ and $2\cos t$ are all of exponential order, with $c=1$, $c=-1$ and $c=0$ respectively: $|t|\le e^t$, $|e^{-t}|\le e^{-t}$ and $|2\cos t|\le2$. Any power $t^n$ is of exponential order because $t^n/e^{ct}\to0$ for every $c>0$. But $e^{t^2}$ is not, because $e^{t^2}/e^{ct}=e^{t^2-ct}\to\infty$ for every $c$.

Theorem 4.1.2 · Sufficient conditions for existence

If $f$ is piecewise continuous on $[0,\infty)$ and of exponential order $c$, then $\mathcal{L}\{f(t)\}$ exists for $s>c$.

Why it works: split $\int_0^\infty$ into $\int_0^T+\int_T^\infty$. The first part is a proper integral of a piecewise continuous function, so it exists. For the second, $|e^{-st}f(t)|\le Me^{-st}e^{ct}=Me^{-(s-c)t}$, and $\int_T^\infty Me^{-(s-c)t}dt$ converges when $s>c$. A smaller positive integrand that sits under a convergent one must converge too.

Theorem 4.1.3 · Behaviour as $s\to\infty$

If $f$ is piecewise continuous and of exponential order, then $\displaystyle\lim_{s\to\infty}F(s)=0$.

This gives a fast check. $F_1(s)=1$ and $F_2(s)=\dfrac{s}{s+1}$ do not go to $0$, so they are not the transform of any piecewise continuous function of exponential order. If your algebra ever produces such an $F(s)$, something went wrong.

Why the table has the conditions it has

Each table entry is valid for $s$ greater than the growth rate of $f$. $\sin kt$ and $\cos kt$ stay bounded, so $c=0$ and they need $s>0$. $e^{at}$ grows at rate $a$, so it needs $s>a$. In practice we just assume $s$ is large enough for everything in a problem, and we rarely write the condition.

Summary
  • $\mathcal{L}\{f\}=\int_0^\infty e^{-st}f(t)\,dt$, computed as a limit.
  • $\mathcal{L}$ is linear, so split sums and pull out constants.
  • Rewrite products like $\sin^2t$ with identities before transforming.
  • Piecewise: split the integral at each break point.
  • A genuine transform always satisfies $F(s)\to0$ as $s\to\infty$.

More worked material and 50 numbered problems: Laplace transforms page.

More worked examples

Example 8$\mathcal L\{t^2\}$ by parts, using an earlier result

Use the definition to find $\mathcal L\{t^2\}$.

  1. Set up. $\mathcal L\{t^2\}=\displaystyle\int_0^\infty t^2e^{-st}\,dt$.
  2. Choose the parts. Let $u=t^2$, so $du=2t\,dt$, and $dv=e^{-st}dt$, so $v=-\dfrac1se^{-st}$. Differentiating $t^2$ lowers its power, which is what we want.
  3. Apply the formula. $$\int_0^\infty t^2e^{-st}\,dt=\left[-\frac{t^2e^{-st}}{s}\right]_0^\infty+\frac2s\int_0^\infty te^{-st}\,dt.$$
  4. The boundary term is zero. At $t=0$ it is $0$ because of $t^2$. As $t\to\infty$, $t^2e^{-st}\to0$ for $s>0$, because an exponential beats any power.
  5. Recognize what is left. The remaining integral is $\mathcal L\{t\}=\dfrac{1}{s^2}$ (Example 2). So $\mathcal L\{t^2\}=\dfrac2s\cdot\dfrac1{s^2}=\dfrac{2}{s^3}$, for $s>0$.
  6. The pattern. Each time, parts gives $\mathcal L\{t^n\}=\dfrac ns\mathcal L\{t^{n-1}\}$. Repeating down to $\mathcal L\{1\}=\dfrac1s$ gives $\mathcal L\{t^n\}=\dfrac{n!}{s^{n+1}}$.
Example 9A ramp that levels off

Find $\mathcal L\{f(t)\}$ for $f(t)=t$ when $0\le t<1$ and $f(t)=1$ when $t\ge1$.

  1. Split the integral at the break. $$F(s)=\int_0^1te^{-st}\,dt+\int_1^\infty e^{-st}\,dt.$$ Each piece uses the formula for $f$ on that interval.
  2. Second piece. $\displaystyle\int_1^\infty e^{-st}\,dt=\left[-\frac{e^{-st}}{s}\right]_1^\infty=\frac{e^{-s}}{s}$.
  3. First piece by parts. With $u=t$, $dv=e^{-st}dt$: $$\int_0^1te^{-st}\,dt=\left[-\frac{te^{-st}}{s}\right]_0^1+\frac1s\int_0^1e^{-st}\,dt=-\frac{e^{-s}}{s}+\frac{1-e^{-s}}{s^2}.$$ This time the upper limit is $1$, so the boundary term does not vanish.
  4. Add. The $\dfrac{e^{-s}}{s}$ terms cancel: $$F(s)=\frac{1-e^{-s}}{s^2}.$$
  5. Check with Section 4.3. Later you will write $f(t)=t-(t-1)\mathcal U(t-1)$, whose transform is $\dfrac1{s^2}-\dfrac{e^{-s}}{s^2}$. Same answer, much less work.
Example 10Linearity with several pieces

Find $\mathcal L\{3-2e^{4t}+5\sin3t-4t^3\}$.

  1. Split by linearity. $3\mathcal L\{1\}-2\mathcal L\{e^{4t}\}+5\mathcal L\{\sin3t\}-4\mathcal L\{t^3\}$.
  2. Use the table. $\mathcal L\{1\}=\dfrac1s$, $\mathcal L\{e^{4t}\}=\dfrac1{s-4}$, $\mathcal L\{\sin3t\}=\dfrac{3}{s^2+9}$, $\mathcal L\{t^3\}=\dfrac{3!}{s^4}=\dfrac{6}{s^4}$.
  3. Combine. $$\frac3s-\frac{2}{s-4}+\frac{15}{s^2+9}-\frac{24}{s^4},\qquad s>4.$$ The condition $s>4$ comes from the exponential, the fastest-growing term.
Example 11Rewrite first, then transform

Find $\mathcal L\{\cos^2t\}$ and $\mathcal L\{(e^{t}-e^{-t})^2\}$.

  1. Why rewrite? The table has no entry for a square. The trick is to turn products into sums, because linearity works with sums.
  2. $\cos^2t$. The double-angle identity gives $\cos^2t=\dfrac12+\dfrac12\cos2t$. So $$\mathcal L\{\cos^2t\}=\frac{1}{2s}+\frac12\cdot\frac{s}{s^2+4}.$$
  3. $(e^t-e^{-t})^2$. Expand: $e^{2t}-2+e^{-2t}$. Then $$\mathcal L=\frac{1}{s-2}-\frac2s+\frac{1}{s+2},\qquad s>2.$$
  4. Warning. $\mathcal L\{f\cdot g\}$ is not $F(s)G(s)$. Products must be rewritten (or handled with the theorems of 4.3 and 4.4).

Test-day checklist

Mistakes that cost marks
  • Leaving $t$ in the answer. $F(s)$ is a function of $s$ only.
  • Dropping the limit at infinity. Say why the boundary term vanishes: $e^{-st}\to0$ for $s>a$.
  • $\mathcal L\{t^n\}=\dfrac{n!}{s^{n+1}}$, not $\dfrac{n}{s^{n}}$. Check: $\mathcal L\{t\}=\dfrac1{s^2}$.
  • Sine and cosine swapped. $\mathcal L\{\sin kt\}=\dfrac{k}{s^2+k^2}$ has $k$ on top; $\mathcal L\{\cos kt\}=\dfrac{s}{s^2+k^2}$ has $s$ on top.
  • Transforming a product as a product. Expand or use an identity first.
  • Piecewise functions: split the integral at each break and use the right formula on each piece.

Practice set · 25 problems

25 more problems on this section, weighted toward the kinds that show up on Test 2. Each one has an answer and a full step-by-step solution that explains why each step is taken. The numbers are fixed, so you can come back to the same problem.

Chapter 4 · Section 4.2 · on Test 2

Inverse transforms and transforms of derivatives

In this section we take two steps towards solving differential equations. First we learn to go backwards, from $F(s)$ to $f(t)$. Then we find what the transform does to a derivative. Together they give the complete method.

Inverse transforms are pattern matching

There is an integral formula for $\mathcal L^{-1}$, but nobody uses it in this course. Instead you reshape $F(s)$ until every piece matches a line of the table, and then read off $f(t)$. Three kinds of reshaping cover almost everything:

  1. Fix the constants. $\mathcal L\{t^4\}=\dfrac{24}{s^5}$, so $\dfrac1{s^5}$ is $\dfrac1{24}$ of a table entry: multiply and divide by $24$.
  2. Split the numerator. $\dfrac{3s+5}{s^2+7}=3\cdot\dfrac{s}{s^2+7}+5\cdot\dfrac{1}{s^2+7}$: a cosine plus a sine.
  3. Partial fractions. A fraction with a factored denominator breaks into simple pieces, one per factor.

$\mathcal L^{-1}$ is linear too, so each piece can be inverted on its own.

Partial fractions: the three patterns

Factor in the denominatorPieces to writeInverts to
$(s-a)$, distinct$\dfrac{A}{s-a}$$Ae^{at}$
$(s-a)^2$, repeated$\dfrac{A}{s-a}+\dfrac{B}{(s-a)^2}$$Ae^{at}+Bte^{at}$
$s^2+k^2$, irreducible$\dfrac{As+B}{s^2+k^2}$$A\cos kt+\dfrac Bk\sin kt$

For distinct linear factors, the cover-up method is fastest: to find the coefficient of $\dfrac{1}{s-a}$, cover the factor $(s-a)$ in the original fraction and substitute $s=a$ into what is left. It works because multiplying the identity by $(s-a)$ and setting $s=a$ kills every other term.

Why derivatives become multiplication by $s$

Integrate by parts with $u=e^{-st}$ and $dv=f'(t)\,dt$:

$$\mathcal L\{f'\}=\Big[e^{-st}f(t)\Big]_0^\infty+s\int_0^\infty e^{-st}f(t)\,dt=-f(0)+sF(s).$$

Apply it twice for the second derivative: $\mathcal L\{f''\}=s^2F(s)-sf(0)-f'(0)$. This is the whole reason the transform solves IVPs: the derivatives turn into powers of $s$, and the initial conditions drop in automatically. You never solve for $c_1$ and $c_2$.

The IVP recipe

  1. Transform both sides. Replace $\mathcal L\{y\}$ by $Y$, $\mathcal L\{y'\}$ by $sY-y(0)$, $\mathcal L\{y''\}$ by $s^2Y-sy(0)-y'(0)$, and transform the right side with the table.
  2. Collect the $Y$ terms: $(as^2+bs+c)Y=\dots$. The factor in front of $Y$ is the auxiliary polynomial from Chapter 3.
  3. Divide to get $Y(s)$ as one fraction.
  4. Use partial fractions and the table to find $y(t)=\mathcal L^{-1}\{Y\}$.
  5. Check $y(0)$ and, if you have time, $y'(0)$.

The inverse transform

If $F(s)$ is the Laplace transform of $f(t)$, that is $\mathcal{L}\{f(t)\}=F(s)$, then we call $f(t)$ the inverse Laplace transform of $F(s)$ and write

$$f(t)=\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{F(s)\}.$$

For example, since $\mathcal{L}\{1\}=\dfrac1s$, $\mathcal{L}\{t\}=\dfrac{1}{s^2}$ and $\mathcal{L}\{e^{-3t}\}=\dfrac{1}{s+3}$, we have

$$1=\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac1s\right\},\qquad t=\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{s^2}\right\},\qquad e^{-3t}=\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{s+3}\right\}.$$

Reading the table of 4.1 from right to left gives the inverse table:

$F(s)$$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{F(s)\}$
$\dfrac1s$$1$
$\dfrac{n!}{s^{n+1}}$$t^n$
$\dfrac{1}{s-a}$$e^{at}$
$\dfrac{k}{s^2+k^2}$$\sin kt$
$\dfrac{s}{s^2+k^2}$$\cos kt$
$\dfrac{k}{s^2-k^2}$$\sinh kt$
$\dfrac{s}{s^2-k^2}$$\cosh kt$

The inverse transform is also linear:

$$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{\alpha F(s)+\beta G(s)\}=\alpha\,\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{F(s)\}+\beta\,\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{G(s)\}. \tag{1}$$

So the whole job of finding an inverse is: rewrite $F(s)$ as a sum of constants times table entries, making each piece match a table entry exactly.

Example 1Fixing the constants

Evaluate (a) $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{1}{s^5}\right\}$ and (b) $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{1}{s^2+7}\right\}$.

  1. (a) Match the pattern. $\dfrac{n!}{s^{n+1}}$ with $n+1=5$ means $n=4$. The table entry needs $4!=24$ on top, but we only have $1$.
  2. (a) Multiply and divide by $24$. $\dfrac{1}{s^5}=\dfrac{1}{24}\cdot\dfrac{4!}{s^5}$. Now linearity lets the $\dfrac{1}{24}$ come out: $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{1}{s^5}\right\}=\dfrac{1}{24}t^4$.
  3. (b) Match the pattern. $s^2+7=s^2+k^2$ with $k=\sqrt7$. There is no $s$ on top, so this is the sine entry, which needs $k=\sqrt7$ on top.
  4. (b) Multiply and divide by $\sqrt7$. $\dfrac{1}{s^2+7}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt7}\cdot\dfrac{\sqrt7}{s^2+7}$, so $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{1}{s^2+7}\right\}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt7}\sin\sqrt7\,t$.
Example 2Splitting the numerator
