Evaluate $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{-2s+6}{s^2+4}\right\}$.

  1. Divide each term of the numerator by the denominator. $\dfrac{-2s+6}{s^2+4}=\dfrac{-2s}{s^2+4}+\dfrac{6}{s^2+4}$. One piece has $s$ on top (cosine), the other a constant (sine).
  2. Fix the constants. Here $k=2$. The sine entry needs $2$ on top, so write $\dfrac{6}{s^2+4}=3\cdot\dfrac{2}{s^2+4}$. The cosine piece is already $-2\cdot\dfrac{s}{s^2+4}$.
  3. Invert each piece. $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{-2s+6}{s^2+4}\right\}=-2\cos2t+3\sin2t$.

Partial fractions

When the denominator factors, the inverse is found by splitting $F(s)$ into partial fractions. This is the single most used algebra skill in Chapter 4. Three cases come up:

  • Distinct linear factors $(s-a)(s-b)$: write $\dfrac{A}{s-a}+\dfrac{B}{s-b}$.
  • Repeated linear factors $(s-a)^2$: write $\dfrac{A}{s-a}+\dfrac{B}{(s-a)^2}$ (handled in 4.3).
  • Irreducible quadratic $s^2+k^2$: write $\dfrac{Bs+C}{s^2+k^2}$.

For distinct linear factors there is a shortcut, often called the cover-up method. To find the coefficient over $(s-a)$, cover up $(s-a)$ in the original fraction and put $s=a$ in what is left. It works because multiplying the partial-fraction equation by $(s-a)$ and then setting $s=a$ kills every other term.

Example 3Distinct linear factors

Evaluate $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{s^2+6s+9}{(s-1)(s-2)(s+4)}\right\}$.

  1. Set up the partial fractions. Three distinct linear factors, so $\dfrac{s^2+6s+9}{(s-1)(s-2)(s+4)}=\dfrac{A}{s-1}+\dfrac{B}{s-2}+\dfrac{C}{s+4}$.
  2. Clear denominators. $s^2+6s+9=A(s-2)(s+4)+B(s-1)(s+4)+C(s-1)(s-2)$. This holds for every $s$, so we may choose convenient values.
  3. Put $s=1$ (this kills the $B$ and $C$ terms). $1+6+9=A(-1)(5)$, so $16=-5A$ and $A=-\dfrac{16}{5}$.
  4. Put $s=2$. $4+12+9=B(1)(6)$, so $25=6B$ and $B=\dfrac{25}{6}$.
  5. Put $s=-4$. $16-24+9=C(-5)(-6)$, so $1=30C$ and $C=\dfrac{1}{30}$.
  6. Invert term by term using $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{1}{s-a}\right\}=e^{at}$: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{F\}=-\frac{16}{5}e^{t}+\frac{25}{6}e^{2t}+\frac{1}{30}e^{-4t}.$$
  7. A quick check. At $t=0$ the answer is $-\dfrac{16}{5}+\dfrac{25}{6}+\dfrac{1}{30}=\dfrac{-96+125+1}{30}=1$. We will see in Example 6 why this matters.
Not every $F(s)$ needs partial fractions

Before splitting, check whether the fraction is already a table entry after fixing constants. Partial fractions are needed only when the denominator is a product of factors.

Transforms of derivatives

Our goal is to transform a differential equation, so we need $\mathcal{L}\{f'(t)\}$ in terms of $\mathcal{L}\{f(t)\}$. Integrate by parts with $u=e^{-st}$ and $dv=f'(t)\,dt$:

$$\begin{aligned}\mathcal{L}\{f'(t)\}&=\int_0^{\infty}e^{-st}f'(t)\,dt\\&=\Big[e^{-st}f(t)\Big]_0^{\infty}+s\int_0^{\infty}e^{-st}f(t)\,dt\\&=-f(0)+s\mathcal{L}\{f(t)\}.\end{aligned}$$

The boundary term at $\infty$ is zero because $f$ is of exponential order and $s$ is large enough. At $0$ it gives $-f(0)$. So

$$\mathcal{L}\{f'(t)\}=sF(s)-f(0). \tag{2}$$

Apply (2) to $f'$ instead of $f$ to get the second derivative:

$$\begin{aligned}\mathcal{L}\{f''(t)\}&=s\mathcal{L}\{f'(t)\}-f'(0)\\&=s\big[sF(s)-f(0)\big]-f'(0)\\&=s^2F(s)-sf(0)-f'(0).\end{aligned} \tag{3}$$
Theorem 4.2.1 · Transform of a derivative

If $f,f',\dots,f^{(n-1)}$ are continuous on $[0,\infty)$ and of exponential order, and $f^{(n)}$ is piecewise continuous, then

$$\mathcal{L}\{f^{(n)}(t)\}=s^nF(s)-s^{n-1}f(0)-s^{n-2}f'(0)-\cdots-f^{(n-1)}(0).$$

The ones you need constantly:

$$\mathcal{L}\{y'\}=sY-y(0),\qquad \mathcal{L}\{y''\}=s^2Y-sy(0)-y'(0).$$

This is the whole point. Differentiation in $t$ becomes multiplication by $s$, and the initial conditions are built in automatically. We never need to find a general solution and then solve for constants.

Solving linear initial-value problems

Consider a linear equation with constant coefficients, such as $ay''+by'+cy=g(t)$ with $y(0)=y_0$ and $y'(0)=y_1$. Transforming both sides with linearity and (3) gives

$$a\big[s^2Y-sy_0-y_1\big]+b\big[sY-y_0\big]+cY=G(s).$$

This is an algebraic equation for $Y(s)$. Collecting the $Y$ terms,

$$(as^2+bs+c)\,Y(s)=a(sy_0+y_1)+by_0+G(s).$$

Notice that the polynomial multiplying $Y$ is exactly the auxiliary polynomial from Section 3.3. Solve for $Y$, then take the inverse transform.

The method in four steps
  1. Transform both sides of the equation, using Theorem 4.2.1 and the initial conditions.
  2. Solve the resulting algebraic equation for $Y(s)$.
  3. Decompose $Y(s)$ into partial fractions (or other table-ready pieces).
  4. Invert term by term to get $y(t)=\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{Y(s)\}$.
Example 4A first-order IVP

Use the Laplace transform to solve $\dfrac{dy}{dt}+3y=13\sin2t$, $y(0)=6$.

  1. Transform both sides. $\mathcal{L}\{y'\}+3\mathcal{L}\{y\}=13\mathcal{L}\{\sin2t\}$.
  2. Use (2) and the table. $\mathcal{L}\{y'\}=sY-y(0)=sY-6$, and $\mathcal{L}\{\sin2t\}=\dfrac{2}{s^2+4}$. So $sY-6+3Y=\dfrac{26}{s^2+4}$.
  3. Solve for $Y$. $(s+3)Y=6+\dfrac{26}{s^2+4}$, so $Y(s)=\dfrac{6}{s+3}+\dfrac{26}{(s+3)(s^2+4)}$.
  4. Set up partial fractions for the second term. The factor $s^2+4$ is irreducible, so $\dfrac{26}{(s+3)(s^2+4)}=\dfrac{A}{s+3}+\dfrac{Bs+C}{s^2+4}$, which means $26=A(s^2+4)+(Bs+C)(s+3)$.
  5. Find $A$ with $s=-3$. $26=A(9+4)=13A$, so $A=2$.
  6. Find $B$ and $C$ by matching coefficients. The $s^2$ terms: $0=A+B$, so $B=-2$. The constant terms: $26=4A+3C=8+3C$, so $C=6$. (Check the $s$ terms: $0=3B+C=-6+6$. Good.)
  7. Put $Y$ together. $Y(s)=\dfrac{6}{s+3}+\dfrac{2}{s+3}+\dfrac{-2s+6}{s^2+4}=\dfrac{8}{s+3}-2\cdot\dfrac{s}{s^2+4}+3\cdot\dfrac{2}{s^2+4}$.
  8. Invert (the last two pieces are Example 2): $$y(t)=8e^{-3t}-2\cos2t+3\sin2t.$$
  9. Check the initial condition. $y(0)=8-2+0=6$. Correct.
Example 5A second-order IVP

Solve $y''-3y'+2y=e^{-4t}$, $y(0)=1$, $y'(0)=5$.

  1. Transform each term. $\mathcal{L}\{y''\}=s^2Y-sy(0)-y'(0)=s^2Y-s-5$. $\mathcal{L}\{y'\}=sY-y(0)=sY-1$. $\mathcal{L}\{e^{-4t}\}=\dfrac{1}{s+4}$.
  2. Substitute. $s^2Y-s-5-3(sY-1)+2Y=\dfrac{1}{s+4}$.
  3. Collect the $Y$ terms on the left and the rest on the right. $(s^2-3s+2)Y=s+2+\dfrac{1}{s+4}$. (From $-s-5+3=-s-2$, moved across.)
  4. Combine the right side into one fraction. $s+2+\dfrac{1}{s+4}=\dfrac{(s+2)(s+4)+1}{s+4}=\dfrac{s^2+6s+9}{s+4}$.
  5. Factor and solve for $Y$. $s^2-3s+2=(s-1)(s-2)$, so $Y(s)=\dfrac{s^2+6s+9}{(s-1)(s-2)(s+4)}$.
  6. This is Example 3. We already found the partial fractions, so $$y(t)=-\frac{16}{5}e^{t}+\frac{25}{6}e^{2t}+\frac{1}{30}e^{-4t}.$$
  7. Check. Example 3 showed $y(0)=1$. Also $y'(0)=-\dfrac{16}{5}+\dfrac{25}{3}-\dfrac{4}{30}=\dfrac{-96+250-4}{30}=5$. Both conditions hold.
Zero-input and zero-state parts

In $(as^2+bs+c)Y=a(sy_0+y_1)+by_0+G(s)$ the solution splits naturally into two pieces. The part from the initial conditions alone (set $G=0$) is the zero-input response. The part from the forcing alone (set $y_0=y_1=0$) is the zero-state response. In Example 5 these would be $\dfrac{s+2}{(s-1)(s-2)}$ and $\dfrac{1}{(s-1)(s-2)(s+4)}$. Engineers use this split all the time when studying circuits and controls.

Summary
  • $\mathcal{L}^{-1}$ is linear. Make each piece match a table entry exactly by multiplying and dividing by constants.
  • Partial fractions: cover-up for distinct linear factors, $\frac{Bs+C}{s^2+k^2}$ for irreducible quadratics.
  • $\mathcal{L}\{y'\}=sY-y(0)$ and $\mathcal{L}\{y''\}=s^2Y-sy(0)-y'(0)$.
  • Transform, solve for $Y$, decompose, invert. Then check the initial conditions.

50 numbered problems with full solutions: Laplace transforms page.

More worked examples

Example 6Reshaping before inverting

Find (a) $\mathcal L^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{1}{s^5}\right\}$ and (b) $\mathcal L^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{3s+5}{s^2+7}\right\}$.

  1. (a) Which entry? $\dfrac{n!}{s^{n+1}}$ with $n+1=5$, so $n=4$ and the entry is $\dfrac{24}{s^5}$.
  2. (a) Fix the constant. $\dfrac{1}{s^5}=\dfrac{1}{24}\cdot\dfrac{24}{s^5}$, so $\mathcal L^{-1}=\dfrac{t^4}{24}$.
  3. (b) Split. $\dfrac{3s+5}{s^2+7}=3\cdot\dfrac{s}{s^2+7}+5\cdot\dfrac{1}{s^2+7}$. Here $k^2=7$, so $k=\sqrt7$.
  4. (b) Cosine part. $\dfrac{s}{s^2+7}$ is exactly $\mathcal L\{\cos\sqrt7t\}$.
  5. (b) Sine part. $\mathcal L\{\sin\sqrt7t\}=\dfrac{\sqrt7}{s^2+7}$, so $\dfrac{1}{s^2+7}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt7}\cdot\dfrac{\sqrt7}{s^2+7}$.
  6. (b) Answer. $3\cos\sqrt7t+\dfrac{5}{\sqrt7}\sin\sqrt7t$.
Example 7Partial fractions with a repeated factor

Find $\mathcal L^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{s+1}{s^2(s+2)}\right\}$.

  1. Set up. $s^2$ is a repeated factor, so it needs two terms: $$\frac{s+1}{s^2(s+2)}=\frac As+\frac{B}{s^2}+\frac{C}{s+2}.$$
  2. Clear denominators. $s+1=As(s+2)+B(s+2)+Cs^2$.
  3. $s=0$. $1=2B$, so $B=\dfrac12$.
  4. $s=-2$. $-1=4C$, so $C=-\dfrac14$.
  5. Compare $s^2$ coefficients. No $s=$ value isolates $A$, so match a power: the left side has no $s^2$, the right has $A+C$. So $A=-C=\dfrac14$.
  6. Invert. $$\mathcal L^{-1}=\frac14+\frac12t-\frac14e^{-2t}.$$
  7. Check. At $t=0$ this gives $0$. That agrees with the rule that $f(0)=\displaystyle\lim_{s\to\infty}sF(s)$, which here is $\displaystyle\lim\frac{s+1}{s(s+2)}=0$.
Example 8An irreducible quadratic

Find $\mathcal L^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{1}{(s+1)(s^2+1)}\right\}$.

  1. Set up. $s^2+1$ does not factor over the reals, so its numerator is linear: $$\frac{1}{(s+1)(s^2+1)}=\frac{A}{s+1}+\frac{Bs+C}{s^2+1}.$$
  2. Cover-up for $A$. Cover $(s+1)$ and set $s=-1$: $A=\dfrac{1}{1+1}=\dfrac12$.
  3. Clear and match. $1=A(s^2+1)+(Bs+C)(s+1)$. The $s^2$ terms: $0=A+B$, so $B=-\dfrac12$. The constants: $1=A+C$, so $C=\dfrac12$.
  4. Invert each piece. $$\frac12e^{-t}-\frac12\cos t+\frac12\sin t.$$
Example 9A complete IVP, every step explained

Solve $y''+3y'+2y=6$, $y(0)=0$, $y'(0)=0$.

  1. Transform the left side. With both initial values zero: $\mathcal L\{y''\}=s^2Y$, $\mathcal L\{y'\}=sY$. So the left side becomes $(s^2+3s+2)Y$.
  2. Transform the right side. $\mathcal L\{6\}=\dfrac6s$.
  3. Solve for $Y$. $(s^2+3s+2)Y=\dfrac6s$. Factor $s^2+3s+2=(s+1)(s+2)$ and divide: $$Y=\frac{6}{s(s+1)(s+2)}.$$
  4. Partial fractions by cover-up. $s=0$: $\dfrac{6}{1\cdot2}=3$. $s=-1$: $\dfrac{6}{(-1)(1)}=-6$. $s=-2$: $\dfrac{6}{(-2)(-1)}=3$. So $$Y=\frac3s-\frac{6}{s+1}+\frac{3}{s+2}.$$
  5. Invert. $y=3-6e^{-t}+3e^{-2t}$.
  6. Check. $y(0)=3-6+3=0$ and $y'(0)=6-6=0$. As $t\to\infty$, $y\to3$, the equilibrium where $2y=6$. Everything agrees.
Example 10Nonzero initial conditions

Solve $y''-y=0$, $y(0)=1$, $y'(0)=3$.

  1. Transform. $\mathcal L\{y''\}=s^2Y-sy(0)-y'(0)=s^2Y-s-3$. So $s^2Y-s-3-Y=0$.
  2. Solve. $(s^2-1)Y=s+3$, so $Y=\dfrac{s+3}{(s-1)(s+1)}$. The initial conditions are now in the numerator.
  3. Cover-up. $s=1$: $\dfrac{4}{2}=2$. $s=-1$: $\dfrac{2}{-2}=-1$. So $Y=\dfrac{2}{s-1}-\dfrac{1}{s+1}$.
  4. Answer. $y=2e^t-e^{-t}$. Check: $y(0)=1$ and $y'(0)=2+1=3$.

Test-day checklist

Mistakes that cost marks
  • Sign errors in $\mathcal L\{y''\}$. It is $s^2Y-sy(0)-y'(0)$: both initial terms are subtracted.
  • Forgetting to transform the right side, or transforming a constant $c$ as $c$ instead of $\dfrac cs$.
  • Wrong partial-fraction form. A repeated factor needs one term per power; a quadratic factor needs $As+B$ on top.
  • Leaving $\dfrac{1}{s^2+k^2}$ as $\sin kt$. It is $\dfrac1k\sin kt$.
  • Not checking $y(0)$. It takes ten seconds and catches most errors.

Practice set · 25 problems

25 more problems on this section, weighted toward the kinds that show up on Test 2. Each one has an answer and a full step-by-step solution that explains why each step is taken. The numbers are fixed, so you can come back to the same problem.

Chapter 4 · Section 4.3 · on Test 2

Translation theorems

It is not practical to compute every transform from the definition. Instead we use theorems that build new transforms out of old ones. This section gives two of the most useful. The first handles functions multiplied by $e^{at}$. The second handles functions that switch on at a later time, which is exactly what we need for forces and voltages that are turned on and off.

This section has two theorems that look alike and are easy to confuse. The way to keep them apart is to ask where the shift happens.

First translation theoremSecond translation theorem
In $t$multiply by $e^{at}$delay by $a$ and switch on at $t=a$
In $s$shift: $s\to s-a$multiply by $e^{-as}$
Formula$\mathcal L\{e^{at}f(t)\}=F(s-a)$$\mathcal L\{f(t-a)\mathcal U(t-a)\}=e^{-as}F(s)$
Recognize it byan $e^{at}$ factor in $t$; $(s-a)$ or a completed square in $s$a step function in $t$; an $e^{-as}$ factor in $s$

The two theorems are mirror images: an exponential in one world is a shift in the other.

The first theorem: why it is true

$\mathcal L\{e^{at}f(t)\}=\displaystyle\int_0^\infty e^{-st}e^{at}f(t)\,dt=\int_0^\infty e^{-(s-a)t}f(t)\,dt$. That is the definition of $F$ with $s$ replaced by $s-a$. So you transform $f$ as if the $e^{at}$ were not there, then replace every $s$ by $s-a$.

Going backwards, a denominator like $s^2+4s+13$ that does not factor is a signal: complete the square, $(s+2)^2+9$, and look for $s+2$ in the numerator. Then the answer has a factor $e^{-2t}$.

The unit step: a switch

$\mathcal U(t-a)$ is $0$ before $t=a$ and $1$ after. Multiplying by it switches a function on at time $a$. Differences of steps switch things on and off:

  • $f(t)\mathcal U(t-a)$ is $f$ turned on at $a$.
  • $f(t)\big[\mathcal U(t-a)-\mathcal U(t-b)\big]$ is $f$ on the window $a\le t<b$ and $0$ elsewhere.
  • A function equal to $g$ before $a$ and $h$ after is $g+(h-g)\mathcal U(t-a)$: start with $g$, and at time $a$ add the change needed to turn it into $h$.

The second theorem: the one catch

The theorem transforms $f(t-a)\mathcal U(t-a)$, where the function is shifted by the same $a$ as the step. Real problems give something like $t^2\mathcal U(t-1)$, where $t^2$ is not written in terms of $t-1$. There are two ways out:

  1. Rewrite: $t^2=\big((t-1)+1\big)^2=(t-1)^2+2(t-1)+1$, then apply the theorem to each term.
  2. Use the alternative form $\mathcal L\{g(t)\mathcal U(t-a)\}=e^{-as}\mathcal L\{g(t+a)\}$. Here $g(t+1)=(t+1)^2=t^2+2t+1$, so the transform is $e^{-s}\left(\dfrac2{s^3}+\dfrac2{s^2}+\dfrac1s\right)$.

Going backwards, an $e^{-as}$ factor means: invert the rest as usual to get $f(t)$, then replace $t$ by $t-a$ and multiply by $\mathcal U(t-a)$. Forgetting either half loses the marks.

Translation on the $s$-axis

Suppose we know $F(s)=\mathcal{L}\{f(t)\}$ and want the transform of $e^{at}f(t)$. From the definition,

$$\mathcal{L}\{e^{at}f(t)\}=\int_0^{\infty}e^{-st}e^{at}f(t)\,dt=\int_0^{\infty}e^{-(s-a)t}f(t)\,dt.$$

The last integral is the definition of $F$, but with $s-a$ where $s$ used to be. So it equals $F(s-a)$.

Theorem 4.3.1 · First translation theorem $$\mathcal{L}\{e^{at}f(t)\}=F(s-a).$$

Multiplying by $e^{at}$ in the $t$-world shifts the graph of $F$ by $a$ units along the $s$-axis. It is often written $\mathcal{L}\{e^{at}f(t)\}=\mathcal{L}\{f(t)\}\big|_{s\to s-a}$: find $F(s)$ first, then replace every $s$ by $s-a$.

Example 1Using the first translation theorem

Evaluate (a) $\mathcal{L}\{e^{5t}t^3\}$ and (b) $\mathcal{L}\{e^{-2t}\cos4t\}$.

  1. (a) Ignore the exponential and transform the rest. $\mathcal{L}\{t^3\}=\dfrac{3!}{s^4}=\dfrac{6}{s^4}$.
  2. (a) Shift. The exponential is $e^{5t}$, so $a=5$. Replace $s$ by $s-5$: $\mathcal{L}\{e^{5t}t^3\}=\dfrac{6}{(s-5)^4}$.
  3. (b) Transform without the exponential. $\mathcal{L}\{\cos4t\}=\dfrac{s}{s^2+16}$.
  4. (b) Shift. Here $a=-2$, so $s-a=s+2$. Replace every $s$, including the one on top: $\mathcal{L}\{e^{-2t}\cos4t\}=\dfrac{s+2}{(s+2)^2+16}$.

The inverse form

Read backwards, the theorem says

$$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{F(s-a)\}=e^{at}f(t).$$

To use it, you must see a function of $s-a$. The usual signs are a repeated linear factor $(s-a)^n$ or a quadratic that does not factor, which we rewrite by completing the square as $(s-a)^2+k^2$. Then the numerator must also be written in terms of $s-a$.

Example 2Partial fractions with a repeated factor

Evaluate $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{2s+5}{(s-3)^2}\right\}$.

  1. Set up partial fractions for a repeated factor. $\dfrac{2s+5}{(s-3)^2}=\dfrac{A}{s-3}+\dfrac{B}{(s-3)^2}$, so $2s+5=A(s-3)+B$.
  2. Solve. Put $s=3$: $11=B$. Compare the $s$ terms: $A=2$.
  3. (Faster way.) Write the numerator in terms of $s-3$ directly: $2s+5=2(s-3)+6+5=2(s-3)+11$. Dividing by $(s-3)^2$ gives the same two pieces.
  4. So $\dfrac{2s+5}{(s-3)^2}=\dfrac{2}{s-3}+\dfrac{11}{(s-3)^2}$.
  5. Invert each piece. $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{1}{s-3}\right\}=e^{3t}$. For the second, $\dfrac{1}{s^2}$ is the transform of $t$, so $\dfrac{1}{(s-3)^2}$ is that shifted by $3$, giving $te^{3t}$.
  6. Answer. $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{2s+5}{(s-3)^2}\right\}=2e^{3t}+11te^{3t}$.
Example 3Completing the square

Evaluate $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{\dfrac{s}{2}+\dfrac{5}{3}}{s^2+4s+6}\right\}$.

  1. Check whether the denominator factors. Its discriminant is $16-24<0$, so it does not. Complete the square instead: $s^2+4s+6=(s^2+4s+4)+2=(s+2)^2+2$.
  2. So $s-a=s+2$, $a=-2$, and $k^2=2$, $k=\sqrt2$. Now the numerator must be written using $s+2$.
  3. Rewrite the numerator. $\dfrac{s}{2}+\dfrac53=\dfrac12(s+2)-1+\dfrac53=\dfrac12(s+2)+\dfrac23$.
  4. Split. $\dfrac{\dfrac12(s+2)+\dfrac23}{(s+2)^2+2}=\dfrac12\cdot\dfrac{s+2}{(s+2)^2+2}+\dfrac23\cdot\dfrac{1}{(s+2)^2+2}$.
  5. Fix the sine constant. The sine entry needs $k=\sqrt2$ on top: $\dfrac23\cdot\dfrac{1}{(s+2)^2+2}=\dfrac{2}{3\sqrt2}\cdot\dfrac{\sqrt2}{(s+2)^2+2}=\dfrac{\sqrt2}{3}\cdot\dfrac{\sqrt2}{(s+2)^2+2}$.
  6. Invert with the shift. Without the shift these would be $\cos\sqrt2\,t$ and $\sin\sqrt2\,t$. The shift by $-2$ multiplies each by $e^{-2t}$: $$\frac12e^{-2t}\cos\sqrt2\,t+\frac{\sqrt2}{3}e^{-2t}\sin\sqrt2\,t.$$
Example 4An IVP with a repeated root

Solve $y''-6y'+9y=t^2e^{3t}$, $y(0)=2$, $y'(0)=17$.

  1. Transform the left side. $s^2Y-2s-17-6(sY-2)+9Y=(s^2-6s+9)Y-2s-5$.
  2. Transform the right side with the first translation theorem. $\mathcal{L}\{t^2\}=\dfrac{2}{s^3}$, shifted by $3$: $\mathcal{L}\{t^2e^{3t}\}=\dfrac{2}{(s-3)^3}$.
  3. Solve for $Y$. $(s-3)^2Y=2s+5+\dfrac{2}{(s-3)^3}$, so $Y=\dfrac{2s+5}{(s-3)^2}+\dfrac{2}{(s-3)^5}$.
  4. The first term is Example 2. It inverts to $2e^{3t}+11te^{3t}$.
  5. The second term. $\dfrac{4!}{s^5}$ is the transform of $t^4$. We have $2$ on top, so write $\dfrac{2}{(s-3)^5}=\dfrac{2}{24}\cdot\dfrac{4!}{(s-3)^5}=\dfrac{1}{12}\cdot\dfrac{4!}{(s-3)^5}$. Its inverse is $\dfrac{1}{12}t^4e^{3t}$.
  6. Answer. $$y(t)=2e^{3t}+11te^{3t}+\frac{1}{12}t^4e^{3t}.$$
  7. Check. $y(0)=2$. Differentiating, $y'(0)=6+11+0=17$. Both hold.
Example 5An IVP with an irreducible quadratic

Solve $y''+4y'+6y=1+e^{-t}$, $y(0)=0$, $y'(0)=0$.

  1. Transform. With zero initial conditions, $\mathcal{L}\{y''\}=s^2Y$ and $\mathcal{L}\{y'\}=sY$. So $(s^2+4s+6)Y=\dfrac1s+\dfrac{1}{s+1}$.
  2. Combine the right side. $\dfrac1s+\dfrac{1}{s+1}=\dfrac{2s+1}{s(s+1)}$, so $Y=\dfrac{2s+1}{s(s+1)(s^2+4s+6)}$.
  3. Set up partial fractions. $Y=\dfrac{A}{s}+\dfrac{B}{s+1}+\dfrac{Cs+D}{s^2+4s+6}$.
  4. Cover-up for $A$ and $B$. At $s=0$: $A=\dfrac{1}{(1)(6)}=\dfrac16$. At $s=-1$: $B=\dfrac{-1}{(-1)(1-4+6)}=\dfrac{-1}{-3}=\dfrac13$.
  5. Match coefficients for $C$ and $D$. Clearing denominators, the $s^3$ terms give $0=A+B+C$, so $C=-\dfrac12$. The $s$ terms give $2=10A+6B+D=\dfrac53+2+D$, so $D=-\dfrac53$.
  6. So $Y=\dfrac{1/6}{s}+\dfrac{1/3}{s+1}-\dfrac{\dfrac{s}{2}+\dfrac53}{s^2+4s+6}$. The last fraction is exactly Example 3.
  7. Answer. $$y(t)=\frac16+\frac13e^{-t}-\frac12e^{-2t}\cos\sqrt2\,t-\frac{\sqrt2}{3}e^{-2t}\sin\sqrt2\,t.$$
  8. Check. $y(0)=\dfrac16+\dfrac13-\dfrac12=0$. Good.

The unit step function

In engineering, forces and voltages are often switched on or off at specific times. A voltage that is $0$ until $t=a$ and then $1$ afterwards is described by the unit step function.

Definition 4.3.1 · Unit step function $$\mathcal{U}(t-a)=\begin{cases}0,&0\le t<a\\ 1,&t\ge a.\end{cases}$$

Multiplying a function by $\mathcal{U}(t-a)$ "turns it on" at $t=a$: the product is $0$ before $a$ and equal to the function after. Multiplying by $1-\mathcal{U}(t-a)$ turns it off at $a$. These two facts let us write any piecewise function as a single formula:

$$f(t)=\begin{cases}g(t),&0\le t<a\\ h(t),&t\ge a\end{cases}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad f(t)=g(t)-g(t)\mathcal{U}(t-a)+h(t)\mathcal{U}(t-a).$$ $$f(t)=\begin{cases}0,&0\le t<a\\ g(t),&a\le t<b\\ 0,&t\ge b\end{cases}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad f(t)=g(t)\big[\mathcal{U}(t-a)-\mathcal{U}(t-b)\big].$$

A good way to think about it: at each break point, add the new formula and subtract the old one, both multiplied by the step at that point.

Interactive · building a piecewise function from steps
Example 6Writing piecewise functions with steps

(a) Write $f(t)=\begin{cases}2,&0\le t<2\\ -1,&2\le t<3\\ 0,&t\ge3\end{cases}$ with unit steps. (b) Do the same for $f(t)=\begin{cases}20t,&0\le t<5\\ 0,&t\ge5.\end{cases}$

  1. (a) Start with the first formula. $f(t)=2$ to begin with.
  2. (a) At $t=2$ the value goes from $2$ to $-1$, a change of $-3$. Add $-3\,\mathcal{U}(t-2)$.
  3. (a) At $t=3$ it goes from $-1$ to $0$, a change of $+1$. Add $\mathcal{U}(t-3)$.
  4. (a) Result. $f(t)=2-3\,\mathcal{U}(t-2)+\mathcal{U}(t-3)$. Check at $t=2.5$: $2-3+0=-1$. At $t=4$: $2-3+1=0$.
  5. (b) Turn $20t$ off at $t=5$. Subtract the old formula there: $f(t)=20t-20t\,\mathcal{U}(t-5)$.

Translation on the $t$-axis

The function $f(t-a)\,\mathcal{U}(t-a)$ is the graph of $f$ shifted $a$ units to the right, with nothing before $t=a$. Its transform is

$$\mathcal{L}\{f(t-a)\mathcal{U}(t-a)\}=\int_a^{\infty}e^{-st}f(t-a)\,dt.$$

(The step kills everything below $a$.) Substitute $v=t-a$, so $t=v+a$ and $dt=dv$, and the limits become $0$ and $\infty$:

$$\int_0^{\infty}e^{-s(v+a)}f(v)\,dv=e^{-as}\int_0^{\infty}e^{-sv}f(v)\,dv=e^{-as}F(s).$$
Theorem 4.3.2 · Second translation theorem $$\mathcal{L}\{f(t-a)\,\mathcal{U}(t-a)\}=e^{-as}F(s),\qquad \mathcal{L}^{-1}\{e^{-as}F(s)\}=f(t-a)\,\mathcal{U}(t-a).$$

A delay of $a$ in time multiplies the transform by $e^{-as}$. With $f=1$ this gives $\mathcal{L}\{\mathcal{U}(t-a)\}=\dfrac{e^{-as}}{s}$, which is what we found by hand in Example 7 of 4.1.

The shift must match

The theorem needs the same shift inside $f$ as in the step: $(t-2)^3\,\mathcal{U}(t-2)$ is fine, but $t^3\,\mathcal{U}(t-2)$ is not in that form yet. For the second kind, use the alternative form below.

Example 7Transform and inverse with a delay

(a) Find $\mathcal{L}\{(t-2)^3\,\mathcal{U}(t-2)\}$. (b) Find $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{e^{-2s}}{s-4}\right\}$. (c) Find $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{s\,e^{-\pi s/2}}{s^2+9}\right\}$.

  1. (a) This is $f(t-2)\,\mathcal{U}(t-2)$ with $f(t)=t^3$, $F(s)=\dfrac{6}{s^4}$ and $a=2$. So the transform is $\dfrac{6e^{-2s}}{s^4}$.
  2. (b) Separate the exponential. $e^{-2s}F(s)$ with $F(s)=\dfrac{1}{s-4}$, so $f(t)=e^{4t}$ and $a=2$.
  3. (b) Shift and switch on. $\mathcal{L}^{-1}=f(t-2)\,\mathcal{U}(t-2)=e^{4(t-2)}\,\mathcal{U}(t-2)$.
  4. (c) Separate the exponential. $a=\dfrac{\pi}{2}$ and $F(s)=\dfrac{s}{s^2+9}$, so $f(t)=\cos3t$.
  5. (c) Shift. $\mathcal{L}^{-1}=\cos3\left(t-\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)\mathcal{U}\left(t-\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)$.
  6. (c) Simplify with the addition formula. $\cos\left(3t-\dfrac{3\pi}{2}\right)=\cos3t\cos\dfrac{3\pi}{2}+\sin3t\sin\dfrac{3\pi}{2}=0-\sin3t$. So the answer is $-\sin3t\;\mathcal{U}\left(t-\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)$.

Alternative form of the second translation theorem

Often we need $\mathcal{L}\{g(t)\,\mathcal{U}(t-a)\}$, where $g$ is not written in terms of $t-a$. Using the same substitution $v=t-a$ as before,

$$\mathcal{L}\{g(t)\,\mathcal{U}(t-a)\}=\int_a^{\infty}e^{-st}g(t)\,dt=e^{-as}\int_0^{\infty}e^{-sv}g(v+a)\,dv.$$
Alternative form $$\mathcal{L}\{g(t)\,\mathcal{U}(t-a)\}=e^{-as}\,\mathcal{L}\{g(t+a)\}.$$

Replace $t$ by $t+a$ inside $g$, transform that, and multiply by $e^{-as}$.

Example 8Using the alternative form

(a) Evaluate $\mathcal{L}\{\cos t\;\mathcal{U}(t-\pi)\}$. (b) Transform $f(t)=20t-20t\,\mathcal{U}(t-5)$ from Example 6.

  1. (a) Shift the argument. $g(t)=\cos t$ and $a=\pi$, so $g(t+\pi)=\cos(t+\pi)=-\cos t$.
  2. (a) Transform and multiply by $e^{-\pi s}$. $\mathcal{L}\{\cos t\;\mathcal{U}(t-\pi)\}=e^{-\pi s}\,\mathcal{L}\{-\cos t\}=-\dfrac{s\,e^{-\pi s}}{s^2+1}$.
  3. (b) The first term is $\mathcal{L}\{20t\}=\dfrac{20}{s^2}$.
  4. (b) The second term. $g(t)=20t$, $a=5$, so $g(t+5)=20t+100$ and $\mathcal{L}\{g(t+5)\}=\dfrac{20}{s^2}+\dfrac{100}{s}$.
  5. (b) Answer. $\mathcal{L}\{f\}=\dfrac{20}{s^2}-e^{-5s}\left(\dfrac{20}{s^2}+\dfrac{100}{s}\right)$.
Example 9An IVP with a force switched on

Solve $y'+3y=f(t)$, $y(0)=5$, where $f(t)=\begin{cases}0,&0\le t<\pi\\ 3\cos t,&t\ge\pi.\end{cases}$

  1. Write $f$ with a step. $f(t)=3\cos t\;\mathcal{U}(t-\pi)$.
  2. Transform it with Example 8(a). $\mathcal{L}\{f\}=-\dfrac{3s\,e^{-\pi s}}{s^2+1}$.
  3. Transform the equation. $sY-5+3Y=-\dfrac{3s\,e^{-\pi s}}{s^2+1}$, so $Y=\dfrac{5}{s+3}-3e^{-\pi s}\cdot\dfrac{s}{(s+3)(s^2+1)}$.
  4. Partial fractions, leaving $e^{-\pi s}$ aside. $\dfrac{s}{(s+3)(s^2+1)}=\dfrac{A}{s+3}+\dfrac{Bs+C}{s^2+1}$. At $s=-3$: $-3=10A$, so $A=-\dfrac{3}{10}$. The $s^2$ terms: $0=A+B$, so $B=\dfrac{3}{10}$. Constants: $0=A+3C$, so $C=\dfrac{1}{10}$.
  5. Multiply by $-3$. $-3\cdot\dfrac{s}{(s+3)(s^2+1)}=\dfrac{9/10}{s+3}-\dfrac{9}{10}\cdot\dfrac{s}{s^2+1}-\dfrac{3}{10}\cdot\dfrac{1}{s^2+1}$.
  6. Invert without the exponential. That gives $\dfrac{9}{10}e^{-3t}-\dfrac{9}{10}\cos t-\dfrac{3}{10}\sin t$.
  7. Apply the second translation theorem. The factor $e^{-\pi s}$ means replace $t$ by $t-\pi$ and multiply by $\mathcal{U}(t-\pi)$. Use $\cos(t-\pi)=-\cos t$ and $\sin(t-\pi)=-\sin t$.
  8. Answer. $$y(t)=5e^{-3t}+\left[\frac{9}{10}e^{-3(t-\pi)}+\frac{9}{10}\cos t+\frac{3}{10}\sin t\right]\mathcal{U}(t-\pi).$$
  9. Read it piecewise. For $0\le t<\pi$, $y=5e^{-3t}$ (no force, just decay). For $t\ge\pi$ the bracket switches on.
Which theorem do I use?
  • $e^{at}$ times something in $t$, or $F(s-a)$ in $s$: first translation theorem.
  • $\mathcal{U}(t-a)$ in $t$, or $e^{-as}$ in $s$: second translation theorem.
  • $e^{at}$ is in $t$. $e^{-as}$ is in $s$. Don't confuse them.
Beams with point loads

Zill also uses the unit step for beams in 4.3: a load that acts only on part of a beam, such as $w(x)=w_0\big[1-\mathcal{U}(x-\dfrac{L}{2})\big]$, turns the fourth-order beam equation of 3.9 into an IVP in $x$ that the Laplace transform handles with unknown $y''(0)$ and $y'''(0)$ found from the far-end conditions.

Summary
  • $\mathcal{L}\{e^{at}f(t)\}=F(s-a)$. Complete the square to spot $s-a$.
  • Repeated factor $(s-a)^n$: write the numerator in powers of $s-a$.
  • Piecewise functions: add the new formula and subtract the old one, times $\mathcal{U}$, at each break.
  • $\mathcal{L}\{f(t-a)\mathcal{U}(t-a)\}=e^{-as}F(s)$ and $\mathcal{L}\{g(t)\mathcal{U}(t-a)\}=e^{-as}\mathcal{L}\{g(t+a)\}$.

More practice: Laplace transforms page and Test 2 practice.

More worked examples

Example 10The first theorem both ways

Find (a) $\mathcal L\{t^2e^{-3t}\}$ and (b) $\mathcal L^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{s}{s^2+4s+13}\right\}$.

  1. (a) Ignore the exponential. $\mathcal L\{t^2\}=\dfrac{2}{s^3}$.
  2. (a) Shift. The factor is $e^{-3t}$, so $a=-3$ and $s$ becomes $s-(-3)=s+3$: $\mathcal L\{t^2e^{-3t}\}=\dfrac{2}{(s+3)^3}$.
  3. (b) The denominator does not factor. Its discriminant is $16-52<0$. Complete the square: $s^2+4s+13=(s+2)^2+9$.
  4. (b) Make the numerator match. We need $s+2$ on top, so write $s=(s+2)-2$: $$\frac{s}{(s+2)^2+9}=\frac{s+2}{(s+2)^2+9}-\frac23\cdot\frac{3}{(s+2)^2+9}.$$
  5. (b) Invert with the shift. Without the shift, these are $\cos3t$ and $\dfrac23\sin3t$. The shift $s\to s+2$ multiplies by $e^{-2t}$: $$\mathcal L^{-1}=e^{-2t}\left(\cos3t-\frac23\sin3t\right).$$
Example 11A three-piece function

Let $f(t)=2$ for $0\le t<2$, $f(t)=-1$ for $2\le t<5$, and $f(t)=0$ for $t\ge5$. Write $f$ with unit steps and find $\mathcal L\{f\}$.

  1. Start with the first value. $f$ begins at $2$.
  2. At $t=2$. The value changes from $2$ to $-1$, a jump of $-3$. Add $-3\mathcal U(t-2)$.
  3. At $t=5$. The value changes from $-1$ to $0$, a jump of $+1$. Add $\mathcal U(t-5)$.
  4. Result. $f(t)=2-3\mathcal U(t-2)+\mathcal U(t-5)$. Check a point in each piece: at $t=1$, $2$; at $t=3$, $2-3=-1$; at $t=6$, $2-3+1=0$.
  5. Transform. $\mathcal L\{\mathcal U(t-a)\}=\dfrac{e^{-as}}{s}$, so $$F(s)=\frac2s-\frac{3e^{-2s}}{s}+\frac{e^{-5s}}{s}.$$
Example 12Inverse with a delay

Find $\mathcal L^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{e^{-\pi s}}{s^2+1}\right\}$ and $\mathcal L^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{e^{-2s}}{s(s+1)}\right\}$.

  1. First: set the exponential aside. $\mathcal L^{-1}\left\{\dfrac1{s^2+1}\right\}=\sin t$.
  2. First: delay by $\pi$. Replace $t$ by $t-\pi$ and multiply by $\mathcal U(t-\pi)$: $\sin(t-\pi)\,\mathcal U(t-\pi)=-\sin t\,\mathcal U(t-\pi)$, since $\sin(t-\pi)=-\sin t$.
  3. Second: partial fractions first. $\dfrac{1}{s(s+1)}=\dfrac1s-\dfrac1{s+1}$, which inverts to $1-e^{-t}$.
  4. Second: delay by $2$. $\big(1-e^{-(t-2)}\big)\mathcal U(t-2)$. It is $0$ until $t=2$, then rises toward $1$.
Example 13An IVP with a switch

Solve $y'+y=\mathcal U(t-1)$, $y(0)=0$. Describe the solution.

  1. Meaning. Nothing happens until $t=1$; then a constant input of $1$ switches on.
  2. Transform. $sY-0+Y=\dfrac{e^{-s}}{s}$, so $Y=e^{-s}\cdot\dfrac{1}{s(s+1)}$.
  3. Invert the part without $e^{-s}$. $\dfrac{1}{s(s+1)}=\dfrac1s-\dfrac1{s+1}$ gives $1-e^{-t}$.
  4. Apply the delay. $$y(t)=\big(1-e^{-(t-1)}\big)\mathcal U(t-1)=\begin{cases}0,&0\le t<1,\\1-e^{-(t-1)},&t\ge1.\end{cases}$$
  5. Check. Before $t=1$, $y=0$ solves $y'+y=0$ with $y(0)=0$. After $t=1$, $y'+y=e^{-(t-1)}+1-e^{-(t-1)}=1$. At $t=1$ both pieces are $0$, so $y$ is continuous.

Test-day checklist

Mistakes that cost marks
  • Shifting the wrong way. $e^{at}$ in $t$ means $s-a$: $e^{-3t}$ gives $s+3$.
  • Applying the second theorem to $g(t)\mathcal U(t-a)$ without rewriting. Use $e^{-as}\mathcal L\{g(t+a)\}$.
  • Inverting $e^{-as}F(s)$ as $f(t)\mathcal U(t-a)$. It is $f(t-a)\mathcal U(t-a)$: shift the function too.
  • Wrong jumps in step notation. Each coefficient is “new value minus old value”. Check one point in every piece.
  • Completing the square but not fixing the numerator. Rewrite $s$ as $(s+h)-h$ so the pieces match the shifted table entries.

Practice set · 25 problems

25 more problems on this section, weighted toward the kinds that show up on Test 2. Each one has an answer and a full step-by-step solution that explains why each step is taken. The numbers are fixed, so you can come back to the same problem.

Chapter 4 · Section 4.4

Additional operational properties

This section adds three more tools: what multiplying by $t$ does, how to undo a product of transforms (convolution), and how to transform a periodic function. They let us solve equations with resonance, integral equations, and circuits driven by square waves.

Sections 4.1 to 4.3 give enough to solve most IVPs. Section 4.4 adds tools for the cases that are left:

  • Multiplying by $t$ in the time world corresponds to differentiating in the $s$ world: $\mathcal L\{tf(t)\}=-F'(s)$. This handles resonance terms like $t\sin kt$.
  • Convolution. The transform of a product is not the product of transforms. But the product of transforms $F(s)G(s)$ is the transform of the convolution $(f*g)(t)=\displaystyle\int_0^tf(\tau)g(t-\tau)\,d\tau$. This gives a way to invert products without partial fractions, and to solve integral equations.
  • Periodic functions. If $f$ repeats with period $T$, you only need to integrate over one period: $\mathcal L\{f\}=\dfrac{1}{1-e^{-sT}}\displaystyle\int_0^Te^{-st}f(t)\,dt$.

A good way to think about convolution: the response of a system to an input is the sum of its responses to each small piece of the input, each delayed by the time it arrived. That “sum of delayed responses” is the integral.

Multiplying a function by $t^n$

Differentiate the definition of $F(s)$ with respect to $s$. The derivative passes inside the integral, and $\dfrac{\partial}{\partial s}e^{-st}=-te^{-st}$:

$$\frac{dF}{ds}=\int_0^{\infty}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\big[e^{-st}\big]f(t)\,dt=-\int_0^{\infty}e^{-st}\,t\,f(t)\,dt=-\mathcal{L}\{tf(t)\}.$$

So $\mathcal{L}\{tf(t)\}=-\dfrac{d}{ds}F(s)$. Doing it again gives $\mathcal{L}\{t^2f(t)\}=\dfrac{d^2}{ds^2}F(s)$, and so on.

Theorem 4.4.1 · Derivatives of transforms $$\mathcal{L}\{t^nf(t)\}=(-1)^n\frac{d^n}{ds^n}F(s),\qquad n=1,2,3,\dots$$
Example 1Transform of $t\sin kt$

Evaluate $\mathcal{L}\{t\sin kt\}$.

  1. Identify $f$ and $n$. $f(t)=\sin kt$, $F(s)=\dfrac{k}{s^2+k^2}=k(s^2+k^2)^{-1}$, and $n=1$.
  2. Differentiate with the chain rule. $\dfrac{dF}{ds}=-k(s^2+k^2)^{-2}\cdot2s=-\dfrac{2ks}{(s^2+k^2)^2}$.
  3. Apply the sign $(-1)^1$. $\mathcal{L}\{t\sin kt\}=-\dfrac{dF}{ds}=\dfrac{2ks}{(s^2+k^2)^2}$.
  4. Same idea for cosine. $-\dfrac{d}{ds}\dfrac{s}{s^2+k^2}=-\dfrac{(s^2+k^2)-2s^2}{(s^2+k^2)^2}=\dfrac{s^2-k^2}{(s^2+k^2)^2}=\mathcal{L}\{t\cos kt\}$.
Example 2Pure resonance

Solve $x''+16x=\cos4t$, $x(0)=0$, $x'(0)=1$. This is an undamped spring driven at exactly its natural frequency $\omega=4$.

  1. Transform. $s^2X-0-1+16X=\dfrac{s}{s^2+16}$.
  2. Solve for $X$. $(s^2+16)X=1+\dfrac{s}{s^2+16}$, so $X=\dfrac{1}{s^2+16}+\dfrac{s}{(s^2+16)^2}$.
  3. First term. $\dfrac{1}{s^2+16}=\dfrac14\cdot\dfrac{4}{s^2+16}$ inverts to $\dfrac14\sin4t$.
  4. Second term: match Example 1 with $k=4$. $\mathcal{L}\{t\sin4t\}=\dfrac{8s}{(s^2+16)^2}$. We have $s$ on top instead of $8s$, so $\dfrac{s}{(s^2+16)^2}=\dfrac18\cdot\dfrac{8s}{(s^2+16)^2}$, which inverts to $\dfrac18t\sin4t$.
  5. Answer. $$x(t)=\frac14\sin4t+\frac18t\sin4t.$$
  6. Interpret. The factor $t$ makes the oscillations grow without bound. That is the resonance you met in 3.8, now found without guessing the form of a particular solution.

Transforms of integrals: convolution

If $f$ and $g$ are piecewise continuous on $[0,\infty)$, their convolution is the function

$$f*g=\int_0^{t}f(\tau)\,g(t-\tau)\,d\tau.$$

It is a kind of weighted running average: at time $t$, every earlier input $f(\tau)$ contributes through the response $g(t-\tau)$ to that input after a delay $t-\tau$. Substituting $v=t-\tau$ shows the order does not matter: $f*g=g*f$.

Theorem 4.4.2 · Convolution theorem $$\mathcal{L}\{f*g\}=\mathcal{L}\{f(t)\}\,\mathcal{L}\{g(t)\}=F(s)\,G(s),\qquad \mathcal{L}^{-1}\{F(s)G(s)\}=f*g.$$

So although $\mathcal{L}\{fg\}\neq FG$, there is an operation in $t$ that corresponds to multiplying transforms. It is convolution.

Why the theorem is true

Write $F(s)G(s)=\int_0^\infty e^{-s\tau}f(\tau)\,d\tau\int_0^\infty e^{-s\beta}g(\beta)\,d\beta$ as a double integral and substitute $t=\tau+\beta$. The region becomes $0\le\tau\le t$, $0\le t<\infty$, and swapping the order of integration gives $\int_0^\infty e^{-st}\Big[\int_0^t f(\tau)g(t-\tau)\,d\tau\Big]dt=\mathcal{L}\{f*g\}$.

Example 3Transform of a convolution

Evaluate $\mathcal{L}\left\{\displaystyle\int_0^{t}e^{\tau}\sin(t-\tau)\,d\tau\right\}$.

  1. Recognize the form. The integral is $f*g$ with $f(t)=e^t$ and $g(t)=\sin t$. (The $\tau$ goes in $f$ and $t-\tau$ goes in $g$.)
  2. Multiply the transforms. $\mathcal{L}\{e^t\}\cdot\mathcal{L}\{\sin t\}=\dfrac{1}{s-1}\cdot\dfrac{1}{s^2+1}=\dfrac{1}{(s-1)(s^2+1)}$.
  3. No integral needed. Doing this from the definition would mean integrating by parts twice and then transforming.
Example 4Inverse transform by convolution

Evaluate $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{1}{(s-1)(s+4)}\right\}$.

  1. See a product. $F(s)=\dfrac{1}{s-1}$ and $G(s)=\dfrac{1}{s+4}$, so $f(t)=e^t$ and $g(t)=e^{-4t}$.
  2. Write the convolution. $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\{FG\}=\displaystyle\int_0^{t}e^{\tau}e^{-4(t-\tau)}\,d\tau=e^{-4t}\int_0^{t}e^{5\tau}\,d\tau$. The $e^{-4t}$ comes out because it does not depend on $\tau$.
  3. Integrate. $e^{-4t}\left[\dfrac{e^{5\tau}}{5}\right]_0^{t}=e^{-4t}\cdot\dfrac{e^{5t}-1}{5}$.
  4. Answer. $\dfrac{e^{t}-e^{-4t}}{5}$. (Partial fractions gives the same result, which is a useful check.)
Example 5A case where partial fractions do not help

Evaluate $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{1}{(s^2+k^2)^2}\right\}$.

  1. Write as a product. $\dfrac{1}{(s^2+k^2)^2}=\dfrac{1}{s^2+k^2}\cdot\dfrac{1}{s^2+k^2}$, and each factor is $\dfrac1k\mathcal{L}\{\sin kt\}$. So $f=g=\dfrac1k\sin kt$.
  2. Convolution integral. $\mathcal{L}^{-1}=\dfrac{1}{k^2}\displaystyle\int_0^{t}\sin k\tau\,\sin k(t-\tau)\,d\tau$.
  3. Product-to-sum. $\sin A\sin B=\dfrac12\big[\cos(A-B)-\cos(A+B)\big]$. Here $A-B=2k\tau-kt$ and $A+B=kt$.
  4. Integrate in $\tau$. $\displaystyle\frac12\int_0^{t}\big[\cos(2k\tau-kt)-\cos kt\big]d\tau=\frac12\left[\frac{\sin(2k\tau-kt)}{2k}-\tau\cos kt\right]_0^{t}$.
  5. Evaluate the limits. At $\tau=t$: $\dfrac{\sin kt}{2k}-t\cos kt$. At $\tau=0$: $\dfrac{\sin(-kt)}{2k}=-\dfrac{\sin kt}{2k}$. The difference is $\dfrac{\sin kt}{k}-t\cos kt$, and half of it is $\dfrac{\sin kt-kt\cos kt}{2k}$.
  6. Answer. Multiply by $\dfrac{1}{k^2}$: $$\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{(s^2+k^2)^2}\right\}=\frac{\sin kt-kt\cos kt}{2k^3}.$$

Transform of an integral. Taking $g(t)=1$, so $G(s)=\dfrac1s$, the convolution theorem gives

$$\mathcal{L}\left\{\int_0^{t}f(\tau)\,d\tau\right\}=\frac{F(s)}{s},\qquad \mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{F(s)}{s}\right\}=\int_0^{t}f(\tau)\,d\tau.$$

Dividing by $s$ integrates, just as multiplying by $s$ differentiates. For example, $\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{1}{s(s^2+1)}\right\}=\displaystyle\int_0^{t}\sin\tau\,d\tau=1-\cos t$.

Example 6An integral equation

Solve for $f(t)$: $\;f(t)=3t^2-e^{-t}-\displaystyle\int_0^{t}f(\tau)\,e^{t-\tau}\,d\tau$. This is a Volterra integral equation: the unknown appears inside an integral.

  1. Recognize the convolution. $\displaystyle\int_0^{t}f(\tau)e^{t-\tau}d\tau=f*e^{t}$, whose transform is $F(s)\cdot\dfrac{1}{s-1}$.
  2. Transform the whole equation. $F=\dfrac{6}{s^3}-\dfrac{1}{s+1}-\dfrac{F}{s-1}$.
  3. Collect $F$. $F\left(1+\dfrac{1}{s-1}\right)=F\cdot\dfrac{s}{s-1}=\dfrac{6}{s^3}-\dfrac{1}{s+1}$.
  4. Solve. $F=\dfrac{s-1}{s}\left(\dfrac{6}{s^3}-\dfrac{1}{s+1}\right)=\dfrac{6}{s^3}-\dfrac{6}{s^4}-\dfrac{s-1}{s(s+1)}$.
  5. Partial fractions for the last piece. $\dfrac{s-1}{s(s+1)}=\dfrac{A}{s}+\dfrac{B}{s+1}$. At $s=0$: $A=-1$. At $s=-1$: $B=\dfrac{-2}{-1}=2$. So $-\dfrac{s-1}{s(s+1)}=\dfrac1s-\dfrac{2}{s+1}$.
  6. Invert. $F=3\cdot\dfrac{2!}{s^3}-\dfrac{3!}{s^4}+\dfrac1s-\dfrac{2}{s+1}$ gives $$f(t)=3t^2-t^3+1-2e^{-t}.$$
Integro-differential equations: the LRC circuit

In a series LRC circuit, Kirchhoff's law can be written with the current alone: $L\dfrac{di}{dt}+Ri+\dfrac1C\displaystyle\int_0^t i(\tau)\,d\tau=E(t)$. Transforming uses both $\mathcal{L}\{i'\}=sI-i(0)$ and $\mathcal{L}\{\int_0^t i\}=\dfrac{I}{s}$, so the equation becomes algebra in $I(s)$ directly.

Working with convolution

The convolution theorem lets you skip the integral. Sometimes you need the integral itself, though: to check an answer, to see what a convolution actually looks like, or to write one formula that solves an equation for every input. This part shows how to evaluate $f*g$ by hand, which algebra rules convolution follows, and how it gives the response of a system to any forcing.

Method · Evaluating $f*g$ by hand
  1. Rename the variable in $f$. Write $f(\tau)$: replace every $t$ in $f$ by $\tau$.
  2. Flip and shift $g$. Write $g(t-\tau)$: replace every $t$ in $g$ by $t-\tau$.
  3. Integrate in $\tau$ from $0$ to $t$. While you integrate, $t$ is a constant, so any factor that depends only on $t$ comes out in front of the integral.
  4. Check at $t=0$. The answer must be $0$ there, because $\displaystyle\int_0^0(\cdots)\,d\tau=0$.

Since $f*g=g*f$, you may choose which function gets the $t-\tau$. Give it to the one that is easier to shift, usually an exponential or a polynomial.

Picture it: flip, slide, multiply, add up

As a function of $\tau$, the graph of $g(t-\tau)$ is the graph of $g$ flipped left to right and slid along so that $g(0)$ sits at $\tau=t$. For a fixed $t$, multiply this flipped copy by $f(\tau)$ and find the area under the product from $\tau=0$ to $\tau=t$. That area is $(f*g)(t)$. As $t$ increases, the flipped copy slides to the right and the area changes, which traces out the whole function $f*g$.

Example 7A convolution straight from the definition

Evaluate $t*e^{t}$ from the definition. Then check the answer with the convolution theorem.

  1. Choose which function to shift. Since $t*e^{t}=e^{t}*t$, either order works. Shifting the exponential is easier, so take $f(\tau)=\tau$ and $g(t-\tau)=e^{t-\tau}$.
  2. Set up the integral. $\displaystyle t*e^{t}=\int_0^{t}\tau\,e^{t-\tau}\,d\tau=e^{t}\int_0^{t}\tau\,e^{-\tau}\,d\tau$. The factor $e^{t}$ comes out because $t$ is a constant while we integrate in $\tau$.
  3. Integrate by parts. With $u=\tau$ and $dv=e^{-\tau}\,d\tau$, we get $du=d\tau$ and $v=-e^{-\tau}$, so $$\int_0^{t}\tau\,e^{-\tau}\,d\tau=\Big[-\tau e^{-\tau}-e^{-\tau}\Big]_0^{t}=1-te^{-t}-e^{-t}.$$
  4. Multiply by $e^{t}$. $$t*e^{t}=e^{t}\left(1-te^{-t}-e^{-t}\right)=e^{t}-t-1.$$
  5. Check at $t=0$. $e^{0}-0-1=0$, as every convolution must be.
  6. Check with the theorem. The theorem says $\mathcal{L}\{t*e^{t}\}=\dfrac{1}{s^2}\cdot\dfrac{1}{s-1}=\dfrac{1}{s^2(s-1)}$. Transforming our answer term by term gives $$\frac{1}{s-1}-\frac{1}{s^2}-\frac{1}{s}=\frac{s^2-(s-1)-s(s-1)}{s^2(s-1)}=\frac{1}{s^2(s-1)}.$$ The two agree.
Example 8Two bounded functions with a growing convolution

Evaluate $\sin t*\cos t$.

  1. Set up the integral. $\displaystyle\sin t*\cos t=\int_0^{t}\sin\tau\,\cos(t-\tau)\,d\tau$.
  2. Use a product-to-sum identity. $\sin A\cos B=\dfrac12\big[\sin(A+B)+\sin(A-B)\big]$. With $A=\tau$ and $B=t-\tau$ we have $A+B=t$ and $A-B=2\tau-t$, so $$\sin t*\cos t=\frac12\int_0^{t}\big[\sin t+\sin(2\tau-t)\big]\,d\tau.$$
  3. Integrate in $\tau$. $\sin t$ is a constant here, so it integrates to $\tau\sin t$. For the other term, $\displaystyle\int\sin(2\tau-t)\,d\tau=-\frac{\cos(2\tau-t)}{2}$. So the integral is $\dfrac12\left[\tau\sin t-\dfrac{\cos(2\tau-t)}{2}\right]_0^{t}$.
  4. Evaluate the limits. At $\tau=t$ the bracket is $t\sin t-\dfrac{\cos t}{2}$. At $\tau=0$ it is $-\dfrac{\cos(-t)}{2}=-\dfrac{\cos t}{2}$. Subtracting, the cosines cancel: $$\sin t*\cos t=\frac12\,t\sin t.$$
  5. Check with the theorem. $\mathcal{L}\{\sin t\}\,\mathcal{L}\{\cos t\}=\dfrac{1}{s^2+1}\cdot\dfrac{s}{s^2+1}=\dfrac{s}{(s^2+1)^2}$. Example 1 with $k=1$ gives $\mathcal{L}\{t\sin t\}=\dfrac{2s}{(s^2+1)^2}$, and half of that is exactly our transform.
  6. Interpret. Both $\sin t$ and $\cos t$ stay between $-1$ and $1$, yet their convolution grows like $t$. This is the resonance of Example 2 seen from the time side: $\cos t$ keeps pushing at exactly the natural frequency of a system whose free motion is $\sin t$.
Properties of convolution

For piecewise continuous $f$, $g$, $h$ and any constant $c$:

$$\begin{aligned} f*g&=g*f &&\text{(commutative)}\\ f*(g+h)&=f*g+f*h &&\text{(distributive)}\\ (f*g)*h&=f*(g*h) &&\text{(associative)}\\ (cf)*g&=c\,(f*g) &&\text{(constants come out)}\\ f*0&=0. \end{aligned}$$

Why they hold. Each rule is a rule of ordinary multiplication, carried back through the convolution theorem. For example, $\mathcal{L}\{f*(g+h)\}=F\,(G+H)=FG+FH=\mathcal{L}\{f*g+f*h\}$, and two continuous functions with the same transform are the same function.

Careful: convolution is not ordinary multiplication

Some rules of ordinary multiplication do not carry over.

  • Convolving with $1$ changes a function. $f*1=\displaystyle\int_0^{t}f(\tau)\,d\tau$, the running integral of $f$. For example $1*1=\displaystyle\int_0^{t}1\,d\tau=t$, not $1$.
  • $f*f$ can be negative. Example 5 with $k=1$ gives $\sin t*\sin t=\dfrac{\sin t-t\cos t}{2}$, which equals $-\pi$ at $t=2\pi$.
  • The function that acts like $1$ for convolution is the Dirac delta of Section 4.5: $f*\delta=f$.
Example 9One formula for every input

A $1$ kg mass hangs on a spring with spring constant $4$ N/m. It starts at rest in its equilibrium position, and from $t=0$ an external force of $g(t)$ newtons pushes on it. The displacement $y(t)$, in metres, satisfies $$y''+4y=g(t),\qquad y(0)=0,\quad y'(0)=0.$$ Find $y(t)$ for any force $g$, then for the constant force $g(t)=1$ N.

  1. Transform. $s^2Y+4Y=G(s)$, so $Y=\dfrac{1}{s^2+4}\cdot G(s)$.
  2. Recognize a product of transforms. $\dfrac{1}{s^2+4}=\dfrac12\cdot\dfrac{2}{s^2+4}=\mathcal{L}\left\{\dfrac12\sin2t\right\}$, and $G(s)=\mathcal{L}\{g(t)\}$.
  3. Invert with the convolution theorem. Giving the shift $t-\tau$ to the sine, $$y(t)=\frac12\int_0^{t}g(\tau)\,\sin2(t-\tau)\,d\tau.$$ This one formula covers every force. You never need $G(s)$, so it works even when $g$ is not in the table.
  4. Use it for $g(t)=1$. Since $\dfrac{d}{d\tau}\cos2(t-\tau)=2\sin2(t-\tau)$, $$y(t)=\frac12\int_0^{t}\sin2(t-\tau)\,d\tau=\frac12\left[\frac{\cos2(t-\tau)}{2}\right]_0^{t}=\frac12\cdot\frac{1-\cos2t}{2}=\frac{1-\cos2t}{4}.$$
  5. Check. $y''=\cos2t$, so $y''+4y=\cos2t+1-\cos2t=1$. Also $y(0)=0$ and $y'(0)=\dfrac{\sin0}{2}=0$.
  6. Interpret. The mass oscillates between $0$ m and $0.5$ m around $0.25$ m, the position where the spring force $4\times0.25=1$ N balances the push. The function $w(t)=\dfrac12\sin2t$ is how the system responds to one sharp unit blow at $t=0$, its impulse response (Section 4.5). The formula $y=g*w$ says each small piece $g(\tau)\,d\tau$ of the force starts its own copy of $w$ at time $\tau$, and the solution adds all the copies up.
Response to any input

For $ay''+by'+cy=g(t)$ with $y(0)=0$ and $y'(0)=0$,

$$y(t)=(g*w)(t)=\int_0^{t}g(\tau)\,w(t-\tau)\,d\tau,\qquad w(t)=\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{as^2+bs+c}\right\}.$$

If the initial conditions are not zero, add the solution of the homogeneous equation that meets them.

Common mistakes with convolution
  • Putting $\tau$ in both functions, or $t-\tau$ in both. One function gets $\tau$ and the other gets $t-\tau$.
  • Treating $t$ as the variable of integration. Only $\tau$ changes. Factors such as $e^{t}$ or $\sin t$ come out of the integral as constants.
  • Assuming $f*1=f$. In fact $f*1=\int_0^{t}f(\tau)\,d\tau$.
  • Skipping the free check: a convolution of piecewise continuous functions is always $0$ at $t=0$.

Try it yourself: convolution

Work each one on paper, then reveal the solution one step at a time.

Try it 1A polynomial with itself

Evaluate $t*t$ from the definition, and check your answer with the convolution theorem.

$t*t=\dfrac{t^3}{6}$

  1. Set up. $f(\tau)=\tau$ and $g(t-\tau)=t-\tau$, so $\displaystyle t*t=\int_0^{t}\tau\,(t-\tau)\,d\tau=\int_0^{t}\big(t\tau-\tau^2\big)\,d\tau$.
  2. Integrate in $\tau$, holding $t$ fixed. $\left[\dfrac{t\tau^2}{2}-\dfrac{\tau^3}{3}\right]_0^{t}=\dfrac{t^3}{2}-\dfrac{t^3}{3}=\dfrac{t^3}{6}$.
  3. Check with the theorem. $\mathcal{L}\{t\}\cdot\mathcal{L}\{t\}=\dfrac{1}{s^2}\cdot\dfrac{1}{s^2}=\dfrac{1}{s^4}=\dfrac{1}{3!}\cdot\dfrac{3!}{s^4}=\mathcal{L}\left\{\dfrac{t^3}{6}\right\}$. They agree.
Try it 2An exponential and a sine

Evaluate $e^{t}*\sin t$ from the definition. Check that your answer is $0$ at $t=0$ and that its transform matches Example 3.

$e^{t}*\sin t=\dfrac{e^{t}-\sin t-\cos t}{2}$

  1. Shift the exponential. Using $e^{t}*\sin t=\sin t*e^{t}$, write $\displaystyle\int_0^{t}\sin\tau\,e^{t-\tau}\,d\tau=e^{t}\int_0^{t}e^{-\tau}\sin\tau\,d\tau$.
  2. Use the standard integral. Integrating by parts twice gives $\displaystyle\int e^{-\tau}\sin\tau\,d\tau=-\frac{e^{-\tau}(\sin\tau+\cos\tau)}{2}$. You can check this by differentiating.
  3. Evaluate from $0$ to $t$. $-\dfrac{e^{-t}(\sin t+\cos t)}{2}-\left(-\dfrac{1}{2}\right)=\dfrac12-\dfrac{e^{-t}(\sin t+\cos t)}{2}$.
  4. Multiply by $e^{t}$. $$e^{t}*\sin t=\frac{e^{t}-\sin t-\cos t}{2}.$$ At $t=0$ this is $\dfrac{1-0-1}{2}=0$.
  5. Check with Example 3. Our answer transforms to $\dfrac12\left[\dfrac{1}{s-1}-\dfrac{1}{s^2+1}-\dfrac{s}{s^2+1}\right]=\dfrac{(s^2+1)-(s-1)-s(s-1)}{2(s-1)(s^2+1)}=\dfrac{1}{(s-1)(s^2+1)}$, which is the answer of Example 3.
Try it 3Convolving $1$ with itself

Find $1*1*1$ in two ways: by convolving one pair at a time, and with the convolution theorem.

$1*1*1=\dfrac{t^2}{2}$

  1. First pair. $1*1=\displaystyle\int_0^{t}1\cdot1\,d\tau=t$.
  2. Then the third $1$. By associativity $1*1*1=(1*1)*1=t*1=\displaystyle\int_0^{t}\tau\,d\tau=\frac{t^2}{2}$.
  3. With the theorem. $\mathcal{L}\{1\}^3=\dfrac{1}{s^3}=\dfrac12\cdot\dfrac{2!}{s^3}=\mathcal{L}\left\{\dfrac{t^2}{2}\right\}$. Same answer.
  4. The pattern. Each extra $*1$ integrates once more, so $n$ ones convolved together give $\dfrac{t^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}$.
Try it 4A spring pushed by a growing force

Write the solution of $y''+y=g(t)$, $y(0)=0$, $y'(0)=0$ as a convolution integral. Then use it to find $y(t)$ when $g(t)=t$.

$y(t)=\displaystyle\int_0^{t}g(\tau)\sin(t-\tau)\,d\tau$; for $g(t)=t$, $y(t)=t-\sin t$

  1. Transform. $(s^2+1)Y=G(s)$, so $Y=\dfrac{1}{s^2+1}\,G(s)$ and $w(t)=\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{1}{s^2+1}\right\}=\sin t$.
  2. General formula. $\displaystyle y(t)=\int_0^{t}g(\tau)\,\sin(t-\tau)\,d\tau$.
  3. Put in $g(t)=t$, shifting the polynomial instead. By commutativity $y=\displaystyle\int_0^{t}\sin\tau\,(t-\tau)\,d\tau=t\int_0^{t}\sin\tau\,d\tau-\int_0^{t}\tau\sin\tau\,d\tau$.
  4. Integrate. $\displaystyle t\int_0^{t}\sin\tau\,d\tau=t(1-\cos t)$ and, by parts, $\displaystyle\int_0^{t}\tau\sin\tau\,d\tau=\Big[\sin\tau-\tau\cos\tau\Big]_0^{t}=\sin t-t\cos t$.
  5. Combine. $y(t)=t-t\cos t-\sin t+t\cos t=t-\sin t$.
  6. Check. $y''=\sin t$, so $y''+y=\sin t+t-\sin t=t$. Also $y(0)=0$ and $y'(0)=1-\cos0=0$.

Transform of a periodic function

If $f(t+T)=f(t)$ for all $t$, the integral over $[0,\infty)$ is a repeat of the integral over one period. Split $\int_0^\infty=\int_0^T+\int_T^\infty$ and substitute $t=u+T$ in the second part. Periodicity makes it $e^{-sT}\mathcal{L}\{f\}$, so $\mathcal{L}\{f\}=\int_0^Te^{-st}f\,dt+e^{-sT}\mathcal{L}\{f\}$. Solving for $\mathcal{L}\{f\}$:

Theorem 4.4.3 · Periodic functions $$\mathcal{L}\{f(t)\}=\frac{1}{1-e^{-sT}}\int_0^{T}e^{-st}f(t)\,dt.$$

You only need to integrate over one period.

Example 10A square wave

Find the transform of the square wave $E(t)=1$ for $0\le t<1$, $E(t)=0$ for $1\le t<2$, repeated with period $T=2$.

  1. Integrate over one period. $\displaystyle\int_0^{2}e^{-st}E(t)\,dt=\int_0^{1}e^{-st}\,dt+0=\frac{1-e^{-s}}{s}$.
  2. Apply the theorem with $T=2$. $\mathcal{L}\{E\}=\dfrac{1}{1-e^{-2s}}\cdot\dfrac{1-e^{-s}}{s}$.
  3. Factor the difference of squares. $1-e^{-2s}=(1-e^{-s})(1+e^{-s})$. Cancel $1-e^{-s}$.
  4. Answer. $\mathcal{L}\{E\}=\dfrac{1}{s\,(1+e^{-s})}$.
Summary
  • $\mathcal{L}\{tf\}=-F'(s)$, and in general $\mathcal{L}\{t^nf\}=(-1)^nF^{(n)}(s)$.
  • $\mathcal{L}\{f*g\}=FG$. Use it to invert products and to solve integral equations.
  • By hand: $(f*g)(t)=\int_0^tf(\tau)\,g(t-\tau)\,d\tau$, with $t$ held constant. Every convolution is $0$ at $t=0$.
  • $f*g=g*f$, and convolution distributes and associates, but $f*1=\int_0^tf\,d\tau\neq f$.
  • Zero initial conditions: the response to any input $g$ is $y=g*w$, where $w=\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{as^2+bs+c}\right\}$.
  • $\mathcal{L}\{\int_0^tf\}=\frac{F}{s}$.
  • Periodic $f$: $\frac{1}{1-e^{-sT}}\int_0^Te^{-st}f\,dt$.

More practice: Laplace transforms page.

More worked examples

Example 11Inverting a product by convolution

Find $\mathcal L^{-1}\left\{\dfrac{1}{s(s^2+1)}\right\}$ using convolution.

  1. Split as a product. $\dfrac1s\cdot\dfrac{1}{s^2+1}$, the transforms of $f=1$ and $g=\sin t$.
  2. Convolve. $\displaystyle(1*\sin)(t)=\int_0^t\sin\tau\,d\tau=\big[-\cos\tau\big]_0^t=1-\cos t$.
  3. Check by partial fractions. $\dfrac{1}{s(s^2+1)}=\dfrac1s-\dfrac{s}{s^2+1}$, which inverts to $1-\cos t$. Same answer.
Common mistakes
  • Writing $\mathcal L\{fg\}=FG$. Only the convolution has transform $FG$.
  • Forgetting the minus sign in $\mathcal L\{tf\}=-F'(s)$.
  • Integrating over $[0,\infty)$ instead of one period for periodic functions.

Practice set · 15 problems

15 more problems on this section. Each one has an answer and a full step-by-step solution that explains why each step is taken. The numbers are fixed, so you can come back to the same problem.

Chapter 4 · Section 4.5

The Dirac delta function

Mechanical systems are often hit by a force of large size that acts only for a very short time: a hammer blow on a spring, a bat hitting a ball, a lightning surge in a circuit. The Dirac delta function is the idealized model of such an impulse.

A hammer blow delivers a large force over a very short time. What matters is not the exact shape of the force but its total effect, the impulse $\int F\,dt$. The Dirac delta $\delta(t-t_0)$ is the idealization: zero everywhere except at $t_0$, with total area $1$. It is not an ordinary function, but it has a perfectly good transform:

$$\mathcal L\{\delta(t-t_0)\}=e^{-st_0}.$$

In an IVP, $\delta(t-t_0)$ on the right side gives the system a sudden kick at $t=t_0$. For a spring, the position stays continuous, but the velocity jumps by the size of the impulse divided by the mass. Before $t_0$ the system does whatever its initial conditions say; after $t_0$ it moves as if freshly started with a new velocity.

The solution method is unchanged: transform, solve for $Y$, invert. The $e^{-st_0}$ factor produces a unit step in the answer, $\mathcal U(t-t_0)$, which switches the response on at the moment of the blow.

Unit impulse

Consider the rectangular pulse

$$\delta_a(t-t_0)=\begin{cases}0,&0\le t<t_0-a\\ \dfrac{1}{2a},&t_0-a\le t<t_0+a\\ 0,&t\ge t_0+a,\end{cases}$$

with $a>0$ and $t_0>0$. It has width $2a$ and height $\dfrac{1}{2a}$, so its area is always $1$:

$$\int_0^{\infty}\delta_a(t-t_0)\,dt=1.$$

This is a unit impulse. As $a$ shrinks the pulse gets narrower and taller, but its area stays $1$.

Interactive · shrinking pulse, constant area

The Dirac delta function

The limit $\delta(t-t_0)=\lim_{a\to0}\delta_a(t-t_0)$ is called the Dirac delta function. It is characterized by two properties:

Properties of $\delta$

(i) $\delta(t-t_0)=\begin{cases}\infty,&t=t_0\\ 0,&t\ne t_0\end{cases}$ $\qquad$ (ii) $\displaystyle\int_0^{\infty}\delta(t-t_0)\,dt=1.$

No ordinary function behaves this way. $\delta$ is a generalized function: it only makes sense inside an integral. The most useful such integral is the sifting property: for any continuous $f$,

$$\int_0^{\infty}f(t)\,\delta(t-t_0)\,dt=f(t_0).$$

The delta "sifts out" the value of $f$ at the one instant $t_0$, because that is the only place it is nonzero.

Its Laplace transform

Write the pulse with unit steps,

$$\delta_a(t-t_0)=\frac{1}{2a}\Big[\mathcal{U}\big(t-(t_0-a)\big)-\mathcal{U}\big(t-(t_0+a)\big)\Big],$$

and use $\mathcal{L}\{\mathcal{U}(t-c)\}=\dfrac{e^{-cs}}{s}$:

$$\mathcal{L}\{\delta_a(t-t_0)\}=\frac{1}{2a}\left[\frac{e^{-(t_0-a)s}-e^{-(t_0+a)s}}{s}\right]=e^{-st_0}\left(\frac{e^{sa}-e^{-sa}}{2sa}\right).$$

As $a\to0$ the bracket has the form $\frac00$. L'Hôpital's rule (differentiate in $a$) gives $\dfrac{s e^{sa}+se^{-sa}}{2s}\to1$. So:

Theorem 4.5.1 · Transform of the Dirac delta $$\mathcal{L}\{\delta(t-t_0)\}=e^{-st_0},\qquad\text{and with }t_0=0,\quad\mathcal{L}\{\delta(t)\}=1.$$

Notice that $\mathcal{L}\{\delta(t)\}=1$ does not go to $0$ as $s\to\infty$. This does not contradict Theorem 4.1.3, because $\delta$ is not a piecewise continuous function.

Example 1A hammer blow on a spring

Solve $y''+y=4\delta(t-2\pi)$ subject to (a) $y(0)=1$, $y'(0)=0$ and (b) $y(0)=0$, $y'(0)=0$.

Physically, a mass on a spring is struck sharply at $t=2\pi$ by an impulse of size $4$.

  1. (a) Transform. $s^2Y-s+Y=4e^{-2\pi s}$, using Theorem 4.5.1 on the right.
  2. (a) Solve for $Y$. $Y=\dfrac{s}{s^2+1}+\dfrac{4e^{-2\pi s}}{s^2+1}$.
  3. (a) Invert the first term. $\cos t$.
  4. (a) Invert the second term with the second translation theorem. $\dfrac{4}{s^2+1}\to4\sin t$. The factor $e^{-2\pi s}$ means replace $t$ by $t-2\pi$ and switch on at $2\pi$: $4\sin(t-2\pi)\,\mathcal{U}(t-2\pi)$.
  5. (a) Simplify. $\sin(t-2\pi)=\sin t$ because sine has period $2\pi$. So $$y(t)=\cos t+4\sin t\;\mathcal{U}(t-2\pi).$$
  6. (a) Read it piecewise. For $0\le t<2\pi$, $y=\cos t$. For $t\ge2\pi$, $y=\cos t+4\sin t$: the blow suddenly increases the amplitude of the motion.
  7. (b) Zero initial conditions. Now the $s$ from $y(0)$ is missing, so $Y=\dfrac{4e^{-2\pi s}}{s^2+1}$ and $y(t)=4\sin t\;\mathcal{U}(t-2\pi)$.
  8. (b) Interpret. The mass sits still until $t=2\pi$. Then the blow sets it oscillating with amplitude $4$.
Notice the kink

In (b), $y$ is continuous at $t=2\pi$ but $y'$ jumps from $0$ to $4$. An impulse changes the velocity instantly, not the position. That is a good check on any delta-function answer.

Example 2A damped system struck once

Solve $y''+2y'+2y=\delta(t-\pi)$, $y(0)=0$, $y'(0)=0$.

  1. Transform. $(s^2+2s+2)Y=e^{-\pi s}$, so $Y=e^{-\pi s}\cdot\dfrac{1}{s^2+2s+2}$.
  2. Complete the square. $s^2+2s+2=(s+1)^2+1$. By the first translation theorem, $\dfrac{1}{(s+1)^2+1}\to e^{-t}\sin t$.
  3. Apply the delay. Replace $t$ by $t-\pi$ and switch on at $\pi$: $y(t)=e^{-(t-\pi)}\sin(t-\pi)\,\mathcal{U}(t-\pi)$.
  4. Simplify. $\sin(t-\pi)=-\sin t$, so $$y(t)=-e^{-(t-\pi)}\sin t\;\mathcal{U}(t-\pi).$$ Nothing happens before $t=\pi$. Afterwards the system rings and dies away because of the damping.
Impulse response and convolution

With zero initial conditions, the solution of $ay''+by'+cy=\delta(t)$ is called the impulse response $w(t)$, and its transform is $W(s)=\dfrac{1}{as^2+bs+c}$. For any other input $g$, $Y=W(s)G(s)$, so by the convolution theorem of 4.4 the response is $y=w*g$. Knowing how a system reacts to one sharp blow tells you how it reacts to everything.

Summary
  • $\delta(t-t_0)$ models a unit impulse at $t_0$: zero everywhere else, total area $1$.
  • Sifting: $\int_0^\infty f(t)\delta(t-t_0)\,dt=f(t_0)$.
  • $\mathcal{L}\{\delta(t-t_0)\}=e^{-st_0}$. Then invert with the second translation theorem.
  • An impulse makes the velocity jump. The position stays continuous.

More practice: Laplace transforms page.

More worked examples

Example 3A kick to a system at rest

Solve $y''+4y=3\delta(t-2)$, $y(0)=0$, $y'(0)=0$.

  1. Transform. $(s^2+4)Y=3e^{-2s}$, so $Y=\dfrac32\cdot e^{-2s}\cdot\dfrac{2}{s^2+4}$.
  2. Invert without the delay. $\dfrac{2}{s^2+4}$ is $\sin2t$.
  3. Apply the delay. $y=\dfrac32\sin\big(2(t-2)\big)\mathcal U(t-2)$.
  4. Meaning. Nothing happens until $t=2$. Then the system oscillates with amplitude $\dfrac32$. Just after the kick, $y'=3$: the impulse $3$ divided by the mass $1$.

Chapter 13 · Section 13.1

Separable partial differential equations

Heat flow, vibrating strings and steady temperatures are all described by fairly simple linear partial differential equations (PDEs). The idea of this whole chapter is to find solutions of a PDE by reducing it to ordinary differential equations (ODEs) that we already know how to solve.

A partial differential equation involves a function of two variables, like $u(x,t)$. We already know how to solve ordinary differential equations, so the strategy is to reduce the PDE to ODEs. Separation of variables does that by guessing a product

$$u(x,t)=X(x)\,T(t).$$

Substituting, the PDE usually rearranges into “a function of $x$ only = a function of $t$ only”. The only way a function of $x$ can equal a function of $t$ for all $x$ and $t$ is if both are the same constant. Calling that constant $-\lambda$ gives two ODEs, one for $X$ and one for $T$, linked by $\lambda$.

The sign of $\lambda$ matters for the same reason as in Section 3.9: it decides whether $X$ is built from exponentials, a straight line, or sines and cosines. Boundary conditions later pick out which $\lambda$ are allowed.

Products alone rarely satisfy every condition. But the equations here are linear and homogeneous, so sums of product solutions are also solutions (the superposition principle). That is how Fourier series enter in Sections 13.2 to 13.5.

Linear PDEs

Like ODEs, PDEs are either linear or nonlinear. In a linear PDE the unknown function and its partial derivatives appear only to the first power and are not multiplied together. If $u$ is the unknown and $x$, $y$ are the independent variables, the general linear second-order PDE is

$$A\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial x^2}+B\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial x\,\partial y}+C\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial y^2}+D\frac{\partial u}{\partial x}+E\frac{\partial u}{\partial y}+Fu=G, \tag{1}$$

where $A,B,\dots,G$ are constants or functions of $x$ and $y$. When $G(x,y)=0$ the equation is homogeneous. Otherwise it is nonhomogeneous. For example,

$$\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial x^2}+\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial y^2}-u=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial x^2}-\frac{\partial u}{\partial y}=xy$$

are linear second-order PDEs. The first is homogeneous and the second is nonhomogeneous. We use subscripts for partial derivatives: $u_x=\dfrac{\partial u}{\partial x}$, $u_{xx}=\dfrac{\partial^2u}{\partial x^2}$, $u_t=\dfrac{\partial u}{\partial t}$ and so on.

A solution of (1) is a function $u(x,y)$ that has all the partial derivatives in the equation and satisfies it in some region of the $xy$-plane. General solutions of PDEs are hard to find and not very useful in applications, so we focus on finding particular solutions that also satisfy given side conditions.

Separation of variables

The method we use is called separation of variables. We look for a solution that is a product of a function of $x$ alone and a function of $y$ alone:

The product assumption $$u(x,y)=X(x)\,Y(y).$$

Why this helps: the partial derivatives of a product like this are just ordinary derivatives of one factor times the other factor,

$$\frac{\partial u}{\partial x}=X'Y,\qquad\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial x^2}=X''Y,\qquad\frac{\partial u}{\partial y}=XY',\qquad\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial y^2}=XY''.$$

Primes now mean ordinary differentiation. With luck, the PDE can be rearranged so all the $x$ stuff is on one side and all the $y$ stuff is on the other. Then each side must equal the same constant, and we get two ODEs.

Example 1Using separation of variables

Find product solutions of $\dfrac{\partial^2u}{\partial x^2}=4\dfrac{\partial u}{\partial y}$.

  1. Substitute $u=XY$. $u_{xx}=X''Y$ and $u_y=XY'$, so the PDE becomes $X''Y=4XY'$.
  2. Separate. Divide both sides by $4XY$: $\dfrac{X''}{4X}=\dfrac{Y'}{Y}$. The left side depends only on $x$ and the right side only on $y$.
  3. Why both sides are constant. If we change $x$ while holding $y$ fixed, the right side cannot change, so the left side cannot either. Likewise for $y$. The only way a function of $x$ can always equal a function of $y$ is if both are the same constant. Call it $-\lambda$ (the minus sign is a convenience that makes later formulas tidier).
  4. Two ODEs. $\dfrac{X''}{4X}=-\lambda$ and $\dfrac{Y'}{Y}=-\lambda$ give $$X''+4\lambda X=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad Y'+\lambda Y=0. \tag{2}$$
  5. Case I, $\lambda=0$. The ODEs are $X''=0$ and $Y'=0$. Integrating: $X=c_1+c_2x$ and $Y=c_3$. So $u=XY=A_1+B_1x$, where $A_1=c_1c_3$, $B_1=c_2c_3$.
  6. Case II, $\lambda=-\alpha^2<0$. The ODEs are $X''-4\alpha^2X=0$ and $Y'-\alpha^2Y=0$. The auxiliary equation $m^2-4\alpha^2=0$ has roots $\pm2\alpha$, so $X=c_4\cosh2\alpha x+c_5\sinh2\alpha x$. The first-order equation gives $Y=c_6e^{\alpha^2y}$. So $$u=e^{\alpha^2y}\big(A_2\cosh2\alpha x+B_2\sinh2\alpha x\big).$$
  7. Case III, $\lambda=\alpha^2>0$. The ODEs are $X''+4\alpha^2X=0$ and $Y'+\alpha^2Y=0$. Now the roots are $\pm2\alpha i$, so $X=c_7\cos2\alpha x+c_8\sin2\alpha x$ and $Y=c_9e^{-\alpha^2y}$. So $$u=e^{-\alpha^2y}\big(A_3\cos2\alpha x+B_3\sin2\alpha x\big).$$
  8. Check one. Take $u=e^{-\alpha^2y}\cos2\alpha x$. Then $u_{xx}=-4\alpha^2u$ and $u_y=-\alpha^2u$, so $4u_y=-4\alpha^2u=u_{xx}$. It works.
Why we always check three cases

The separation constant can be zero, negative or positive, and each gives a different kind of ODE solution: polynomials, hyperbolic functions (or real exponentials), or sines and cosines. In 13.3 to 13.5 the boundary conditions will decide which cases survive. This is exactly the eigenvalue analysis of Section 3.9.

Example 2A first-order equation

Find product solutions of $x\,\dfrac{\partial u}{\partial x}=y\,\dfrac{\partial u}{\partial y}$.

  1. Substitute $u=XY$. $xX'Y=yXY'$.
  2. Separate. Divide by $XY$: $\dfrac{xX'}{X}=\dfrac{yY'}{Y}=\lambda$.
  3. Solve each ODE. $\dfrac{X'}{X}=\dfrac{\lambda}{x}$ integrates to $\ln|X|=\lambda\ln|x|+C$, so $X=c_1x^{\lambda}$. In the same way $Y=c_2y^{\lambda}$.
  4. Product. $u=XY=A\,(xy)^{\lambda}$ for any constants $A$ and $\lambda$.
Not every PDE separates

Separation of variables is not a general method. For example, try $u=XY$ in $u_{xx}-u_y=x$. You get $X''Y-XY'=x$, and there is no way to put all the $x$ terms on one side and all the $y$ terms on the other. Mixed derivatives $u_{xy}$ and nonhomogeneous terms are the usual culprits.

The superposition principle

Theorem 13.1.1 · Superposition principle

If $u_1,u_2,\dots,u_k$ are solutions of a homogeneous linear PDE, then the linear combination $u=c_1u_1+c_2u_2+\cdots+c_ku_k$ is also a solution.

This is the PDE version of Theorem 3.1.2, and it is true for the same reason: derivatives of a sum are sums of derivatives. Throughout the chapter we also assume that an infinite sum of solutions, $u=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c_ku_k$, is a solution, provided the series converges nicely. Infinite sums are what let us match an arbitrary initial temperature or shape.

Classification of equations

Linear second-order PDEs with constant coefficients come in three types, and the type depends only on the coefficients of the second derivatives in (1).

Definition 13.1.1 · Classification

The equation $Au_{xx}+Bu_{xy}+Cu_{yy}+Du_x+Eu_y+Fu=0$ is

  • hyperbolic if $B^2-4AC>0$,
  • parabolic if $B^2-4AC=0$,
  • elliptic if $B^2-4AC<0$.

The names come from the conic sections $Ax^2+Bxy+Cy^2=1$, which use the same discriminant. The type matters because it tells you what kind of side conditions make sense, as you will see in 13.2.

Example 3Classifying equations

Classify (a) $3u_{xx}=u_y$, (b) $u_{xx}=u_{yy}$ and (c) $u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0$.

  1. (a) Move everything to one side. $3u_{xx}-u_y=0$, so $A=3$, $B=0$, $C=0$. Then $B^2-4AC=0$: parabolic. (This is a heat equation.)
  2. (b) $u_{xx}-u_{yy}=0$, so $A=1$, $B=0$, $C=-1$, and $B^2-4AC=0-4(1)(-1)=4>0$: hyperbolic. (A wave equation.)
  3. (c) $A=1$, $B=0$, $C=1$, and $B^2-4AC=-4<0$: elliptic. (Laplace's equation.)
Summary
  • Assume $u=X(x)Y(y)$, substitute, divide to separate, set both sides equal to $-\lambda$.
  • Solve the two ODEs in the three cases $\lambda=0$, $\lambda=-\alpha^2$, $\lambda=\alpha^2$.
  • Sums of solutions of a homogeneous linear PDE are solutions.
  • $B^2-4AC$: positive hyperbolic, zero parabolic, negative elliptic.

50 numbered problems with full solutions: PDE practice page.

More worked examples

Example 4Separating the heat equation

Find the product solutions of $ku_{xx}=u_t$.

  1. Substitute $u=XT$. $u_{xx}=X''T$ and $u_t=XT'$, so $kX''T=XT'$.
  2. Separate. Divide by $kXT$: $\dfrac{X''}{X}=\dfrac{T'}{kT}$. The left depends on $x$ only and the right on $t$ only, so both equal a constant $-\lambda$.
  3. Two ODEs. $X''+\lambda X=0$ and $T'+k\lambda T=0$.
  4. Solve for $\lambda=\alpha^2>0$. $X=c_1\cos\alpha x+c_2\sin\alpha x$ and $T=c_3e^{-k\alpha^2t}$. Product solutions decay in time, as heat flow should.
  5. The other cases. $\lambda=0$: $X=c_1+c_2x$, $T=c_3$. $\lambda=-\alpha^2$: $X=c_1\cosh\alpha x+c_2\sinh\alpha x$, $T=c_3e^{k\alpha^2t}$.

Practice set · 12 problems

12 more problems on this section. Each one has an answer and a full step-by-step solution that explains why each step is taken. The numbers are fixed, so you can come back to the same problem.

Chapter 13 · Section 13.2

Classical PDEs and boundary-value problems

For the rest of the chapter we work with three equations from mathematical physics. This section shows where they come from, what the boundary and initial conditions mean physically, and the Fourier series tools we will need to solve them.

EquationDescribesNeeds
$ku_{xx}=u_t$ (heat)temperature in a rod spreading out over timeone initial condition $u(x,0)$, two boundary conditions
$a^2u_{xx}=u_{tt}$ (wave)small vibrations of a stringtwo initial conditions $u(x,0)$ and $u_t(x,0)$, two boundary conditions
$u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0$ (Laplace)steady-state temperature in a plateno time; a condition on each edge

The number of initial conditions matches the time derivatives: the heat equation is first order in $t$, the wave equation second order.

Reading boundary conditions

  • $u(0,t)=0$: the end is held at temperature zero, or the string is fixed there.
  • $u_x(0,t)=0$: no heat flows through the end (insulated), or the string end slides freely.

Why Fourier series

Separation gives solutions like $e^{-k(n\pi/L)^2t}\sin\dfrac{n\pi x}{L}$. At $t=0$ a sum of these is a sine series. To match an initial temperature $f(x)$ we must write $f$ as a sine series, and the coefficients come from the half-range formulas. That is the only reason Fourier series appear in this chapter.

The three equations

$$k\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial x^2}=\frac{\partial u}{\partial t},\qquad k>0 \tag{1}$$ $$a^2\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial x^2}=\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial t^2} \tag{2}$$ $$\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial x^2}+\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial y^2}=0 \tag{3}$$

These are the one-dimensional heat equation, the one-dimensional wave equation and Laplace's equation in two dimensions. "One-dimensional" means there is one space variable $x$ plus time $t$. In (3), $x$ and $y$ are both space variables. Laplace's equation is often written $\nabla^2u=0$, where $\nabla^2u=u_{xx}+u_{yy}$ is the Laplacian.

Comparing with Definition 13.1.1 (with $t$ playing the role of $y$), the heat equation is parabolic, the wave equation is hyperbolic and Laplace's equation is elliptic.

Equation$u$ meansConditions needed
Heat $k\,u_{xx}=u_t$temperature in a thin rod2 boundary conditions, 1 initial condition
Wave $a^2u_{xx}=u_{tt}$displacement of a string2 boundary conditions, 2 initial conditions
Laplace $u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0$steady temperature in a platea condition on every edge

Where the heat equation comes from

Take a thin rod of length $L$ along the $x$-axis with cross-sectional area $A$. Assume heat flows only along the rod (the curved surface is insulated), no heat is generated inside, and the density $\rho$, specific heat $\gamma$ and thermal conductivity $K$ are constants. Two laws from physics are used:

  1. The heat in a piece of mass $m$ at temperature $u$ is $Q=\gamma mu$.
  2. Heat flows from hot to cold at a rate proportional to the area and the temperature gradient: $Q_t=-KAu_x$.

Look at a thin slice between $x$ and $x+\Delta x$. Its mass is $\rho A\Delta x$, so it holds heat $Q=\gamma\rho A\Delta x\,u$, and the rate this changes is $Q_t=\gamma\rho A\Delta x\,u_t$. That change is caused by heat flowing in at the left minus heat flowing out at the right:

$$-KAu_x(x,t)-\big[-KAu_x(x+\Delta x,t)\big]=KA\big[u_x(x+\Delta x,t)-u_x(x,t)\big].$$

Setting the two rates equal and dividing by $\gamma\rho A\Delta x$:

$$\frac{K}{\gamma\rho}\cdot\frac{u_x(x+\Delta x,t)-u_x(x,t)}{\Delta x}=u_t.$$

As $\Delta x\to0$ the fraction becomes $u_{xx}$. With $k=\dfrac{K}{\gamma\rho}$, called the thermal diffusivity, this is $k\,u_{xx}=u_t$.

Where the wave equation comes from

A string of length $L$ is stretched tight between $x=0$ and $x=L$. Let $u(x,t)$ be its vertical displacement. Assume the string is perfectly flexible and uniform (mass per unit length $\rho$), the displacements and slopes are small, and the tension $T$ is the same everywhere and much larger than gravity.

On a small piece between $x$ and $x+\Delta x$, the tension pulls along the string at each end. For small angles, $\sin\theta\approx\tan\theta$, and $\tan\theta$ is the slope $u_x$. So the net vertical force is

$$T\sin\theta_2-T\sin\theta_1\approx T\big[u_x(x+\Delta x,t)-u_x(x,t)\big].$$

The piece has mass $\rho\Delta x$ and vertical acceleration $u_{tt}$. Newton's second law gives $T\big[u_x(x+\Delta x,t)-u_x(x,t)\big]=\rho\Delta x\,u_{tt}$. Divide by $\Delta x$ and let $\Delta x\to0$: $Tu_{xx}=\rho u_{tt}$. With $a^2=\dfrac{T}{\rho}$ this is (2). A tighter or lighter string has larger $a$, and you will see in 13.4 that this means a higher pitch.

Laplace's equation

The heat equation in a thin flat plate is $k(u_{xx}+u_{yy})=u_t$. If the temperatures on the edges are held fixed, the plate eventually settles into a steady state where nothing changes in time, so $u_t=0$. What is left is $u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0$. So Laplace's equation describes steady-state temperature. It also appears in electrostatics, fluid flow and the static shape of membranes.

Initial conditions

Since the heat equation has $u_t$ (first order in time), we need one initial condition: the starting temperature $u(x,0)=f(x)$. The wave equation has $u_{tt}$ (second order in time), so we need two: the starting shape $u(x,0)=f(x)$ and the starting velocity $u_t(x,0)=g(x)$. A string "released from rest" has $g(x)=0$.

Boundary conditions

At an end of the rod, say $x=L$, there are three common kinds of condition:

TypeConditionMeaning for a rod
Dirichlet$u(L,t)=u_0$the end is held at temperature $u_0$
Neumann$u_x(L,t)=0$the end is insulated: no heat flows through it
Robin$u_x(L,t)=-h\big(u(L,t)-u_m\big)$heat leaks into a medium at temperature $u_m$

Why Neumann means insulated: the heat flow through the end is $-KAu_x$, so zero flow means $u_x=0$. For a string, $u(0,t)=0$ means the end is fixed, and $u_x(L,t)=0$ means the end is free to slide on a vertical rod.

A condition is homogeneous when it is "something $=0$", like $u(0,t)=0$ or $u_x(L,t)=0$. Separation of variables needs homogeneous boundary conditions on the variable we expand in, which is why we will sometimes shift a problem first.

Example 1Words to a boundary-value problem

A rod of length $L$ has its left end held at $0^\circ$, its right end insulated, and initial temperature $f(x)$ throughout. Write the boundary-value problem.

  1. The PDE. Temperature in a rod: $k\,u_{xx}=u_t$, for $0<x<L$, $t>0$.
  2. Left end, held at zero. A fixed temperature is Dirichlet: $u(0,t)=0$.
  3. Right end, insulated. No heat flow is Neumann: $u_x(L,t)=0$.
  4. Initial temperature. $u(x,0)=f(x)$, for $0<x<L$.
Example 2A string and a plate

(a) A string of length $L$ is fixed at both ends, has initial shape $f(x)$ and initial velocity $g(x)$. (b) A rectangular plate $0\le x\le a$, $0\le y\le b$ has its left and right edges insulated, its bottom held at $0$ and its top held at temperature $f(x)$. Write each problem.

  1. (a) PDE. $a^2u_{xx}=u_{tt}$, $0<x<L$, $t>0$.
  2. (a) Fixed ends. $u(0,t)=0$ and $u(L,t)=0$.
  3. (a) Two initial conditions. $u(x,0)=f(x)$ and $\left.\dfrac{\partial u}{\partial t}\right|_{t=0}=g(x)$.
  4. (b) PDE. Steady temperature, so Laplace: $u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0$, $0<x<a$, $0<y<b$.
  5. (b) Insulated sides. $u_x(0,y)=0$ and $u_x(a,y)=0$.
  6. (b) Bottom and top. $u(x,0)=0$ and $u(x,b)=f(x)$. There are no initial conditions because nothing depends on time.
Variations

If the rod loses heat through its sides into a medium at temperature $u_m$, the equation becomes $k\,u_{xx}-h(u-u_m)=u_t$. A string with external, damping and restoring forces satisfies $a^2u_{xx}=u_{tt}+cu_t+ku-F(x,t)$. The methods of this chapter extend to many of these.

The Fourier series toolkit

Every problem in 13.3 to 13.5 ends the same way: we must write a given function $f(x)$ on $0<x<L$ as an infinite sum of sines or of cosines. These are half-range expansions (Zill 12.3).

Half-range sine series $$f(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}b_n\sin\frac{n\pi x}{L},\qquad b_n=\frac{2}{L}\int_0^{L}f(x)\sin\frac{n\pi x}{L}\,dx.$$
Half-range cosine series $$f(x)=\frac{a_0}{2}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_n\cos\frac{n\pi x}{L},$$ $$a_n=\frac{2}{L}\int_0^{L}f(x)\cos\frac{n\pi x}{L}\,dx,\qquad n=0,1,2,\dots$$

Why the formulas work: sines with different $n$ are orthogonal on $[0,L]$, meaning $\int_0^L\sin\frac{m\pi x}{L}\sin\frac{n\pi x}{L}\,dx=0$ when $m\ne n$. Multiply the series by $\sin\frac{m\pi x}{L}$ and integrate. Every term dies except $n=m$, and that one gives $b_m\cdot\frac{L}{2}$. Cosines work the same way.

Facts you will use in nearly every coefficient: $\sin n\pi=0$, $\cos n\pi=(-1)^n$, and $1-(-1)^n$ is $0$ for even $n$ and $2$ for odd $n$.

Interactive · partial sums of a Fourier series
Example 3Sine series of a constant

Expand $f(x)=100$ in a sine series on $(0,\pi)$.

  1. Here $L=\pi$, so $\dfrac{n\pi x}{L}=nx$ and $b_n=\dfrac{2}{\pi}\displaystyle\int_0^{\pi}100\sin nx\,dx$.
  2. Integrate. $\displaystyle\int_0^{\pi}\sin nx\,dx=\left[-\frac{\cos nx}{n}\right]_0^{\pi}=\frac{-\cos n\pi+1}{n}=\frac{1-(-1)^n}{n}$.
  3. So $b_n=\dfrac{200}{\pi}\cdot\dfrac{1-(-1)^n}{n}$. This is $\dfrac{400}{n\pi}$ for odd $n$ and $0$ for even $n$.
  4. Series. $$100=\frac{200}{\pi}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-(-1)^n}{n}\sin nx=\frac{400}{\pi}\left(\sin x+\frac{\sin3x}{3}+\frac{\sin5x}{5}+\cdots\right).$$
Example 4Cosine series of $x$

Expand $f(x)=x$ in a cosine series on $(0,\pi)$.

  1. The constant term. $a_0=\dfrac{2}{\pi}\displaystyle\int_0^{\pi}x\,dx=\dfrac{2}{\pi}\cdot\dfrac{\pi^2}{2}=\pi$, so $\dfrac{a_0}{2}=\dfrac{\pi}{2}$ (the average value of $x$ on the interval).
  2. Set up $a_n$. $a_n=\dfrac{2}{\pi}\displaystyle\int_0^{\pi}x\cos nx\,dx$. Integrate by parts with $u=x$, $dv=\cos nx\,dx$.
  3. Integrate. $$\begin{aligned}\int_0^{\pi}x\cos nx\,dx&=\left[\frac{x\sin nx}{n}\right]_0^{\pi}-\frac1n\int_0^{\pi}\sin nx\,dx\\&=0-\frac1n\cdot\frac{1-(-1)^n}{n}=\frac{(-1)^n-1}{n^2}.\end{aligned}$$
  4. So $a_n=\dfrac{2\big((-1)^n-1\big)}{\pi n^2}$, which is $-\dfrac{4}{\pi n^2}$ for odd $n$ and $0$ for even $n$.
  5. Series. $$x=\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{4}{\pi}\left(\cos x+\frac{\cos3x}{9}+\frac{\cos5x}{25}+\cdots\right).$$
Summary
  • Heat (parabolic), wave (hyperbolic), Laplace (elliptic).
  • Heat needs $u(x,0)$. Wave needs $u(x,0)$ and $u_t(x,0)$. Laplace needs every edge.
  • Held at a value: Dirichlet. Insulated or free: Neumann, $u_x=0$.
  • Half-range series: $b_n=\frac2L\int_0^Lf\sin$, $a_n=\frac2L\int_0^Lf\cos$.

More practice: PDE practice page.
