Chapter 13 · Section 13.3

Heat equation

A thin rod of length $L$ starts with temperature $f(x)$, and both ends are held at $0$ for all time. We find the temperature $u(x,t)$ by separation of variables. The same six steps solve every problem in 13.3 to 13.5.

The solution of the heat problem with ends held at zero is

$$u(x,t)=\sum_{n=1}^\infty b_ne^{-k\left(\frac{n\pi}{L}\right)^2t}\sin\frac{n\pi x}{L}.$$

Each term is a mode: a fixed shape $\sin\dfrac{n\pi x}{L}$ that keeps its shape and just fades. Higher modes ($n$ large) are wigglier and fade much faster, because the exponent grows like $n^2$. So after a short time the sharp details of the initial temperature disappear and the profile looks like the first mode, a single hump, slowly sinking to zero. That is exactly what heat does: it smooths things out.

The coefficients $b_n$ are the sine-series coefficients of the initial temperature, so the whole method is: separate, solve the eigenvalue problem for $X$, solve for $T$, superpose, and match $u(x,0)=f(x)$ with a Fourier series.

When an end is held at a nonzero temperature, first subtract the steady state $\psi(x)$ (a straight line between the end temperatures), solve the zero-end problem for the difference, and add $\psi$ back.

The problem

$$k\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial x^2}=\frac{\partial u}{\partial t},\qquad0<x<L,\ t>0 \tag{1}$$ $$u(0,t)=0,\qquad u(L,t)=0,\qquad t>0 \tag{2}$$ $$u(x,0)=f(x),\qquad0<x<L \tag{3}$$

Step 1 · Separate the variables

Put $u(x,t)=X(x)T(t)$ into (1): $kX''T=XT'$. Divide by $kXT$ to separate:

$$\frac{X''}{X}=\frac{T'}{kT}=-\lambda.$$

This gives two ODEs:

$$X''+\lambda X=0 \tag{4}$$ $$T'+k\lambda T=0 \tag{5}$$

Step 2 · Move the boundary conditions onto $X$

The condition $u(0,t)=X(0)T(t)=0$ must hold for every $t$. If $T$ were always zero then $u\equiv0$, which is useless (it cannot match a nonzero $f$). So we need $X(0)=0$. In the same way $X(L)=0$. Together with (4) this is the eigenvalue problem from Section 3.9:

$$X''+\lambda X=0,\qquad X(0)=0,\qquad X(L)=0.$$

Step 3 · Solve the eigenvalue problem

  • $\lambda=0$: $X=c_1+c_2x$. $X(0)=0$ gives $c_1=0$, then $X(L)=c_2L=0$ gives $c_2=0$. Only $X=0$.
  • $\lambda=-\alpha^2<0$: $X=c_1\cosh\alpha x+c_2\sinh\alpha x$. $X(0)=0$ gives $c_1=0$, then $c_2\sinh\alpha L=0$. Since $\sinh\alpha L\ne0$ for $\alpha>0$, $c_2=0$. Only $X=0$ again.
  • $\lambda=\alpha^2>0$: $X=c_1\cos\alpha x+c_2\sin\alpha x$. $X(0)=0$ gives $c_1=0$. Then $X(L)=c_2\sin\alpha L=0$. We want $c_2\ne0$, so we need $\sin\alpha L=0$, which means $\alpha L=n\pi$.
Eigenvalues and eigenfunctions $$\lambda_n=\frac{n^2\pi^2}{L^2},\qquad X_n(x)=\sin\frac{n\pi x}{L},\qquad n=1,2,3,\dots$$

Step 4 · Solve for $T$ and form product solutions

With $\lambda=\lambda_n$, (5) is $T'+\dfrac{kn^2\pi^2}{L^2}T=0$, a first-order equation with solution $T=c_3e^{-kn^2\pi^2t/L^2}$. Multiplying, each

$$u_n(x,t)=A_n\,e^{-kn^2\pi^2t/L^2}\sin\frac{n\pi x}{L}$$

satisfies the PDE (1) and both boundary conditions (2).

Step 5 · Superpose

A single $u_n$ only matches an initial temperature that happens to be a sine curve. To match a general $f$ we add them all up (Theorem 13.1.1):

$$u(x,t)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}A_n\,e^{-kn^2\pi^2t/L^2}\sin\frac{n\pi x}{L}.$$

Step 6 · Use the initial condition

Put $t=0$. Every exponential becomes $1$:

$$f(x)=u(x,0)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}A_n\sin\frac{n\pi x}{L}.$$

This is the half-range sine series of $f$, so $A_n$ must be its sine coefficient $b_n$.

Solution of (1)–(3) $$u(x,t)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}A_n\,e^{-kn^2\pi^2t/L^2}\sin\frac{n\pi x}{L},\qquad A_n=\frac{2}{L}\int_0^{L}f(x)\sin\frac{n\pi x}{L}\,dx.$$

Every term decays exponentially, and higher $n$ (wigglier shapes) decay much faster because of the $n^2$. So the rod quickly smooths out into a single sine hump and then cools to $0$.

Interactive · a cooling rod
Example 1A rod starting at $100^\circ$

Solve (1)–(3) with $L=\pi$, $k=1$ and $u(x,0)=100$.

  1. Specialize the formula. With $L=\pi$, $\dfrac{n\pi x}{L}=nx$, and with $k=1$ the exponent is $-n^2t$. So $u=\sum A_ne^{-n^2t}\sin nx$.
  2. Find $A_n$. $A_n=\dfrac{2}{\pi}\displaystyle\int_0^{\pi}100\sin nx\,dx$. This is Example 3 of 13.2: $A_n=\dfrac{200}{\pi}\cdot\dfrac{1-(-1)^n}{n}$.
  3. Answer. $$u(x,t)=\frac{200}{\pi}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-(-1)^n}{n}\,e^{-n^2t}\sin nx.$$
  4. Only odd terms survive. Writing them out: $u=\dfrac{400}{\pi}\left(e^{-t}\sin x+\dfrac{e^{-9t}\sin3x}{3}+\dfrac{e^{-25t}\sin5x}{5}+\cdots\right)$. After a short time the first term dominates.
  5. Sanity check. As $t\to\infty$, $u\to0$, which makes sense because both ends are held at $0$.

Insulated ends

If both ends are insulated, the boundary conditions are $u_x(0,t)=0$ and $u_x(L,t)=0$. Separation gives the same ODEs, but now $X'(0)=0$ and $X'(L)=0$. Watch the case $\lambda=0$ carefully:

  • $\lambda=0$: $X=c_1+c_2x$, $X'=c_2=0$. But $c_1$ is free, so $X=1$ is an eigenfunction. Its $T$ equation is $T'=0$, so $T$ is a constant.
  • $\lambda=-\alpha^2$: only the zero solution, as before.
  • $\lambda=\alpha^2$: $X'(0)=\alpha c_2=0$, then $X'(L)=-\alpha c_1\sin\alpha L=0$ gives $\alpha=\dfrac{n\pi}{L}$ and $X_n=\cos\dfrac{n\pi x}{L}$.

So the solution is a cosine series with a constant term:

$$u(x,t)=A_0+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}A_n\,e^{-kn^2\pi^2t/L^2}\cos\frac{n\pi x}{L},$$ $$A_0=\frac1L\int_0^Lf(x)\,dx,\qquad A_n=\frac2L\int_0^Lf(x)\cos\frac{n\pi x}{L}\,dx.$$
Example 2Insulated ends

Solve $u_{xx}=u_t$, $0<x<\pi$, with $u_x(0,t)=0$, $u_x(\pi,t)=0$ and $u(x,0)=x$.

  1. Which series? Insulated ends give the cosine eigenfunctions $\cos nx$, plus the constant from $\lambda=0$. With $k=1$, $L=\pi$: $u=A_0+\sum A_ne^{-n^2t}\cos nx$.
  2. Match at $t=0$. $x=A_0+\sum A_n\cos nx$. This is the cosine series of $x$ from Example 4 of 13.2, with $A_0=\dfrac{a_0}{2}$ and $A_n=a_n$.
  3. Coefficients. $A_0=\dfrac{\pi}{2}$ and $A_n=\dfrac{2\big((-1)^n-1\big)}{\pi n^2}$.
  4. Answer. $$u(x,t)=\frac{\pi}{2}+\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^n-1}{n^2}\,e^{-n^2t}\cos nx.$$
  5. Interpret. As $t\to\infty$, $u\to\dfrac{\pi}{2}$, the average starting temperature. With insulated ends no heat escapes, so the heat just spreads out evenly.

Nonzero end temperatures

Separation of variables needs homogeneous boundary conditions. If an end is held at a nonzero temperature, first find the steady-state solution $\psi(x)$ (the temperature the rod settles to), then solve for the difference $v=u-\psi$, which has zero boundary conditions.

Example 3One end held at $100^\circ$

Solve $u_{xx}=u_t$, $0<x<1$, with $u(0,t)=0$, $u(1,t)=100$ and $u(x,0)=0$.

  1. Steady state. When nothing changes in time, $u_t=0$, so $\psi''=0$ and $\psi=c_1+c_2x$. The end conditions $\psi(0)=0$, $\psi(1)=100$ give $\psi(x)=100x$.
  2. Shift. Let $u=v+100x$. Since $(100x)_{xx}=0$ and $(100x)_t=0$, $v$ satisfies the same PDE $v_{xx}=v_t$.
  3. New conditions for $v$. $v(0,t)=0-0=0$, $v(1,t)=100-100=0$, and $v(x,0)=0-100x=-100x$. Now the boundary conditions are homogeneous.
  4. Solve for $v$ with the standard formula. $L=1$, $k=1$: $v=\sum A_ne^{-n^2\pi^2t}\sin n\pi x$ with $A_n=2\displaystyle\int_0^1(-100x)\sin n\pi x\,dx$.
  5. Integrate by parts. $\displaystyle\int_0^1x\sin n\pi x\,dx=\left[-\frac{x\cos n\pi x}{n\pi}\right]_0^1+\frac{1}{n\pi}\int_0^1\cos n\pi x\,dx=-\frac{(-1)^n}{n\pi}+0$.
  6. So $A_n=-200\cdot\left(-\dfrac{(-1)^n}{n\pi}\right)=\dfrac{200(-1)^n}{n\pi}$.
  7. Answer. $$u(x,t)=100x+\frac{200}{\pi}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^n}{n}\,e^{-n^2\pi^2t}\sin n\pi x.$$ As $t\to\infty$, $u\to100x$, a straight-line temperature profile.
The six steps (use them in 13.4 and 13.5 too)
  1. Put $u=XT$ and separate with $-\lambda$.
  2. Turn the boundary conditions into conditions on $X$.
  3. Solve the eigenvalue problem, checking $\lambda=0$, $\lambda<0$, $\lambda>0$.
  4. Solve the $T$ equation and multiply.
  5. Superpose with an infinite sum.
  6. Match the initial condition with a Fourier series.

More practice: PDE practice page.

Chapter 13 · Section 13.4

Wave equation

A string of length $L$ is fixed at both ends and set vibrating with initial shape $f(x)$ and initial velocity $g(x)$. We find its displacement $u(x,t)$ with the same six steps as the heat equation. The one difference is that time now appears as a second derivative, so the time factor oscillates instead of decaying.

The wave equation is solved exactly like the heat equation, with one difference: $T$ satisfies $T''+a^2\lambda T=0$, a second-order equation. Its solutions are sines and cosines in time, so each mode oscillates rather than decays:

$$u(x,t)=\sum_{n=1}^\infty\left(A_n\cos\frac{n\pi a}{L}t+B_n\sin\frac{n\pi a}{L}t\right)\sin\frac{n\pi x}{L}.$$
  • $A_n$ are the sine coefficients of the initial shape $f(x)$.
  • $B_n\dfrac{n\pi a}{L}$ are the sine coefficients of the initial velocity $g(x)$. Divide by $\dfrac{n\pi a}{L}$ to get $B_n$.

Each mode is a standing wave: the shape $\sin\dfrac{n\pi x}{L}$ stays put and its height oscillates with frequency $\dfrac{na}{2L}$ cycles per second. The first is the fundamental tone of the string; the others are overtones at whole-number multiples, which is why a plucked string sounds musical.

The problem

$$a^2\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial x^2}=\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial t^2},\qquad0<x<L,\ t>0 \tag{1}$$ $$u(0,t)=0,\qquad u(L,t)=0,\qquad t>0 \tag{2}$$ $$u(x,0)=f(x),\qquad\left.\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}\right|_{t=0}=g(x),\qquad0<x<L \tag{3}$$

Separation and the eigenvalue problem

Put $u=X(x)T(t)$: $a^2X''T=XT''$. Divide by $a^2XT$:

$$\frac{X''}{X}=\frac{T''}{a^2T}=-\lambda,$$

so

$$X''+\lambda X=0,\qquad T''+a^2\lambda T=0.$$

The fixed ends give $X(0)=0$ and $X(L)=0$. This is the same eigenvalue problem as in 13.3, so again only $\lambda=\alpha^2>0$ gives nonzero solutions:

$$\lambda_n=\frac{n^2\pi^2}{L^2},\qquad X_n=\sin\frac{n\pi x}{L},\qquad n=1,2,3,\dots$$

The time equation

With $\lambda=\lambda_n$, the $T$ equation is $T''+\left(\dfrac{n\pi a}{L}\right)^2T=0$. That is simple harmonic motion (Section 3.8) with angular frequency $\dfrac{n\pi a}{L}$:

$$T=c_3\cos\frac{n\pi a}{L}t+c_4\sin\frac{n\pi a}{L}t.$$

Multiplying and superposing:

$$u(x,t)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(A_n\cos\frac{n\pi a}{L}t+B_n\sin\frac{n\pi a}{L}t\right)\sin\frac{n\pi x}{L}. \tag{4}$$

Matching the two initial conditions

Shape. At $t=0$, $\cos0=1$ and $\sin0=0$, so $f(x)=\sum A_n\sin\dfrac{n\pi x}{L}$. That is the sine series of $f$.

Velocity. Differentiate (4) term by term in $t$ and put $t=0$:

$$g(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(B_n\frac{n\pi a}{L}\right)\sin\frac{n\pi x}{L}.$$

That is the sine series of $g$, but the coefficient is the whole bracket $B_n\dfrac{n\pi a}{L}$. So we divide by $\dfrac{n\pi a}{L}$ at the end.

Solution of (1)–(3)

$u$ is the series (4) with

$$A_n=\frac{2}{L}\int_0^{L}f(x)\sin\frac{n\pi x}{L}\,dx,\qquad B_n=\frac{2}{n\pi a}\int_0^{L}g(x)\sin\frac{n\pi x}{L}\,dx.$$

If the string is released from rest, $g=0$ and every $B_n=0$.

Example 1A string released from rest

Solve (1)–(3) with $L=1$, $a=1$, $f(x)=\dfrac14x(1-x)$ and $g(x)=0$.

  1. Released from rest, so $B_n=0$. With $L=1$, $a=1$: $u=\sum A_n\cos n\pi t\,\sin n\pi x$.
  2. Set up $A_n$. $A_n=2\displaystyle\int_0^1\dfrac14(x-x^2)\sin n\pi x\,dx=\dfrac12\int_0^1(x-x^2)\sin n\pi x\,dx$.
  3. First integral. From Example 3 of 13.3, $\displaystyle\int_0^1x\sin n\pi x\,dx=\frac{(-1)^{n+1}}{n\pi}$.
  4. Second integral, by parts with $u=x^2$. $\displaystyle\int_0^1x^2\sin n\pi x\,dx=\left[-\frac{x^2\cos n\pi x}{n\pi}\right]_0^1+\frac{2}{n\pi}\int_0^1x\cos n\pi x\,dx$.
  5. Evaluate the pieces. The bracket is $-\dfrac{(-1)^n}{n\pi}$. For the last integral, by parts again, $\displaystyle\int_0^1x\cos n\pi x\,dx=\left[\frac{x\sin n\pi x}{n\pi}+\frac{\cos n\pi x}{n^2\pi^2}\right]_0^1=\frac{(-1)^n-1}{n^2\pi^2}$.
  6. So $\displaystyle\int_0^1x^2\sin n\pi x\,dx=-\frac{(-1)^n}{n\pi}+\frac{2\big((-1)^n-1\big)}{n^3\pi^3}$.
  7. Subtract. $$\begin{aligned}\int_0^1(x-x^2)\sin n\pi x\,dx&=\frac{(-1)^{n+1}}{n\pi}+\frac{(-1)^n}{n\pi}-\frac{2\big((-1)^n-1\big)}{n^3\pi^3}\\&=\frac{2\big(1-(-1)^n\big)}{n^3\pi^3}.\end{aligned}$$ The first two terms cancel.
  8. Halve it. $A_n=\dfrac{1-(-1)^n}{n^3\pi^3}$.
  9. Answer. $$u(x,t)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-(-1)^n}{n^3\pi^3}\cos n\pi t\,\sin n\pi x.$$ Only odd $n$ contribute, and the $n^3$ makes the series converge very fast: the motion is almost a pure first mode.
Example 2A string struck from its rest position

Solve (1)–(3) with $L=\pi$, $a=1$, $f(x)=0$ and $g(x)=1$.

  1. No initial displacement, so $A_n=0$. With $L=\pi$, $a=1$: $u=\sum B_n\sin nt\,\sin nx$.
  2. Use the $B_n$ formula. $\dfrac{2}{n\pi a}=\dfrac{2}{n\pi}$, so $B_n=\dfrac{2}{n\pi}\displaystyle\int_0^{\pi}\sin nx\,dx$.
  3. Integrate. $\displaystyle\int_0^{\pi}\sin nx\,dx=\frac{1-(-1)^n}{n}$.
  4. So $B_n=\dfrac{2\big(1-(-1)^n\big)}{\pi n^2}$.
  5. Answer. $$u(x,t)=\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-(-1)^n}{n^2}\sin nt\,\sin nx.$$
  6. Check. At $t=0$ every $\sin nt=0$, so $u(x,0)=0$. Good.
Interactive · a plucked string

Standing waves

Each term of (4) is called a standing wave or normal mode. Using the amplitude form from Section 3.8, it can be written

$$u_n(x,t)=C_n\sin\left(\frac{n\pi a}{L}t+\phi_n\right)\sin\frac{n\pi x}{L},\qquad C_n=\sqrt{A_n^2+B_n^2}.$$

At a fixed point $x$, the string moves in simple harmonic motion with frequency

$$f_n=\frac{na}{2L}.$$

Every point of a mode vibrates at the same frequency, but with its own amplitude $C_n\sin\dfrac{n\pi x}{L}$. The points where $\sin\dfrac{n\pi x}{L}=0$ never move. They are called nodes, and the $n$th mode has $n-1$ of them inside the string. For example, the second mode has a node at $\dfrac{L}{2}$ and the third has nodes at $\dfrac{L}{3}$ and $\dfrac{2L}{3}$.

The lowest frequency $f_1=\dfrac{a}{2L}=\dfrac{1}{2L}\sqrt{\dfrac{T}{\rho}}$ is the fundamental frequency, which sets the pitch of the note. Tighter strings (larger $T$) and shorter strings sound higher. The other frequencies $f_n=nf_1$ are the overtones.

Superposition of easier problems

You can also solve (1)–(3) by splitting it into two problems: one with shape $f$ and zero velocity, the other with zero shape and velocity $g$. Each is easier, and their sum $u=u_1+u_2$ solves the original because the PDE and boundary conditions are linear and homogeneous.

Summary
  • Same $X_n=\sin\frac{n\pi x}{L}$ as the heat equation. $T$ oscillates with frequency $\frac{n\pi a}{L}$.
  • $A_n$ is the sine coefficient of $f$. $B_n$ is the sine coefficient of $g$ divided by $\frac{n\pi a}{L}$.
  • Released from rest: $B_n=0$. Starting flat: $A_n=0$.
  • Mode $n$ has frequency $\frac{na}{2L}$ and $n-1$ interior nodes.

More practice: PDE practice page.

Chapter 13 · Section 13.5

Laplace’s equation

Now we find the steady-state temperature $u(x,y)$ in a rectangular plate. There is no time, so instead of an initial condition we have a condition on each of the four edges. The key idea: put the homogeneous conditions on one pair of opposite edges, expand in that variable, and use the last edge to find the coefficients.

Laplace's equation $u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0$ describes a temperature that has stopped changing. There is no time, so all conditions are on the edges of the region. For a rectangle, separation gives an eigenvalue problem in the direction where both edges have homogeneous conditions (zero, or insulated), and exponential or hyperbolic functions in the other direction.

The usual pattern for a plate $0\le x\le a$, $0\le y\le b$ with three edges at zero and the edge $y=b$ at $f(x)$:

$$u(x,y)=\sum_{n=1}^\infty A_n\sinh\frac{n\pi y}{a}\sin\frac{n\pi x}{a},\qquad A_n\sinh\frac{n\pi b}{a}=\text{sine coefficient of }f.$$

The $\sinh$ is chosen because it is zero at $y=0$, which satisfies the bottom-edge condition automatically.

If more than one edge is nonzero, split the problem: solve one problem per nonzero edge, each with the other edges at zero, and add the solutions. This works because Laplace's equation is linear.

The problem

Take the plate $0\le x\le a$, $0\le y\le b$. The left and right edges are insulated, the bottom is held at $0$, and the top is held at temperature $f(x)$:

$$\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial x^2}+\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial y^2}=0,\qquad0<x<a,\ 0<y<b \tag{1}$$ $$\left.\frac{\partial u}{\partial x}\right|_{x=0}=0,\qquad\left.\frac{\partial u}{\partial x}\right|_{x=a}=0,\qquad0<y<b \tag{2}$$ $$u(x,0)=0,\qquad u(x,b)=f(x),\qquad0<x<a \tag{3}$$

Separate the variables

Put $u=X(x)Y(y)$: $X''Y+XY''=0$. Move one term across and divide by $XY$:

$$\frac{X''}{X}=-\frac{Y''}{Y}=-\lambda,$$

so

$$X''+\lambda X=0,\qquad Y''-\lambda Y=0.$$

Notice the sign difference. When $X$ gets sines and cosines, $Y$ gets hyperbolic functions, and the other way round.

The eigenvalue problem in $x$

The insulated sides (2) give $X'(0)=0$ and $X'(a)=0$. This is the insulated-ends problem from 13.3:

  • $\lambda=0$: $X=c_1$ (a constant) works.
  • $\lambda<0$: only $X=0$.
  • $\lambda=\alpha^2>0$: $X=\cos\dfrac{n\pi x}{a}$ with $\lambda_n=\dfrac{n^2\pi^2}{a^2}$, $n=1,2,3,\dots$

The $Y$ equation and the bottom edge

The bottom condition $u(x,0)=0$ gives $Y(0)=0$.

  • For $\lambda=0$: $Y''=0$ gives $Y=c_3+c_4y$, and $Y(0)=0$ gives $c_3=0$. So $Y=c_4y$ and the product is $u_0=A_0y$.
  • For $\lambda_n$: $Y''-\dfrac{n^2\pi^2}{a^2}Y=0$. On a finite interval it is easiest to write the solution with $\cosh$ and $\sinh$: $Y=c_3\cosh\dfrac{n\pi y}{a}+c_4\sinh\dfrac{n\pi y}{a}$. Since $\cosh0=1$ and $\sinh0=0$, $Y(0)=0$ gives $c_3=0$. So $Y=c_4\sinh\dfrac{n\pi y}{a}$.

Superposing all the product solutions:

$$u(x,y)=A_0y+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}A_n\sinh\frac{n\pi y}{a}\cos\frac{n\pi x}{a}. \tag{4}$$
Why $\sinh$ and not $e^{\pm y}$

Both $\{e^{ky},e^{-ky}\}$ and $\{\cosh ky,\sinh ky\}$ are valid pairs of solutions. On a bounded interval starting at $0$, the hyperbolic pair is smarter because a condition at $y=0$ immediately kills one of them. On an unbounded interval ($y\to\infty$), use the exponentials instead, because the condition "stays bounded" kills $e^{ky}$.

The top edge

Put $y=b$ in (4) and set it equal to $f(x)$:

$$f(x)=A_0b+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(A_n\sinh\frac{n\pi b}{a}\right)\cos\frac{n\pi x}{a}.$$

This is a half-range cosine series on $(0,a)$. Matching its constant term $\dfrac{a_0}{2}$ and its coefficients $a_n$:

Solution of (1)–(3) $$A_0=\frac{1}{ab}\int_0^{a}f(x)\,dx,\qquad A_n=\frac{2}{a\sinh\frac{n\pi b}{a}}\int_0^{a}f(x)\cos\frac{n\pi x}{a}\,dx.$$

The Dirichlet problem

When the value of $u$ is given on every edge, it is called a Dirichlet problem. Suppose three edges are held at $0$ and the top at $f(x)$:

$$u(0,y)=0,\quad u(a,y)=0,\quad u(x,0)=0,\quad u(x,b)=f(x).$$

Now $X(0)=X(a)=0$, which gives sines instead of cosines and no $\lambda=0$ term. Everything else is the same:

$$u(x,y)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}A_n\sinh\frac{n\pi y}{a}\sin\frac{n\pi x}{a},$$ $$A_n=\frac{2}{a\sinh\frac{n\pi b}{a}}\int_0^{a}f(x)\sin\frac{n\pi x}{a}\,dx.$$
Interactive · steady-state temperature map
Example 1A square plate with a hot top

Find the steady-state temperature in the square $0\le x\le\pi$, $0\le y\le\pi$ if the top edge is held at $100$ and the other three edges at $0$.

  1. Which formula? Three edges at zero, so this is the Dirichlet problem with $a=b=\pi$, $f(x)=100$. Then $\dfrac{n\pi x}{a}=nx$ and $\dfrac{n\pi b}{a}=n\pi$.
  2. Set up. $A_n=\dfrac{2}{\pi\sinh n\pi}\displaystyle\int_0^{\pi}100\sin nx\,dx$.
  3. Integrate (Example 3 of 13.2): $\displaystyle\int_0^{\pi}100\sin nx\,dx=\frac{100\big(1-(-1)^n\big)}{n}$.
  4. So $A_n=\dfrac{200\big(1-(-1)^n\big)}{n\pi\sinh n\pi}$.
  5. Answer. $$u(x,y)=\frac{200}{\pi}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1-(-1)^n}{n\sinh n\pi}\sinh ny\,\sin nx.$$
  6. Check the top edge. At $y=\pi$ the $\sinh n\pi$ cancels, leaving the sine series of $100$. Good.
Example 2Insulated sides

Solve (1)–(3) with $a=b=\pi$ and $f(x)=x$.

  1. Use the insulated-sides formula. With $a=\pi$: $u=A_0y+\sum A_n\sinh ny\cos nx$.
  2. Constant term. $A_0=\dfrac{1}{\pi\cdot\pi}\displaystyle\int_0^{\pi}x\,dx=\frac{1}{\pi^2}\cdot\frac{\pi^2}{2}=\frac12$.
  3. Cosine coefficients. $A_n=\dfrac{2}{\pi\sinh n\pi}\displaystyle\int_0^{\pi}x\cos nx\,dx$. From Example 4 of 13.2 that integral is $\dfrac{(-1)^n-1}{n^2}$.
  4. So $A_n=\dfrac{2\big((-1)^n-1\big)}{\pi n^2\sinh n\pi}$.
  5. Answer. $$u(x,y)=\frac{y}{2}+\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^n-1}{n^2\sinh n\pi}\sinh ny\,\cos nx.$$

Superposition: more than one hot edge

A Dirichlet problem with nonzero values on several edges is split into problems that each have just one nonzero pair of opposite edges. For example, if

$$u(0,y)=F(y),\quad u(a,y)=G(y),\quad u(x,0)=f(x),\quad u(x,b)=g(x),$$

solve problem 1 with the sides at zero (keep $f$ and $g$) and problem 2 with the top and bottom at zero (keep $F$ and $G$). Then $u=u_1+u_2$, because each edge value adds up correctly and the sum of two solutions of Laplace's equation is a solution.

Example 3Top and bottom both nonzero

Set up the solution of Laplace's equation on $0<x<a$, $0<y<b$ with $u(0,y)=0$, $u(a,y)=0$, $u(x,0)=f(x)$, $u(x,b)=g(x)$.

  1. The sides are zero, so $X_n=\sin\dfrac{n\pi x}{a}$ as in the Dirichlet problem.
  2. Now both $Y$ terms stay, because there is no zero condition in $y$: $u=\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(A_n\cosh\frac{n\pi y}{a}+B_n\sinh\frac{n\pi y}{a}\right)\sin\frac{n\pi x}{a}$.
  3. Bottom edge, $y=0$. $\cosh0=1$ and $\sinh0=0$, so $f(x)=\sum A_n\sin\dfrac{n\pi x}{a}$ and $A_n=\dfrac2a\displaystyle\int_0^af(x)\sin\frac{n\pi x}{a}\,dx$.
  4. Top edge, $y=b$. $g(x)=\sum\left(A_n\cosh\dfrac{n\pi b}{a}+B_n\sinh\dfrac{n\pi b}{a}\right)\sin\dfrac{n\pi x}{a}$. The bracket must equal the sine coefficient of $g$.
  5. Solve for $B_n$. $$B_n=\frac{1}{\sinh\frac{n\pi b}{a}}\left(\frac2a\int_0^ag(x)\sin\frac{n\pi x}{a}\,dx-A_n\cosh\frac{n\pi b}{a}\right).$$
Summary
  • $\frac{X''}{X}=-\frac{Y''}{Y}=-\lambda$: one variable gets trig functions, the other gets hyperbolic ones.
  • Expand in the direction whose two edges have homogeneous conditions.
  • Zero at $y=0$: use $\sinh$. Insulated sides: cosines plus an $A_0y$ term.
  • Match the last edge with a Fourier series. Several hot edges: superpose.
